Design bible · working title Placeholder name

Riftgate

A portrait-mode tactical hex battler for Android. A handful of customizable war machines hold the last city gates against a whole genre invading, the JRPG world's wildlife and its heroes pouring through rifts, in a roguelike campaign built from procedurally generated battlefields.

Version0.411
Date2 November 2026
PlatformAndroid, portrait; nineties 3D against sixteen-bit 2D
EngineGodot 4.8 (4.8-beta2 until it ships), GDScript

Decided
Agreed in design discussion. Build against it.
Recommended
Proposed direction, not yet confirmed. Prototype it unless a better idea shows up.
Open
Unresolved. Needs a decision or a playtest.
Pinned
Parked deliberately for later, not rejected.
Lean
A maker's or a side's tendency: the theme, words and keywords its pieces carry most. Never an exclusion: a piece carries what its fantasy calls for, whoever makes it.
Decided

How to read a row.

A Decided row is one of three things: a design test (what the player feels, section 2), a rule the engine runs, or a content call for a named piece. A maker's or a side's theme, word or keyword is a Lean, never a Decided rule. A "never" carries its reason and the condition that would reopen it; a "never" without either records the current state, not a law. A row that retires a mechanism names the form that failed and the test it failed, so a new form that passes the test is free to be proposed. Every row is open to debate (section 2) and a passive may break any rule as a one-off (section 7): a proposal argues for the change in the open rather than designing around the row, and its bench shows the ideas a row killed, so Ryan can overrule the row and not only the idea. Decided 13 October, after the review of that day.

What a row holds (Ryan, 23 October: the bible is "primarily used for" session context): the decision as it stands, its reason, what reopens it, and Ryan's words where they set its strength. The rest has a home of its own: how a thing is built is the code's, docs/rules.md's and git's; a run's numbers are docs/measurements.md's, a row keeping the conclusion and a pointer; a placeholder number is content's, so a row quotes a number only when the number is Ryan's call or the rule itself; a design session's rounds are its brief's; a task is docs/todo.md's.

1

Concept and tone

This document records what is decided and why. The rules as they stand are in docs/rules.md, how things stand today in docs/status.md, and what comes next in docs/todo.md. Each row says what holds now; history lives in git.

The game

Decided

Three nested loops, and the depth lives in the skirmish.

The campaign is the roguelike run. A battle is a hex field, the gate at the bottom and the rift at the top, won by killing the boss that powers the rift. A skirmish is the dice fight opened on one or more packs at a time.

Decided

A few machines against a horde.

The player fields a few machines, none of them disposable, through many battles, with no unit micromanagement. An enemy hex is a pack, so a skirmish is a few machines against many foes.

Decided

Learnable in play.

Tooltips and an in-game cheat sheet, never a wiki, a build planner or damage-per-second maths.

Open

The game's name.

"Riftgate" is a placeholder.

Tone

Decided

Doom and Space Marine: over the top, played straight.

Propaganda is in; manufacturer adverts and Borderlands-style gags are out, because this is the end of civilisation. Never edgy, and a city falling is never funny. Grimdark is how this game plays it straight: the world is grim and nobody in it winks, though a player may take it as dark humour.

Decided

Grounded military mecha.

The machines are in the MechWarrior and StarCraft Terran line, never Evangelion's demon-mecha: rugged, brutal, heavy, serviced rather than cobbled together.

Decided

The Arsenal is twisted by the occult, never merged with it.

Each of its machines is a battlemech with a sealed occult power source where a fusion reactor would be, drawn by an engineer who has had things whispering in his ear and twisting his thoughts. Mechanical only: no flesh and no gore. One may borrow another line's frame, twisted (a corrupted Aile Strike, an Evangelion frame), and it still moves with the game's weight (section 7); none has a mind of its own. CARE pilots fly them, and CARE would never let anything else into its hangars.

Decided

The makers are provenance, a livery and a voice, never advertising.

In-your-face advertising turns the tone to black comedy. A maker calling the commander its best customer is fine.

The world

Decided

The Council rules the cities.

The Council for the Ultimate Liberation of Terra; its acronym is never written or spoken. It is a martial-law regime pretending otherwise, believing in its cause while profiting from it: a god-emperor's court closer to Pacific Rim than to BattleTech. It rewards innovation that helps the fight, so the makers compete for its favour, and it keeps the best hardware for its own military.

Decided

The player is CARE's Battlefield Commander.

CARE, the Civil Auxiliary for Rift Emergencies: the Council chose the name in condescension. The commander is a general, never a politician. CARE's people are the true believers, pragmatic and rebellious, played straight; the Council takes the credit and none of the responsibility.

Decided

The Arsenal is the Council's own maker.

It sells CARE a trickle and never its best. It is the one research path the player cannot force: its tiers open only as the campaign turns dire.

Decided

What the Council names a machine and what it manifests as can differ.

Ryan, 11 October: the Council, and by extension the Arsenal, are self-righteous seekers of knowledge and power who think of themselves as the saviours of humanity, so the names they give their machines and what those machines turn out to be can be incongruent.

Decided

The occult is the Council's, and the Arsenal says it in liturgy.

The domains moved the invasion to the genre's high fantasy, so among the makers the demonic and the eldritch are the Council's and its Arsenal's: the one place the player meets the dark on our side. The invasion meets its own dark at depth (section 9: the Convergence's last tier wears the final dungeon's cosmic register), which is the enemy's and never a maker's. The Arsenal is demonic in look and effect and liturgical in what the player reads: its manual never says demon; it says devotion, penance, judgement. That is the incongruence above made a rule: names in the register of justice and vengeance (section 7), machines that manifest as Hell, and a player who looks a name up finds both. Machines that named their demons would make the Council's nature the premise instead of a reveal, and that stays open (below).

Decided

Lore is Ryan's.

design.fantasy in content holds his homages and is never shown.

Open

The lore holes.

Is CARE an Auxiliary or an Agency? What does the Council do when the player fails, can CARE's contract be lost, and how does the Council's credit-taking become plot?

Pinned

Room to expand, left open on purpose.

Whether this is mankind's last bastion, whether other colonies look away, whether the Council had a hand in the rifts (its rise may become the campaign's spine).

The invasion's register

Decided

The JRPG genre invades, played dead straight.

Final Fantasy is the spine; homage spans the genre (the Gel is Dragon Quest's slime, the Needler the Cactuar made sinister). Every foe is drawn in one style of our own, never in a series' design language. Homage is by archetype (Ryan, 15 October): a series' own coinages take names of ours, as the Cactuar became the Needler, a homage's look is never the series' (the Exor homage need not even be a sword), and a mechanic its publisher holds a patent on stays out (Nintendo and The Pokémon Company sued over Palworld on patents, 2024, not on looks). Detailed high fantasy against gritty realism is the game's dichotomy, and it replaces most of the verbal irony (section 9).

Decided

Our side is 3D geometry; theirs is painted 2D.

The genre mash is literal: everything human is geometry, the foes are flat sixteen-bit sprites. The map is the overworld and the skirmish is the battle screen. On the map a machine is its skirmish model and a pack is a flat token; in the skirmish everything is full size.

Art direction

Decided

Ally art is mid-nineties 3D, built by code.

tools/lowforge builds each machine from a written brief: a few hundred flat-shaded facets wearing small generated tiles in its maker's metal, no hand modelling, no unwrapping, and no image model touches a machine (section 7 has the rules for models and motion).

Decided

Each maker has its metal.

Minerva matte steel and military green; Kestrel chrome, silver and blue; Wainwright & Sutler matte iron and warm brown; Aphelion pearl white and red; the Arsenal black chrome and red glass, its cockpit glass lit with a glow at rest that its fierce acts flare (Ryan, 29 October: “apply that material change to all Arsenal cockpit glass so that they all light up and have a passive glow”, and for the Nemesis “some flare during relevant animations, like roar”); a flare brightens the red toward a hot pink and never to white (the phone's light eased, the reference-glass row; it had peaked white-hot, which “doesn't really sell” a hot look, Ryan, 30 October).

Decided

Foes are painterly sixteen-bit battle sprites in ornate high fantasy.

The anchor is the SNES Final Fantasy IV to VI enemies: realistic proportions with a small, low-set head, a frozen mid-menace pose, eyes without pupils, a weapon only where the brief names one. A brief names the genre's tradition, never a series' design, and a domain's look is a mood, never a palette. Heroes are drawn in the elaborate boss register, and the party is as varied as people are: genders, skin tones, ages, builds and postures differ across the eight (Ryan, 13 October), and their costumes are to differ too (Ryan, 19 October; the to-do). Each caster's power is its own shape and colour, never shared: the Cleric's crystal, the Augur's dark omen-smoke, a green so deep it is nearly black with pale edges (Ryan, 19 October: dark, not light), the Black Mage's flame, the Summoner's deep violet rune circles, showing no fire and no rock since the Elemental is the lava field's (Ryan, 13 October), and deep because mid violet is the map's rift-intent field under a deutan simulation.

Decided

Foe art comes from an image model on the development Mac, picked by Ryan.

tools/foeforge runs FLUX.2 Klein through mflux: four candidates per foe from its one-line brief (design.sprite), its codex and its design takes; Ryan picks on a page, at the desk or on the phone. A flaw his notes name is tried by the model before any hand, by kind (Ryan, 18 October: image to image is the better outcome most of the time): a flaw of structure (a limb, a hand, a grip, a weapon) goes through image to image from the picked render at strength 0.6, which keeps the composition; a flaw of colour alone through the edit mode on the picked source, which leaves the drawing untouched; a change of costume is a fresh render from a rewritten take, since image to image keeps the costume with the composition. Image to image drifts a little each pass, so it chains from the pick Ryan liked, never from the last pass. The model's licence is checked before shipping.

Decided

Foes are cut small and sharp, and readability is the cut's job.

72x96 (96x144 when massive, 36x48 for a hero's chibi), gently sharpened, sixteen colours with dithered shading and a one-pixel outline, shown at whole device pixels, never smoothed and never lit by the scene: the clash is painted pixel art against flat-shaded geometry. Each pick's source render is kept with its prompt and cut options (tools/foeforge/picks), since AI art wants fixes on the source before a recut.

Decided

The battlefield is CARE's holographic reconstruction.

Gritty, sunlit 3D ground, gate, walls and machines against the rift's bright, flat 2D world bleeding in through a hard dithered seam. Height is landscape only. The rift vortex is four frames of dithered pixel art, never smoothed or lit.

Decided

The skirmish is one field in perspective.

Ryan, 29 October, of its options: “it's 100% field, it looks really good.” One floor seen by one level camera from behind the machines: the other world far, under the foes, painted as a sixteen-bit battle screen's backdrop (a dithered sky, the far land, a floor) in the domain's palette and dressed by the contact hex's ground, the palette kept beside the map's tileset and its water the map's own liquid, so the two never drift (30 October: the fight's lava had stayed the yellow-orange that read as acid, and its Marsh kept the genre's poison purple on the enemy's violet); our reconstruction's ground near, under the machines, meeting it at a ragged dithered seam. Everything stands on it: a foe's feet on its rank's line, what flies over its shadow, the breathing planted at the feet; the squad in four fixed hexes of the display's grid, a squad of one or two in the middle ones with the outer spots left empty and a squad of three from the left (Ryan, 29 October), each machine drawn by the floor's own camera and turned a little toward the middle, those left of it to their right and those right of it to their left (“player units on the left side can face slightly to the right, and units on the right can face slightly left”); foes face left. Retired: the overworld's tiles blown up under the foes with the seam's chequered strip over the machines, which failed his test of a fight's ground (“amateurish, distracting, and immersion-breaking, regardless of tileset”), and the machines' rear three-quarter turned left (“their angle is wrong for the field perspective”); a new form of either answers the same test.

Decided

The gate and walls are huge armoured Arsenal models.

They fill the bottom of the field with tower guns, and the gate projects the field over the city, its mouth the one gap every pack heads for.

Open

The gate's look.

A more sci-fi gate than the gothic one, how the projected field shows, the walls as emitters.

Sound and music

Decided

Industrial, grooving and grounded.

Music is a heavy industrial core carried by the beat, never fast (Tool, Nine Inch Nails, The Omnific, Teho, Hozho); effects are weight and machinery, never space opera. The grounded sound carries the seriousness that lets the game be silly elsewhere. Everything is synthesised from recipes in tools/soundforge/forge.py.

Decided

Two palettes, mixed on every screen.

Human: drums, chugged guitar, driven bass, electric piano, synth lead, metal chime, and a held saw drone, sparingly: over-used, it tires (Ryan, 29 October, of Night Shift's battle half: “Drone is over-used, it gets tiring. Maybe cut it out entirely for now”, so that half drops it for now; Hold The Line's map half takes it 4 dB down on his note the same day). Rift: orchestra, choir, long-echo bells, backwards swells and written dissonance. Bells and choir play in both.

Decided

The music never builds, drops the bass or plays a speed snare.

A section changes by what plays, lasts eight bars or more, and adds instruments, not loudness. Complexity comes from harmony, counterpoint and the groove (Night Shift's battle half is a jam whose lines change rhythm “even bar to bar”, Ryan, 30 October), and a fill marks a phrase's turn (of Hold The Line's, 29 October: “The fill worked fine”); the kick never moves, so a change of metre sits over a bar of the same length. Boss themes are epic, not intense.

Decided

Every track is a pair: a map half and a battle half on one timeline.

Ryan, 24 and 28 October: a track is written once and voiced twice, as FTL's sectors are, so changing screens changes what plays, never where the music is. The map half is re-voiced, not turned down: no kit, the chugged figures on the clean muted guitar (the ticking, "great"), the soft sub for the driven bass, the riffs on the tine piano, the harp and the cellos, the bell for the fight's metal chime, the drone and the pads, at the map's level. The map chooses the track, and a fight takes the battle half of whatever the map is playing: it fades in where the map has it, over three seconds from ENGAGE, and the map half fades back the same way on BACK TO THE FIELD (Ryan: "probably split the difference", of the dive's 2.15 s and 4 s). A track never changes in the middle of a fight. Every track is paired, the calm ones taking battle halves and the fast ones map halves (Ryan: "I know that is a little janky"). Since a fight joins the track wherever the map has it, a battle half needs less dynamic range than a lone fight track did: the calm lives in its map half, and a quiet section in a battle half is a writing matter (Ryan, 28 October). On the map a track probably loops a couple of times before the next (his expectation, a number to set by ear). The pairs replace the separate sets of 25 September, the map's two calm tracks and each fight's pick of three from its top: a fight continues the map's music instead of starting its own. Every new half is sketched in tools/soundforge/pair_sketches.py on its shipped recipe's own groove call, levelled through the phone model (28 October): Hold The Line's map half first (its bells 3 dB down on his note), then the map halves of Full Bore, The Far Bank and The Crossing and the battle halves of Night Shift and Low Ground, each waiting on his ear. The tracks share ideas and never remix one another: Night Shift's first battle half skewed so close to Hold The Line's structure that it sounded “more like a remix than shared musical ideas” (Ryan, 29 October: “That isn't necessarily a bad thing, so let's try to adjust it first before significantly changing it”), so it is adjusted before anything larger, its drums wanting “Hold the Line energy and complexity over the whole track” (his note on that half). His notes on both tracks are sketched as further rounds on the same page (hold_sketches.py, night_sketches.py).

Do how it was, but the first 14 bars”), on drums between its own and the climax's with the lead guitar added to its walk, its crashes shorter (“they're too much now that the drums are doing more”), its long crash falling 20 dB in a second and a half (“1.5s crash in K and keep the tempo drop”); the first lift on the crunch synth and the synth lead over its backing guitar 6 dB down (“hearing the crunch synth and synth lead, with the -6 is actually perfect”), the second on the crunch synth and the lead guitar (“keep Crunch synth and lead guitar for second lift”); M as it ships with the crunch synth added (“I was wrong about M, it needs to go with how it ships, but add the crunch synth to it”)The three-stage riff and a drum solo; wraps to bar nine; locked

Decided

A domain may have a boss theme.

It starts from its top when the boss's fight opens, the one place a fight changes the track, since the reveal is a moment of its own; a domain without one takes the battle half of the map's track. A theme is paired like every track (28 October); where its map half plays is section 12's.

Open

Boss themes for the Meadow, the Waste, the Peak and the finale.

Decided

One master, judged through the phone.

A gentle tilt, a presence lift at 1 to 2 kHz, no hard clip; the weight sits in the 150 to 800 Hz band a phone can play, the sub a bonus for headphones. The mix is measured through the phone model (a 220 Hz high-pass, a 9 kHz roll-off).

Open

Level the other five tracks against Hold The Line, through the phone model.

Decided

Sound lands by A/B on Ryan's phone.

A sketch never overwrites a shipped file; a pick ships from the function that rendered it, at the level he heard (the forge's SHIP table); melodies come through the Melody Desk.

Decided

Nothing glides in pitch.

Filters and loudness may move, so every effect is an impact, a mechanism or a burst. The exceptions are Aphelion's falling death whine and a short glide into a console note.

Decided

Combat has Doom's punch for both sides, never comical.

Each spend is deliberate, so a blow is drawn out, with a boom tail and weight in the 250 to 2000 Hz band. The scan and the idle are silent.

Decided

Effects fall into four registers.

The console; the iron (a blow by its weapon, then the hit, block or shield in the struck machine's metal, and footfalls by way of moving); the rift (each sound family's blow, heavy blow and arrival, and the boss reveal); the red (contact, the cut, the gate and the outcome, tuned to E).

Decided

A blow sounds by its component's shape and entry.

Content names a sound word only where those cannot (barrel, twin_barrel, rack, blade, blunt, laser, plasma, deflector, coil). The blunt weapons share one blow: a car hitting a wall.

Decided

Deaths are short for foes and personal for machines.

A foe dies in one short blast. A machine dies at the end of the enemy's turn, one at a time, in its maker's own explosion with a gap left for Jenny: Minerva two booms, Wainwright & Sutler winding down, Kestrel crash and fire, Aphelion discharge and beeps, the Arsenal flames over a ghost. No knocks; a shield breaks in a discharge, without shards.

Decided

The console is a nineties war room.

StarCraft, MechWarrior 2, Descent: retro-future and weighty, never arcade, chirpy or fast. Bowed glass and plate that groan, a boop in the register of StarCraft's nuke warning, a pulse, a soft click. Each kind of event has its own figure, since shared pitches read as confirmation; heat signals never fall (cancel) and never rise (confirm).

Recommended

Calls made while landing the effects stand until overturned.

A hovercraft steps like a flyer, random pitch goes only on footfalls and struck plates, and there is no route-drawing tick.

Pinned

The weapons' blows are acceptable as they are.

A burn or shock modification adding its own layer is a stretch goal; each family's own foe death comes later.

Jenny

Decided

The console speaks as Jenny.

The Jenny dataset (Dioco), rendered offline by Piper through the mid chain, slowed by a third, plosives tamed, on her own bus about ten decibels over the music, credited "Voice: Jenny (Dioco)".

Decided

Terse and military, and always on information.

She speaks only sometimes, but every time on information: a machine lost, a component destroyed, heat past three quarters, an overheat, the gate breached. A rare personality layer (slang, references, Irish turns of phrase) is the only break in the fourth wall. She comments on the machine from outside it, censors herself under a tone, and calls the flattening corruption.

Decided

A line Ryan has heard stays unless he cuts it.

An unheard line comes in only when he adds it, every pool is rendered in full so each line can be judged, and the buff pool stays generic until the vocabulary is locked.

Pinned

The idle nag ("awaiting orders"); the loss pools stay thin.

2

Design tests

Every mechanic is held to these tests. Slice and Dice is the touchstone: target priority, semi-controlled randomness, build preparation and synergy.

Decided

Elegant fun, never complexity for its own sake.

Simple is fine: if every ability is a wall of text, the game is not playable on a phone.

Decided

A round is about thirty seconds of thought, with one to three real decisions.

Decided

The build is visible every round.

Components act on the die, never as mere stat bonuses.

Decided

The randomness is the player's, and it shows.

The die decides what a machine can do; a rolled weapon's damage decides how well. No hidden hit rolls and no crits. Enemies never roll damage: they draw their move and target from the seeded stream as the round opens and show both, exact, before the player acts (Griftlands); the one chance on their side, Numbed's resist, shows on its chip. One source of randomness means one the player can see and shift: the die, a rolled range, a rider's chance, the foe a Chain's charge lands on (Ryan, 29 October: drawn where the aim leaves room, shown as it lands; the Chain row).

Decided

The game does the arithmetic.

A flat weapon never rolls, however many things feed its number, and the die shows the number its blow will deal, for the foe it is dragged over.

Decided

Chances live only on riders, and riders are additive.

Never a chance on the damage (a rolled range is variance the player sees and can reroll, not a chance) and never a binary win or lose. A chance the player can shift by their own choices is a decision; one they cannot touch is noise.

Decided

A damage rider on an action the player takes anyway needs a limit.

It costs no tempo, so nothing else holds it back (Ryan, 13 October, of the Wainwright & Sutler boiler, the Whip Scorpion since 20 October, whose vents blast a random foe for the heat they shed: the chance is its limit, and the player times it, so it stays a decision).

Decided

The player always has tools against the roll.

Rerolls before or after attacking, flat weapons, modules that raise a floor, visible enemy health: variance is a choice to mitigate, never a punishment.

Decided

Avoid binary play where possible.

A soft counter, taking back part of what the other side gains, beats a hard one wherever it makes sense: burn never stops healing, so a burning healer is a race, not a wall. Hard counters are not banned; the game has a stun.

Decided

Asymmetry over parity.

Pieces need no numeric parity with each other; asymmetric balance is harder, and it is where the interesting choices are. The guard is against must-picks (pick share in the runner, section 11), not against difference.

Decided

Builds grow from soft synergies, never a lynchpin.

The player crafts what works from what they find. A piece without which a build does not work (an ARPG's chase item) makes the player farm for it; this game avoids that (section 7, tags).

Decided

Every pack poses a question.

Finish the resurrectors, interrupt the big hit: never a damage race in new art.

Decided

Rank and file die to a die or two; the big foes open a gap between smashing and playing.

Ryan, 21 and 22 October, from the scale discussion: three tests the numbers are held to. Kill times: a rank-and-file foe dies to one good blow or two ordinary ones, so the round's question is which die goes on which foe and no combo is needed to clear trash ("One to two for rank and file makes sense"); a massive foe takes a team a round or two of focus, a boss three to five with its mechanics. The coordination gap: on the big foes a team that sets up and cashes (a Shock under the hitter, Flak under the rest, area on a bunched row) should need about half the rounds of a team that spends each die on the nearest foe, since armour makes basics bounce and setups pay and the HP span lets several rounds show it; on trash there is no gap, by design (Ryan: the big foes "might need to be higher in order for strategies to actually emerge during the skirmish that aren't just smash your face against it"). The wear: hull never heals in a battle, so a machine fought through about four hard fights without a withdrawal arrives at the boss worn, not wrecked ("Four hard fights might be okay"), read on a bot that never withdraws.

How the runner reads them: rounds to win per cell beside the win rate, and the gap as blind play's rounds over the planner's (section 11's rungs) on the big fights and on the rank and file, counting only the cells both rungs win a quarter of; the wear in the campaign report. Blind play is a rung of its own because the random rung's waste read as slowness on trash too, which is not the team working together. What they are read on (Ryan, 23 October: "I think your suggestions are sound"): the kill time on a squad of four, the squad the player fields (section 4), a trio's figure a diagnostic beside it; the gap on built teams fitted with the setup pieces a run finds, since a stock is basics (section 10's tiers row) and a team of basics has nothing to set up. Set aside: lower massive HP, which would close the room the gap needs (if the planner still matched blind play on a built team, its valuation was read before any content moved); and for Wainwright & Sutler's light trio, whose far higher wear on a formation is accepted since its two armour-0 machines are where its decisions are, a point of armour on its cars (against armour by role, section 5) or softer rank-and-file blows. What the runs leave is section 12's. Numbers moved to pass these are placeholders until the bands say so.

Decided

Mismatch costs time, never ability.

A wrong loadout fights longer or takes more damage, but always acts.

Decided

Never helpless, sometimes unlucky.

The design removes helplessness, not luck.

Decided

Rule text is a budget, not a cap.

About three rule texts on a chassis's description, its passive and its modules; a component's rule shows in its own popup and is not counted (Ryan, 12 October). Each is one idea in three lines at most (an upside and its cost are one idea). Common components carry none; a rare one carries a unique rule (the chase: the player may break the game now and then); a module is a small number and a stat line. Judged running on a phone with every trigger firing; the Clydesdale overruns it on purpose.

Decided

A map action that buys a fight buff is not a condition.

Moving or standing still, joining a fight, being caught: the player does these anyway, so the buff they switch on is a boost for a good part of the fight, and completely free.

Decided

A one-off exception for balance is a symptom: find the root cause first.

Ryan, 18 October: "If a situation occurs where it seems reasonable to propose a one-off exception for balance reasons, look into the root causes as to why that seems like a reasonable solution." The case in point was the revolver: "Rapid once a round on it" looked reasonable because a Rapid chain on a stepping die was free, and the root was that Rapid pieces paid no heat, which the stock pass priced, not a rule on one chassis. A passive may still break a rule as a one-off for its design (section 7); this row is about exceptions reached for to fix a number.

Decided

The offence, defence and heat triangle: every component has a price, and a build that cuts a side and stays effective is the accomplishment.

Ryan, 28 October, with heat a core pillar "undervalued as a gameplay element": the player works the triangle for efficiency as in a eurogame, "cutting one of the sides of the pyramid out while still being effective should feel like a huge accomplishment, and the enemy threats are the wrench that prevents a fully efficient build from just doing its infinite thing forever". So every component carries a price, in heat, hull or shield, vanilla vents excluded; a cooldown can be the price, and a payoff or consumer piece that is a dead entry without its setup needs no other. The basics carry heat too: a heatless basic "teaches the player zero heat as the norm and not how to interact with the heat system", and it "neuters many possible tier 1 chassis and makes others irrelevant (Puffin, for instance)", since a kit that reads an ally's heat needs allies that run hot (retired the same evening: Claude's lean that heat 0 is the basics' mark). Passive cooling is the lever that makes the player meet the system by playstyle or by build, Ryan's lean to cut it ("maybe it should be?"), so a kit cools by its vents, its cold entries or a chassis whose identity is its cooling (the Kite's 2, the Swallowtail's, the Giant Petrel's), read before it lands (the heat ladder's cooling 0 of 27 October warmed every row ten to fifteen points of peak and moved no rounds, the greedy holding dice rather than venting, so the read wants a persona that vents). The cooling turn wants options beyond a plain vent, "although sometimes you do just need a big fat vent": vent payoffs have a place (a vent that cashes what it vents, a different piece a maker). The levers the heat knee by the maximum read on 28 October (docs/measurements.md): the warm classes bite on a cold die only at a maximum of 8, and a fleet at 8 overheats the hot kits every round, so the bite comes from the prices and the cooling before the maxima, and a soft zone or a harder overheat last. The session is the to-do's; each price is content's and read on the vanilla ladder's heat grid before it lands.

Decided

Every rule and guideline is open to debate.

A good reason moves any of them: the tests in this section, a maker's profile, a settled row. That is how the game iterates and improves, so a proposal argues for changing a rule in the open rather than designing around it.

3

Campaign loop

The campaign is the run ("run" never means a battle), and battles are its floors. The rules are in docs/rules.md, the campaign part.

Decided

Losing the capital loses the run.

Everything in the run goes with it; only the pool, the codex, difficulty tiers, a Sponsorship piece and livery carry over (section 10).

Decided

Five cities.

The capital at the centre (the Council and the Arsenal) and four around it, each hosting a maker. A city's gate is its only health number and carries between battles.

Decided

The mission map is a choice of front, never city management.

A front's card names its domain and guest, its ground, the Convergence's tier and how long its gate holds if ignored; its formation is fuzzy until scouted.

Decided

The whole roster goes to one front; the fronts left alone bleed.

Ryan, 13 October: the intent, which the records had let blur, is to bring every machine to one front and fight it out while the other open fronts weaken their cities by attrition; the fronts keep moving and new ones open, so every front is tackled in time. Splitting the squad across two fronts was never the intention. A hangar larger than the squad is the answer to a battle that goes badly: spare chassis let the next front be fought without sitting the whole team out. The rules already say one battle a turn with up to four ready machines, and the campaign runner sends every ready machine to one front; what thins its team today is wrecks sitting out with no spares, which the hangar and the economy answer, and fights inside a battle that one machine fights alone (section 4's pacing row).

Decided

The capital's gate is the run's life bar.

Into the Breach's grid power: fallen cities drain it, drawn as hordes marching on the centre, and the finale is fought on what is left. Its repair is the one priced repair, so it weighs survival against the team.

Decided

A city falls when its gate reaches zero, fought or not.

It costs that battle's salvage and its maker's future blueprints, research and stock, never the player's kit; its rift stays open and its horde drains the capital.

Decided

No forced last stand, and a won battle restores the gate free.

The map keeps its one choice and no city runs on a one-way clock. The restore is free of salvage, not of Favour: the win's fee is docked a point per gate point lost, which is what pushers let through cost (section 3's tally row). The free restore is a lever for higher difficulty tiers.

Decided

A front is a rift whose domain is rolled as it opens.

Never fixed to a city: a fixed pairing would solve the map.

Decided

The Convergence makes the finale inevitable; finals are a choice.

Three bosses of one domain beaten draw its hero into a final, whose win beats that domain, gone from the rifts (the row below) and the finale (section 9). Four finals are impossible by design, so which domains to chase is always a choice: one or two the goal, three a feat.

Decided

A beaten domain's rifts close.

Ryan, 25 October: "if a domain gets beat, other rifts for that domain should close. That probably helps answer our questions on how to achieve a perfect run - closing off domains gets you the turn or two you need to make it possible. That's the difficult but achievable difference." His calls of 26 October: beaten means its final won, and every standing city's rift it leads closes then, a free reprieve: the city is quiet for a turn as a won city is ("if that is too easy then we take it away"), its gate stays as it stood ("Only a win. Again, a tuning point"), and nothing is paid for the closure ("You probably get paid extra for beating a domain final, though": the economy pass's number). The domain leads and guests no new rift, and keeps its place as the guest of a rift it was dealt into before. A fallen city's rift stays, as art: "there's no reason to interact with it anyway, so the domain it was doesn't matter and it shouldn't be selectible to find out". It also ends a second final of one domain refighting the hero it was beaten of (the mechanics audit). The exits: the quiet turn if the reprieve plays too easy, the gate if it should mend. Built 26 October.

Decided

The Rupture meter and Anathema fill by the threat-weighted tally.

Ryan, 13 October: what a foe is worth by its threat powers both the battle's meter and each domain's bar, because a count of fights or kills would make picking off small packs with flyers the optimal play and leave a strong foe worth seeking only for its drop. The gate is the other price on that play: a won battle restores the gate free, but the win's Favour is docked a point per gate point lost, so pushers let through cost the roster's growth; whether that bites is the dock rate and Favour's worth, the economy pass's numbers. Threat-weighted, a detachment's kills add little meter, so its worth is defence and tempo (section 4). The meter counts live during the battle; salvage still tallies at its end. Built 30 October: menace, read off the content (a foe's heaviest blow, damage times strikes and a breach one more, plus a quarter of its HP rounded up, an elite's doubled; no new number per foe), is the one weight for the meter, for Anathema (the tally by domain, recorded beside the count of three bosses, which still decides the final until Ryan judges the count against it) and for salvage (two a point, set so a battle banks about what it banked at ten a kill).

Decided

Difficulty grows first by smarter foes and nastier compositions; a tier's units carry the numbers that keep them relevant, and the question is what makes the tier.

Aims, conditions, behaviours, back ranks, elites and pack count, so upgrades buy adaptability against questions, not damage against hull: an aspiration, not a rule (Ryan, 28 October: never-inflate "needs to be removed as a rule. It's an aspiration. But with component tiers and synergy payoffs, inflation is just a matter of course for some units", and tier-two units "will still need on average bigger numbers than tier 1 just to stay relevant"). Measured (docs/measurements.md, 28 October, the 200-seed matrix grouped by chassis tier): against the rank and file at their one set of numbers, a fight that costs a tier-one machine with its stock kit half its hull costs a tier three a third, and the tiers' kit buys options more than numbers, so a tier's units need the floor that keeps them a fight against the tier's kit. So a tier's rank and file are their own units, with their own numbers and names (section 9's recolour row), the floor set against the tier's kit and read on the bands; HP is the general ramp, the fight's length, the Meadow's count in its place, and the damage's shape is each domain's identity sharpening as the tiers rise, a spike, a breach, a volume, a return, with a guest's members in the formation so no hard counter steamrolls a domain (Ryan, 28 October: "these all sound like domain identities to me ... each domain's identity should also sharpen as the tiers rise, with guest appearances preventing the player from building a hard counter"; the levers row of section 9); armour is the wall's number whoever fields it, Flak and Overcharged its answer; and the question stays what makes the tier. What stays out, with Ryan's reason ("I don't really want to have a 5hp goblin in tier 1 ramp 1 and a 50hp goblin in tier 3 ramp 10, that's lame"): the same unit scaled in place by tier or ramp. A boss, a final's hero and the finale's line scale in place (section 9's boss row; Ryan, 26 October: "the numbers do need to scale to keep the challenge up, with both ramp and tier", the columns being few), and a promoted form keeps what its class change adds. A battle has one lead domain and at most one guest until the last tier, whose signature is anything from the roster.

Decided

The tier is the kind, locked and shown; the ramp is the count and the speed, live and unshown.

Ryan's form (the curve proposal, 24 October: a soft ramp with the Convergence and jumps per rift the player sees and chooses; 30 October: “tier (the hard scaling) and ramp (the soft intratier scaling)”), decided 30 October. The tier is set as the rift opens, by the Convergence's tier then, never changing under the player and shown on its card: who comes (the tier's compositions and recolours, the guest), the formations' and patrols' sizes (content's, by their tier bands), the Fractures a battle (three, four, five), how many elites and which, the boss's numbers and escort count, the loot's tier. The ramp is the live Convergence, the same for every open front and never shown: the compositions on the field at the start (one formation and two patrols at the start of a run, three and one at its top, the lead's and the guest's in turn) and their density (each member's strength scaled, one to one and a half times), Rift Pressure's fill (twelve turns to eight), the tears a peak (one, two from Convergence 8), the seam's creep; one table of placeholders (sim/ramp.gd). The ramp is monotone and never resets at a tier step: with the tier locked and the ramp live, a reset would make an old rift easier by waiting for the step; so a tier's first battle is its new units at the ramp's current count, the gentlest those units ever come, and the step is the kind alone, which keeps the stages from spiking (Ryan's worry of 24 October). On neither: the thresholds, fractions of the field's menace at the start, and the step inside a battle. An old front fought late is its tier's units at the current count and timer: the safe front costs the same time and kinder units, so the trade shows. Ryan's goals of 23 October stand as the shape the table serves (early simpler foes, thinner formations, a longer timer, about one elite; middle mixed, denser, shorter, elites with curveballs, guests; late little fodder, very dense, short, elites regular), and targeting rules stay a lever for the advanced foes (section 5's aim row). Retired: the rift that deepens while left alone (Claude's form; Ryan: “Your method works”), which hid a spiral in a front left alone. Every number a placeholder; exit: the campaign runner's reads by ramp and Ryan's play.

Decided

The hangar has no chores.

Repair is free and refit instant; resources go only to upgrades, builds and the capital's gate, and a rushed wreck is the one priced exception. The wreck's time in the shop is why a roster outgrows the squad. A machine reads in two words: in the shop or ready, dressed or bare.

Decided

A team wipe is a graded loss.

The horde strikes the gate unopposed and the city falls only if the gate gives out, so the gate a city carries in buys its survival.

Recommended

A passed turn is a turn.

Waiting for the shop never stops the clock. Built.

Decided

The starting draft is a tender.

One tier-one machine from each city maker, so every maker gets a hearing; the player takes three. The Arsenal never offers.

Recommended

Twelve to sixteen battles, four to six hours with saves.

Ryan, 13 October: a longer campaign is fine, so the roster grows and is outfitted, which is the mech fantasy, as long as a battle stays short; the length to find is where racing the game's power feels right and a new chassis is worth trying rather than the starters being all one can afford. The design already removes the sunk cost: kit moves freely, refits are instant, repair is free, no chassis is strictly better, the fourth slot is empty from the start and wrecks force rotation. The levers are the offer's chassis (one on the table after every win, Ryan, 22 October) and the battle count; the test is how many different chassis a run fields, which the runner reads once offers exist. The acts are the Convergence's tiers.

Open

Sensor contacts, at the campaign layer only.

Formations fuzzy until scouted, scouting a research path. In battle a pack may show as a cosmetic blip, since hiding its type would break the exact preview.

Decided

The quick campaign: battles without the map, a test bed first.

Ryan, 25 October: Slice and Dice's flow with the map and the cities cut out, "useful for testing but also a potential 'quick campaign' mode", so the curve and the enemy's tiers can be felt without a whole campaign, every fight a complete formation, "a bit of a balance stress test". A battle is its fights back to back against one domain, as many as the Rupture's Fractures with the elites folded in ("fold the elites into the fracture count"), then the boss; nothing mends until the boss falls, one field recharge between fights ("we'll see how it feels before giving a full fill"), no withdrawal, and a wipe ends the run. The boss's fall pays the battle's loot, the map's moved there ("replacing map loot"), the token as a component in place of a blueprint, and an offer from any maker ("Arsenal pieces should be in the maker offer otherwise you'll never see chassis"). Kit moves between battles, never between fights, in the game's first hangar. Kept simple on purpose ("this isn't the time to be making a full second game mode when the main one isn't finished"): no shop and no economy; three tier-one chassis picked freely and the fourth from an offer, since the campaign's first battle would not start with four; the campaign's twelve battles for now, since "I'm not sure if player ramp is fast enough to beat a properly scaled final at 20", a short form being scaled differently or started at a chosen tier ("a later decision"). The content it waits on is coded around ("use what we've got"): an elite's slot holds the lead's formation alone until the elites land (Ryan, 25 October, of the bench hero that stood in: "I don't think that using the bench hero is a great elite, but I see why you did it"; 27 October, taking it out: it "obviously shouldn't stay as a stand-in elite, that's just a can of worms"), the boss one of its domain's slate with its formation (the massive stand-in until the slate landed, 30 October), a domain's final drawn as one more boss from tier two (Ryan, 25 October: "finale bosses can just appear as a random boss in tier 2 or 3. Again, can be fine tuned later, I just need all the content in"), and the enemy the same at every tier until the foes' fresh start. It reads what the campaign runner cannot, a squad fighting every fight together (tools/quick_run.gd), and nothing of the map. The rules are docs/rules.md's quick campaign part; built 25 October. An experiment, not a feature (Ryan, 28 October: “don't consider the quick campaign for anything right now, its an experiment, not a feature. I will decide if that changes”): no decision reads its runs and no balance call cites them until he says so; its first battle's reads of 25 to 27 October stay in docs/measurements.md as history. Its lists scroll by a finger's drag from anywhere on them, cards and buttons included, a tap still picking and a drag never (Ryan, 2 November: “quick campaign draft menu is hard to scroll”: a touch on a card stopped there, so only the gaps scrolled); the harness's pages the same.

Pinned

An endless mode, after the bounded campaign.

4

Battlefield layer

The battlefield is the layer with positioning, so the skirmish has none. Its rules are in docs/rules.md, the battlefield part.

The field

Decided

A pointy-top hex grid nine wide, gate south, rift north.

Taller than a screen and scrolled, one unit to a hex. Nine is the thumb's limit, holds two fights apart and gives a detachment room; the two-and-two split it was sized for is an emergency now (the four-machines row), and nine stays until phone reads say the hexes are too small (Ryan, 13 October); pointy-top makes defensive lines straight rows; a hex is at least 48 px wide on screen. Ryan, 30 October: a wider map would need horizontal scroll, which he was never against while the map-edge tags work, and it is parked (“might make a good finale fight at the Capital's gate”): a finale lever, the capital's field wider and scrolled, never a tier's. The reasons it was not widened now: the row's own reason, and that the whole wall struck gives nine lanes against four machines already, so every lane past nine is one the squad was never going to cover, while the phone cost is real.

Decided

The whole wall is struck, and the strike has no floor.

Ryan, 30 October: “I agree about making the whole wall a target”, and “a reduction to the minimum strike to make it more fair”. A pusher ending its walk beside any hex of the bottom rows, the gate's or the wall's, strikes the gate's bar for a third of its strength times ten with no floor but one, so a stray pair chips six where a trio chips ten. The reason: a five-hex apron was the stall's last fort, four ground machines plugging four hexes of it and the fifth taking one strike a turn; a wall struck anywhere cannot be plugged, so the stall's price is unshieldable, which the pricing needs (section 4's stall row); and it sells the horde. The gate stays the look and the bar's name; city defences, when built, stand along the wall. The holes named: a perfect battle gets hard, since four machines cover four lanes of nine, so the dock becomes a tax on every battle and its rate is re-read in the economy pass; the wipe's six phases hit harder. Built 30 October; exit: the campaign runner's gate damage a battle and Ryan's play.

Decided

Every map guarantees the horde a crossing at least three hexes wide through its walls.

Holding every pack takes three machines. Since 29 October the horde wades water as the machines do (the foes' row), so water never cuts its way and only walls make a crossing to hold: a ford held slows the horde and never stops it, and a line is held in a city's streets or between the badlands' mesas. Water and walls are the shape's (the next row); the one water feature a map of 2 October, a river with fords or a lake band, retired with the shapes on 29 October, three of which lay none. Height is landscape and a look alone; no rule reads it (Ryan, 30 October: “scrap terrain height as anything other than a purely visual effect. Too complicated, not worth.”).

Decided

Six map shapes, and the name is the intel.

The player reads a word, never a share, and the word is the shape: each tests one thing the movement rule prices (Ryan, 28 October: “I would prefer to rework them into shapes”), named on the front card (Ryan, 29 October): RIVER CROSSING (a river across with fords: the wade, on both sides, and where to cross), FJORD (water down the field, a lane either side: lane choice and a detachment across water), BADLANDS (grouped mesas and ponds: winding paths through the narrowest crossings), OPEN STEPPE (relatively clear: the baseline), RUINED CITY (bands of towers across with streets: the streets held, a machine to a street, and the flyer’s one crossing of its own) and GORGE (cliff walls down the field: the hardest commitment to a lane). Each passes “mismatch costs time, never ability”. A band’s streets are three or four hexes in all, one to four of them and one to three hexes each (Ryan, 29 October: “we can allow more than one ford in a line, especially for the city map, and each can be smaller, as long as we have a total of say, 3 or 4 empty spaces. It symbolizes ‘streets’, and splitting the team up to cover them all forces the team to fight apart, which is dangerous”), so holding every street of a band splits the team, each machine fighting where it stands. A river’s fords are three or four hexes in all too, but one or two of them: more broke the river into ponds (its laying printed seed by seed), and a ford needs no width since the horde wades. The five types of 4 October retired with them: once moves went to hexes and ground to ground (the next row), a type changed only water and walls, two of the five were looks alone and Mixed Ground tested nothing a shape does not. A battle that names no shape draws one by its seed, every shape alike, and a domain never sets or leans it (Ryan, 30 October: “I don’t see why the domain should be related to the map type in any way. That’s just less variety and more subtle balance to tweak.”); the shape shows on the front card once the mission map exists. The walls are named by what they are, one rule under every name (Ryan: “the busted skyscraper that serves as a ruins crag, shouldn’t be called a crag”): crags, mesas, towers and cliffs, on the ground tap and a fight’s label. The decoration is laid to be read (Ryan: it “can go a long way towards making the map less cluttered and easier to read, while still looking believable”): one or two decorative grounds a shape, never all four; what stands on the ground stands where it makes sense (reeds on the banks, scree at the cliffs’ feet, rubble round the towers); knee-high, so a wall is the one tall thing on the field; dense in the scenery and against the walls and the water, sparse in the lanes; and the roads lead somewhere, or a shape lays none. The relief is the shape’s too (Ryan, 29 October: “I like the look but maybe it’s a bit too much for the phone screen”, then, on Claude’s read, “just go ahead, sounds good”): laid by noise it stood about half the field’s neighbouring land at different heights and jogged a pack’s ring at about half its joints, so the field is level but for the badlands’ mesas on their shoulders and the steppe’s one rise, at most two steps and never a lone hex, and the valleys’ sides rise out in the scenery (docs/measurements.md). What reopens it: the shapes judged running on the phone, which may move any look; the shapes themselves are Ryan’s.

Decided

A move is a count of hexes, and ground is ground: water is the one priced hex.

Every step is a hex. Open ground, rough and ruins are a step to everyone; rough and ruins are landscape, like height. Water costs a ground machine (tracked, walker, wheeled) two of its move and can always be entered with whatever move is left, so a move of 2 wades a hex a turn, 3 and 4 two, 5 three; a hovercraft and a flyer cross it at one, and since 29 October a pack wades it as a ground machine does (the foes' row). Walls stop all but a flyer. Moves follow size and role, 2 to 5 hexes. A way of moving is the model, the footfall and a word a passive may read, never a rule (Ryan, 28 October, asked whether tracks, wheels and legs need to differ in anything but their look: no reason beyond visual variety). Ryan, 27 October, after playing the map a lot: the move points and the terrain table, built "before numbers were reduced, and when upgrades were more plentiful", "just add confusion and overhead for no strategic gain". Water priced was his suggestion of 28 October, taken because it narrows the hover's edge without pricing anything and keeps water a cost in time for the squad. Retired: the per-way table of fast, normal and slow grounds with its stop (2 October), and with it the content check that flagged a ground chassis outpacing another way everywhere. The form that failed is the table; the test it failed is Ryan's play of 27 October with the map survey's read of 8 October (the trip north barely moves, the lanes do): the pathfinder weighed the words, and the player saw a blob and a stop sign. What reopens a second priced ground: a decision the player makes on the map, never a shape of reach or a way's identity. What reopens water's price: Ryan's play finding the squad walled or the wade unread.

Decided

Foes move as the machines do: land a step, water waded at two, walls their wall.

Ryan, 29 October: “Enemies can cross water just like player units, since that seems fair”, which “lets fords be less that 3 if we want, and plugging more difficult on water maps”. Every pack crosses land at a step and wades water as a ground machine does, a water hex two of its move and entered with whatever is left, and never enters walls; a winged foe's pack (the Cockatrice, the Ghost, the Bone Wyvern, the Bomb, the Drake, and their lines' recolours, since a recolour is its base's sprite in a new hue; winged in content) crosses both as a flyer does and may stand there (Ryan, 28 October, the movement proposal's lever 2). That is parity, not a bonus: the winged take a flyer's refuge over a wall, and a hovercraft out on the water is no refuge from any pack. Terrain is never a bonus to foes. Retired: foes moving binary, ground or wall and never slowed (2 October, the Final Fantasy overworld rule that no ground delays a foe); the test it failed is parity, one side slowed by water and the other walled by it, so a river held the horde at its fords and a ford was a plug. On a water map a held line now slows the horde and never stops it. What reopens it: Ryan's play finding a pack's wade unread on the map.

Decided

Nobody stops on another unit's hex; a flyer and the ground meet only in a fight, and packs pass packs.

Ryan, 29 October: “Make sure that everything can pass under a player or enemy flyer, but not stop there”, and its mirror, “flyers and ground never interact except to fight”; and “Enemies can pass through other enemies. That also seems fair, since player units can shuffle around to let each other through, and really, enemy units are flat sprites so it's not like they take up space, thematically”. So a flyer, a machine or a winged pack, passes over every unit and every unit passes under it; a pack walks through the packs; and a grounded machine never passes another grounded machine or a grounded pack, each move landing at once so the player sequences them, while a grounded machine is ground the horde goes round, never a stop. The bodyblock stays, priced by the guarantee row: holding every crossing takes three machines. What it does to the fights is wanted (Ryan: “Fights getting bigger and formation packs staying together has always been a concern of mine, so that's a good thing”): packs no longer queue behind each other, so they arrive together and more of them stand in each other's rings. A tall walker stepping over a unit is open (Ryan: “Not sure if that's a useful property to use or not”); by the next row's ground-is-ground a way of moving carries no rule, so it would be one chassis's passive.

Decided

The player never does arithmetic on the map: the map draws reach and nothing else.

A selected machine's reach is lit, every hex alike under one edge, and a drawn route never leaves it, so its end needs no gauge; the codex, the hangar and the readout give move as a number of hexes; a ground tap says who crosses it, who wades it and who cannot. Retired with the per-way table (28 October): the pip gauge on a drawn route (3 October) and the stop sign on a slow end hex (25 October), which showed the fast and slow hexes the table made; Ryan had found the gauge's boxes "aren't intuitive and covered by a thumb" (25 October). Move rewards, if rewards come, come in hexes.

Decided

The crossing is priced where balance is read, never by a map rule.

Eleven of 24 chassis cross something (six flyers, five hovers), and the briefs add more; Ryan, 28 October: "how do we stop flyers and hovers from taking over the game as the best pieces". Hexes narrow the gap rather than widen it, since a flyer already had no bad ground and the ground machines lost their stops; crossing buys a flank and, over a wall, a refuge, not tempo (equal phases to the rift at equal move; since 29 October the horde wades, so a hovercraft out on the water is no refuge); move follows size, five of the eleven being lights; a squad moves at its slowest, and the depth lives in the fight, where nothing reads the ground. So three levers and no map rule (Ryan: "I think your leans are correct, include 1, 2, and 3. I keep wanting to add more complexity to the map, but ultimately I'm doing more harm than good"): the pricing run prices a crossing chassis in salvage and Favour, or its fill takes a step under its size; winged foes cross as flyers do (the foes' row); a flyer over a wall is no refuge from a hunter, which pounces two hexes and, short of that, takes the machine standing nearest its prey (the fights-picked row). A rule on the crossers (no attacking from water, a price to cross) is the retired table in another form. Exit: the pricing run's read of the crossers, and Ryan's play.

Decided

A pack is one sprite and a number badge.

Machines are their skirmish models with their mounts, turned the way they move; foes breathe and animate to attack; partly hidden objects get an outline. A pack is a composition (the foes' fresh start, 30 October): several foe types with their strengths, dealt from its domain's content as a patrol (one or two front types, three to five foes, a detachment's fight), a formation (a front and a back, the body's fight and the unit the Fractures are counted in) or an elite (the curveball a Fracture spawns), its sprite its lead's, its badge the count of every member, its members listed on a tap, its ring the largest of its members', its pace the slowest, winged only when every member is; a fight on it holds both ranks from the first round, and its survivors are kept by member and return whole (packs keeping their damage between fights was not taken: a number on the map and attrition by withdrawal, whose price is already right). Not taken: a formation as a front pack and a back pack walking together, since while two packs stand adjacent either's ring joins the other, so two hexes buy no peel and no decision, only drift when their speeds part. The compositions are content, authored by hand (the first set a partition of the ladder's lists by tier, placeholders for Ryan's marks), never generated.

Decided

City defences stand near the gate and can be targeted.

The gate's flanking guns first, turrets later; how they work is open (section 12). Not built.

Open

The multi-hex boss.

When and how a boss takes three hexes in a triangle again; the engine keeps the footprint, and the boss stands on one hex meanwhile.

The round

Decided

Packs show where they walk; you fight one fight at a time; survivors walk on.

The enemy never attacks a machine on the map: hunters catch machines, pushers threaten only the gate.

Decided

A move is tapped or drawn; an attack is an order.

Tap for the cheapest way, or draw the route. An order is a tap or drag onto a pack, and nothing opens until ENGAGE, so a mis-drop is harmless. No panels or toggles.

Decided

Moves are planned and played on EXECUTE, ENGAGE or END TURN; UNDO runs in the order things were done; END TURN asks while a machine can still act.

Ryan, 25 October, from play: "should not need to wait for a move animation to complete before performing other actions. Actually, it's probably better to highlight and display the planned move like we used to, and then on engage or end turn actually animate and resolve, with engagement happening after the animations complete. Makes it easier to see who's moved." So a move shows as its route from where the machine stands, the next move or order reads from its end, and ENGAGE and END TURN play every planned move before the fight opens or the packs walk; a move into a hex another planned move left plays after it. And "undo should happen in global order, not on a per-unit basis. So the undo stack is stored in order, and undos in order, always", attack orders included (his "attack orders on the map should be able to undo"): an order with its move into range is one step. A fight is the stack's floor (Ryan: "yes"): what came before it has been played out and fought over, so UNDO reaches back to the last fight or the turn's start, where the per-machine undo took a machine back until it fought. Retired: a move walked at once, which made every next action wait on its walk, and UNDO MOVE by machine. A move is given in one go (Ryan, 28 October, the movement proposal): one move an action, the whole of it, then the machine may fight or stand; what moving in pieces bought, a look at the attack band after a step, the band shows before the move. That closes the line his play of 25 October left open (a move given in one go, or a clearer mark that a machine has move left) by removal: no move is left to mark. Planned moves play on EXECUTE too, and the turn goes on (Ryan, 2 November: “when entering movement, it shouldn't ‘end turn’ when a command is entered. Movement should be able to be executed in sequence if the player wants, and undone. It should show ‘execute’ when a move command is entered, and only end turn if no order is entered. If end turn is hit and there are movements/actions remaining, it should alert the player”): the one button reads ENGAGE while a fight is ready, EXECUTE while planned moves wait, END TURN otherwise; an executed move stays undoable, its machine set back where it stood with nothing to play twice; END TURN asks while any machine can still move or fight. Retired: END TURN playing the plan and ending the turn in one press while moves waited. A caught machine's fight shows as its attack order, the order's line drawn to each hunter that caught it and the board naming it (“when caught, the unit should show the attack order on the enemy that caught it for clarity”).

Decided

The enemy's rings are always on the map, in its violet; a selected machine shows where it could attack from.

Ryan, 25 October, from play: "Enemy engagement ranges need to be a lot more clear. They need to be always visible, and become more visible when the enemy is tapped, and done in red not hud colour", and "when a unit is highlighted to move, it needs to show not only its available move spaces, but its available attacking spaces at the edge of that move, in red". Every pack's ring is a dashed edge at rest, and the pack tapped (or one that would join the fight shown) has its ring tinted under a heavy solid edge with bright dashes crawling on it, in place of the display's-light reach a tap used to show. A selected machine's lit move is ringed by a band, tinted under one solid edge, every hex within its attack range of where its move reaches, and a pack it could be ordered onto is bracketed. Both wore red until 30 October and wear the enemy's violet since, the band with the rings (Ryan: "keep purple for enemy activities"; the band's move his agreeing Claude's lean), parting from each other by structure (a dashed edge against a filled band; the move's lit cells against one band) and from the packs' violet routes the same way (a ring of dashed hex edges against a line with its target), since Ryan is colourblind. The hex grid's resting light came down a fifth on 25 October to calm a busy screen ("base hex outline brightness can be reduced a bit") and went back once the grid's lines held one width at every zoom (Ryan, 30 October: "With these changes, I think it could go back how it was"). A hunted machine wears a tag in place of the orange target under it (Ryan: "a tag hovering by the unit with the hunter's portrait (probably cropped) and some kind of target symbol"): a plate a hunter, up and left of the machine, the hunter's map picture and a target turning. Exit: his read on the phone.

Decided

The button pulses once nothing else is left to do.

Ryan, 25 October: "when no actions remain in a skirmish turn (excluding rerolls), end turn should pulse. Same with the map phase, when all units are out of actions". In a fight, once no die has anywhere to be spent, rerolls left or not; on the map, once every machine has fought, is ordered, is caught, or can neither move nor reach a pack where it stands (Ryan agreed: nothing useful left, "since technically that incorporates eventual commander actions if they appear"), so ENGAGE pulses too when orders wait. Rings breathe out past the button and its face brightens, by motion as well as light; RIFTGATE_PULSE_DEBUG=1 keeps it pulsing to judge it running.

Decided

A machine moves before it fights, never after.

A fight spends its action however it ends, so a hunter pins what it catches until the team kills it. The reason is the pin; a machine that moves after it fights is pinned below, not rejected, and a passive could bend this as a one-off.

Decided

Four machines, moving as one; a detachment is a decision priced by time.

Ryan, 13 October, after the one-to-four measurement (docs/measurements.md): the squad stays four, and the fights come in sizes, formations for the body and small packs that one or two machines take for a tenth of their hull. Concentration halves the hull a fight costs with every machine added, so the squad moves as one by default; a detachment is a decision the map prices with time (the gate under pushers, a flank pack, the meter) and the skirmish withdrawal bails out. The roster needs detachment-capable identities among its fast machines: the cars cannot fight alone and the Honey Badger can, which balancing for real measures as solo value beside team value for every chassis. The stock pass's bands (22 October) read a team of three meeting its decisions only in the formations and a machine alone meeting walls; the numbers stay (Ryan, 23 October, agreeing with Claude's lean): the tanks' base armour walling the rank and file's blows is the armour row working as decided (section 5), three machines against one wildlife pack is the mismatch this row sizes the fights against, and the sizes are the foes' fresh start's. It reopens if the fresh start's fights still hand a squad free wins. The map stays nine wide (the grid row).

Decided

Fights are picked on the map, by ring and range.

Packs join a fight by their ring (front-rank foes small, back-rank foes large, a hunter’s two at least); machines by their attack range (two hexes); a hunter that ends its walk with what it comes for inside its ring forces that fight first. So formation and the order of attack set each fight’s composition. The radii and the range are placeholders for play. A hunter’s ring is one number, its catch, the fights it joins and the ring drawn, and two at least, so it pounces over whatever stands between (Ryan, 29 October: “a minimum range of 2 instead of 1 (wolfs pounce, etc.)”; “I meant one number”). It joins a fight it did not start as every pack does; Ryan asked about that the same day, and it stands on Claude’s lean until he says otherwise: the ring then means one thing on every pack, and a fight started on or beside a hunter is how the player takes it on a fight of their choosing. A hunter keeps its prey while the prey stands and goes for it by any route; only when walls and machines seal the prey off does it take the machine that a walk this turn can put inside its ring and that stands nearest the prey, its caveat breaking a tie, and with none in reach it closes on its prey (Ryan, 29 October: “my target if I can reach him, otherwise the closest machine to my target that I can reach this turn, otherwise chase my target”, reach meaning at all, “which should be a rare case. It’s just punishing hiding, which is the intent”; “Safety in speed, not hiding, seems more fair to me”). Nearest the prey and never nearest the hunter, so it “might force a fight with another backline or detached unit, and not just slam my face into the tank right in front of me”, and a tank covering the hunted “will need to hang back, which is a strategic decision”. So no ground and no line neutralises a hunter: a prey that keeps its distance is safe and so is everyone else, and a prey hidden costs whoever stands nearest it. Retired: the re-pick (28 October, the movement proposal’s lever 3), which moved the lock for good to another machine when the ground alone kept a hunter from its prey and read no machines, so a line of them hid the prey and the hunter caught nobody, the hiding this rule refuses (Ryan: “that was kind of janky anyway”); and the hunters’ ring of one, past which a single machine in the way kept a hunter off its prey. Exit: the pounce overbearing in play, when the ring comes back down (Ryan: “If it’s overbearing then we send the engage range back down”).

Decided

The boss is attacked like any pack, and its fight takes every machine and cannot be left.

It guards the rift, immune until the Rupture meter fills (Ryan, 24 October). The seal is built (30 October): a sealed boss cannot be ordered onto and joins no fight, its ring inert and drawn faint, its readout SEALED, until the last Fracture or the stall guard breaks it (the pacing row); it joins no fight while sealed because a foe that strikes in a fight and cannot be hurt in it is the helplessness section 2 removes, and a sealed boss joining would drag the whole squad into a fight it cannot win (the other form, joining immune, was not taken). The multi-hex boss stays the field row's. Its fight opens with the reveal and the domain's boss theme. Ryan, 25 October, from play: "The boss fight should automatically make all units attacking when one unit targets it, regardless of their range. Disable withdraw for the boss fight - the player is committed to closing the rift or getting scrapped trying. That sorts out the defense issue, and there's no reason to move any units once engage is clicked, just go right into the boss animation. Doesn't matter if a unit has fought this turn already, the only way that would actually happen is 1) the player is greeding for farm, 2) a hunter needs to get resolved first, which is fair." So one order onto the boss brings every machine, ENGAGE goes straight to the reveal, and the fight ends the battle, the rift sealed or the team scrapped; the rift is sealed only when the fight is won, escort and all, and a team wrecked after the boss falls is scrapped (Ryan, 27 October; the mechanics audit's 26). A fight the boss joins by its ring is its fight too, so a machine sent at a pack beside the boss is never locked in alone with it, and the packs whose rings cover the boss join its fight as any fight's do (Ryan, 26 October: "I think it's correct since it forces the player to think about when to engage, but if it's really unbalanced we can re-evaluate"; the boss joining from its own ring is "fine for the same reason, and that can be fixed by removing the boss ring entirely if needed"). Retired: withdrawing from the boss's fight (13 October), kept then because a lock read as the forced last stand section 3 refuses and the withdrawal already cost what it should; Ryan's commitment to the rift settles it, and engaging the boss is now the battle's last decision. What reopens it: a boss that is fought more than once (a regrowing boss, the Rupture re-sealing).

Open

A battle withdrawal: not encouraged, not yet refused.

Ryan, 13 October: never built, by intent, since what the gate then takes is hard to evaluate and easy to abuse, and he would rather not encourage it; then, the same day, that a team wipe already grades a lost battle, so a withdrawal may be possible. Claude's proposal: the map prices it. To withdraw, the machines walk out through the gate: hunters catch stragglers, pushers strike the gate while they retreat, Rift Pressure spawns, flyers leave cheap because that is what flyers are, and a team deep north pays six turns for leaving late. The city then takes the wipe's unopposed phases; the machines are not wrecked. A player forced to play out a twenty-minute loss is the helplessness section 2 refuses. Ryan to decide. Ryan, 13 October, later: the walk-out makes thematic sense; decided later.

Decided

Damage sticks.

Hull never repairs during a battle and damage never slows a machine. The battle is a race to close the rift; blitz or slow and steady, even a defender left at the gate, is the player's call.

Decided

One clock, Rift Pressure, ticking on the map and never in a fight.

It spawns a pack at the rift when full, in place of any cap on a fight. Ryan, 13 October: the tick per round fought went. It stood in for the round cap he refused, but it could not tell an epic fight from a stall, taxed the five-to-eight-round fights the pacing wants, and punished care invisibly, since nothing in the fight said a pack was forming; measured the same day (docs/measurements.md), it had been spawning packs into the plain player's long solo fights. A long fight now costs one map turn like any other. The guard against a stall is enemy damage outpacing sustain (section 5), the runner marking fights past sixty rounds, and a backstop held in reserve and not built: pressure ticking late in a fight, from round eight or so, and saying so in the HUD. A tick per fight is the lever not pulled: it would price many small fights against one big one, turn-efficient against hull-efficient, and the game starts without it. Built 13 October. Since 30 October (the pacing session) the timer is the ramp's, twelve turns at a run's start and eight at its top (section 3's curve row), a peak sends a composition from the rift and a patrol from every tear open before it and creeps the seam (the tears row), and the step by which each peak shortens the next is on the table at zero (the stall row); the backstop held in reserve, pressure ticking late in a fight, stays unbuilt.

Pacing

Decided

A battle is a short run of fights toward a boss the player earns.

Ryan, 13 October: four to five meaningful skirmishes and then the boss, the count flat across tiers and the threat rising with them, never twenty two-round fights, which would make every build burst. Claude's targets until play says otherwise: a meaningful fight costs a four about a tenth of its hull, a battle 60 to 80% of a fresh squad's hull at competent play, the boss with its formation the exam, a battle twenty to twenty-five minutes. The enemy's compositions and the engagement rules produce the shape. Built 30 October (the pacing session): the Rupture meter fills with the menace of the kills (section 3's tally row) against three, four or five Fractures by the rift's tier, the thresholds fractions of the field's menace at the start, the last at two thirds of it and the steps growing (1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3), so later Fractures cost more, the thresholds follow the tier's numbers and the ramp's count without a table of their own, and the Rupture is earnable from the field alone with a third of it left to leave and pay for at the gate; a Fracture pays out as a fight ends, the log naming it and the elite the tier names emerging at the rift (the first, the first and third, the first, second and fourth); the last Fracture is the Rupture, which breaks the boss's seal, and the stall guard breaks it too when no pack but the boss's stands as a fight ends, so a team that cleared the field never waits on the clock. The prize a Fracture may bring is not built (Ryan, 24 October: the picks are the battle's roguelike part, “maybe not warranted at all”), and whether one rides each Fracture is decided after the meter is played, with the economy pass. Exit: the campaign runner's reads of Fractures reached, turns to the Rupture and the stall guard's share (docs/measurements.md, 30 October), and Ryan's play.

Decided

Three clocks, and what each prices.

Rift Pressure is time, the Rupture meter is progress, the gate is neglect (the pacing session, 29 and 30 October; Claude's lean, Ryan: “your reads on everything seem great”). The formations stand on the field at the start as the meter's fuel; a peak of Rift Pressure sends a composition from the rift and a patrol from every tear; the elites arrive at the Fractures.
