So every turn asks progress, defence or the curveball, and nothing the rift sends is needed to earn the Rupture. The arithmetic that set it (29 October): at a timer of twelve a bot battle ended before the clock fired and a fifteen-turn battle saw one pack, so the timer priced nothing; the pushers and the Favour dock were the clock, the whole fee gone by turn eleven against three domains of four; the boss could be rushed in a six-turn walk; and with the meter on kills every spawn was fuel, the rift opening itself. What reopens it: a read that the three pull the player the same way every battle.

Decided

The names: the Rupture meter, a Fracture, the Rupture.

Capitalised. A Fracture earned in a fight pays out when the fight ends.

Decided

A stall is priced, never free.

Ryan, 30 October: “As a player, I like tanking and tanky builds. I know that if allowed, I'll build a thick team that refuses to take hits and burns enemies down slowly. I also know that in this game, I don't want a 2-hour battle where I take no damage and get crazy amounts of farm to be a legitimate way to play.” The flood alone cannot answer it: with each peak shortening the next, a stall is finite but not unprofitable, every spawn a kill and every kill salvage, so the flood feeds the farm until the break, and a thick team at a five-hex apron kills it as it lands. So three prices that exist or are decided: the gate, through the tears (a pack landing on the apron strikes that turn, and the whole wall is struck, so a team holding the gate pays the fee whatever its shields); the yield, by menace (the reinforcements light); and the flood's break. The step inside a battle, each peak shortening the next, is on the table as a ramp entity at zero until the turtle read asks for it (Ryan: the escalation rate “should be on the table”, since “a team that specs for it could maybe stall better than a team that doesn't”; the flood's bite is relative to the team's power). Two design tests: the flood breaks any team at about the same turn whatever its kit, read on the thickest fours; a fought battle meets one or two tears, a stall five. Refused, with the reason: a loss when the seam reaches the gate (a round cap by another name, and a loss with nothing to fight is the helplessness section 2 removes), and a forced boss (Loop Hero's: the boss's fight takes every machine and nobody leaves, and there is no battle withdrawal to retreat into). On the bench, each with the rule it bends: the battle's salvage capped at the rift's bounty and shown on the card, against section 10's kill row, the cleanest kill of the farm if the weighting leaves it worth doing; a long battle bleeding the other fronts, against a turn being one battle; the seam swallowing the map's loot as it advances (FTL's fleet), the one form that adds a decision, waiting on loot lying on the map. Exit: the turtle persona's read on the campaign runner (docs/measurements.md, 30 October) decides whether the step moves and whether the bounty cap leaves the bench.

Decided

Rift Pressure feeds the boss fight only by what the rift itself sends.

The tears carry the flood off the rift's ring (30 October), so a late engage meets the rift's own last composition in the boss's fight and nothing more: a price on lateness, bounded, and no boss growing harder with the clock. Before, every spawn landed beside the boss and a rush would have raced a boss growing harder, which hung on in-battle rewards that did not exist.

Decided

Tears: the mouths beside the rift's, on the ramp.

Ryan's name (30 October) and his shape: “The Battle starts with just the Rift. As it ramps and the bleed pushes towards the gate, more micro rifts open, and stay open, creating more spawn points without crowding and the packs getting hung up on one another. A hunter spawning at a forward location is more favourable for the hunter, just like the pushers have more threat the closer they are to the gate. The most threatening formations can spawn from the main, large rift, but spreading other rifts across the X axis of the map makes it harder for the player to stay grouped, which forces riskier detachments to prevent chip damage from stray packs.” Built the same day: one clock, Rift Pressure, with more mouths as the ramp rises. At a peak the rift sends the cycle's next composition, every tear open before the peak sends the patrol cycle's next, the seam creeps south, and the ramp's count of new tears open on the seam's row, each on a free land hex far from the other mouths and no machine beside it, to send from the next peak, so where the next flood comes from is known an interval ahead (Ryan's question, “do you mean the tear appears before spawning a unit?”, taken: a tear opens a peak before it first sends). Tears stay open. A spawn lands at the end of the enemy's turn on the nearest free hex to its mouth with no machine adjacent, so the player sees it and its walk for a whole turn and nothing appears on top of a detachment. The hole named: the split comes from the seam's row more than its spread, since on a field nine wide a flank is one move away, and a mouth behind a squad that pushed north is the strongest pressure in the game; the seam's creep rate decides whether the late run is hold the line or race the boss, a placeholder the runner reads. Exit: Ryan's read of the tears and the seam running on the phone, and the turtle and detaching reads.

Open

The landscape twisted by the rift.

Ground shaped to funnel the horde; rift tiles per domain (bone yards, lava, the nether) as real hexes with rules, commoner near the rift. A flyer weakness, if wanted, lives only on a rift tile. If a skirmish ever reads terrain, it reads the ground the enemy paints and never our own (Ryan, 30 October: “If there’s ever a skirmish terrain effect, it will be on enemy painted terrain, never normal terrain.”); height is a look alone (the crossing row). Ryan, 29 October: art only for now, no rule on a rift tile (“I don't know how much mechanics we want to cram into a feature that only becomes relevant late into a battle, plus the fact that the foe carries a flattened area with them would make it very hard to play around. Goes against the simplicity we just gained. I will think about what makes sense.”). Decided within it, 30 October: the flattening is the clock's picture. The seam, the row the rift's bleed reaches, creeps south two rows a peak of Rift Pressure as far as the apron, the tears open on it, and no rule reads the flattened hexes; a loss when it reaches the gate and a rule on the flattened ground are refused (the stall row). What stays open is the rift tiles' own rules, if any, Ryan's.

Open

The round's numbers by play.

The rings, the attack range, the hunters' moves, the fights' sizes, slipping round a ring-one front pack to its back rank. The rift sends formations since 30 October (compositions, section 5's pack row), and a dedicated hunter foe landed as the Reaper, an elite placeholder for Ryan's marks (section 9's tiers row).

Pinned

A chassis passive that moves after a fight; a map taunt for a slow Arsenal bruiser.

The round stays move, then fight.

5

Skirmish layer

The skirmish is where the depth lives. Its rules (the round, dice, reach, keywords, buffs and debuffs, mitigation, heat, injury, foes) are in docs/rules.md; this section says why they are so.

Shape

Recommended

The player side has no positioning, and the round is one interleaved phase.

Roll, lock, spend and reroll in any order before the enemy acts; the interleave is what damage ranges earn. A zoomed-in hex grid was set aside: it repeats the battlefield's decision and fights thumbs in portrait. Built and played.

Decided

The enemy stands in two ranks of four, never wider.

A phone-readability cap. Ranks are enemy-side only, bodies in front and problems behind, and they hold.

Decided

Foes strike in the order they stand, and the heaviest hitters deploy left.

No initiative numbers.

Decided

Reinforcements fill gaps at the end of each enemy turn, and a kill clears its slot at once.

A brief window on the back rank.

Open

Ways for the player to act on the reinforcement phase.

Decided

No round limit.

A cap punishes defensive and status builds, and so did a Rift Pressure tick per round (section 4). Withdrawal and tuning keep a fight finite, enemy damage outpacing sustain, with a meaningful fight five to eight rounds (section 4's pacing row); a stall, which the runner marks past sixty rounds, is a number to tune, never a clock.

Decided

Withdrawal is open at any point of the player's turn, and always costs one enemy turn.

Nothing may skip that turn: forced contact is bad strategy, not bad luck.

Decided

The enemy's turn replays blow by blow.

Parting blows included, before the next dice or the outcome. The beat is the ENEMY PACE setting. The player's spends land the same way, a strike at a time (Ryan, 2 November: “flechette pod and other multistrikes need to clearly show the strikes in order, with individual ticks of damage. The damage animation is individual, but the damage is not - damage needs to be timed to the animation across the board”): each strike fires on its own beat, its blows land with it, every gauge stepping down as its blow lands with the blow's number rising off the foe, a killed foe standing until its killing blow, a last act's damage landing as it is named; the gap between strikes is a placeholder to judge running. Each foe is lit as it acts, a violet glow behind it and heavy corners breathing round it, from a part of the beat before its move lands until the next foe's lights (Ryan, 2 November: “enemies need to highlight during their attacks, starting right before”), the lead taken from the beat after the last blow so the turn keeps its pace; RIFTGATE_ACT_DEBUG=1 cycles it to judge running.

Decided

Every kill counts, however a fight ends.

Toward salvage and the Rupture meter. Defeat wrecks the machines (section 3).

Weapons

Decided

A weapon is rolled or flat by its niche.

Flat is reliable (a flat blow kills a foe of its number every time, a range only some of the time) and the finisher; rolled is not, and is the opener and the boss chipper. Ryan, 13 October: heat is a resource and a balancing attribute beyond damage, the secondary cost beside a cooldown (section 8), and like reach it is set by the component's niche. Rolled or flat and hot or cold are four corners, never two pairs: flat weapons making heat and rolled ones none was the old kinetic and energy classes' split, never the rule, and the stock pass priced heat by niche. Where a flat weapon's average matches a rolled one's, the reliability is paid for somewhere, heat the usual place, or flat becomes the default; where it does not, heat is free to say something else about the piece.

Decided

No weapon classes, no crits, no Execute, no Steady.

Each failed a test of section 2, and the test is the rule: crits put a chance on the damage; Execute was a binary kill; Steady was an average, a number dressed as a rule; classes asked for parity. A new idea that is none of those is free to be proposed. Chances live only on riders. Tags carry no rule of their own (section 7). Damage that grows with the target's missing hull is a unique rule on one piece today (the Coriolis Accelerator); a second carrier would make it a keyword, as with the Boar's spill. A foe that gets back up is answered by Suppress, Disrupt and kill order.

Decided

Two reaches, and no falloff.

Direct reaches the back only through a hole; Indirect reaches anyone. A third, Close, was a worse Direct whose price existed only against a back rank: two archetypes are plenty. Melee is a tag; the old close weapons came down to Direct's band with the stock pass.

Decided

Reach is priced.

At equal heat Direct hits harder than Indirect, unless the runner shows heat is the better price for Indirect: the numbers carry the price (measured 11 October, docs/measurements.md).

Decided

Indirect is reach only, and area belongs to a keyword.

Ryan, 11 October, agreeing the line-up's proposal: danger close goes, so a beam or a phased emitter can be Indirect without exploding, and an explosive says so with an area keyword on any reach; danger close priced nothing and only steered Indirect toward the back. Gone since 22 October, the Artillery Rangefinder's rule that spared it replaced by the Ram Frame's mirror (the catalogue row); which explosives carry the area keywords is the row below's.

Decided

Area by shape, never by strength.

Ryan, 11 and 12 October: three shapes, three keywords, Penetrate the column, Spread the row (the neighbours, never the whole row, so the aim still matters) and Splash the plus, each a footprint with its own aim; an explosive needs no price on the firer (measured 12 October, docs/measurements.md). The number the foes past the aimed one take is the keyword's, printed: Spread N, Penetrate N and Splash N (Ryan, 28 October: “it fixes the most issues and opens the most design space”, big hitters that chip their neighbours as shrapnel does, and dedicated area pieces that clear trash with flatter spreads), read on the die as Burn N is, each piece's own number, and a piece carrying the row and the column carries Splash N; the firer's modifiers and consumers then land on N as on the aimed foe's blow, and each foe struck reads its own (the blow's order, below). Retired 28 October: the half, rounded down, decided at the old scale as a proportional price (three points on the Badger, 11 October) and built 26 October at single digits, where it failed the kill-time test's arithmetic: a neighbour's 1 or 2 kills no rank and file at full HP, so it was a removal and not a half, and rounding up or a fixed 2 read the same as it (docs/measurements.md, 27 October); whole area, the other form, made the carriers must-picks (the 23 October grid) and the aim moot on a swarm, a price on the firer buying nothing at 100% wins. Each carrier's N is content's, a placeholder set 28 October on Ryan's go from Claude's proposals in chat. A debuff's area carries no number, and the validator holds every weapon's to one. Built 28 October. Chain is the fourth footprint since 28 October, the conductors, a status in place of a geometry (the Chain row below); area is no part of Kestrel's brief, so the Kite is never balanced around it. A reach over every foe is a keyword too, Every Foe, and its mirror on the team's side Every Ally (Ryan, 2 November: “Every foe is a targeting mode, just like spread, penetrate, and splash, it should be listed as such and given a keyword”; working names after the foes' Every Machine): a chip on the die and a line in the list, no star, and a Silence mutes it as it mutes the shapes. Every Foe stays a debuff's (the Heliographic Siphon's lure-all), since a weapon reaches wide by a shape with its number; Every Ally is a shield's or a buff's (the Mooring Screens'), never a vent's. Built 2 November. The column keywords earn their keep once the foes' fresh start puts back ranks in more fights.

Decided

Chain is Shock's other payoff: the blow arcs to every Shocked foe on the field, then charges another.

Ryan, 28 October, on Claude's proposal in chat; its form 29 October, his pick from six looks measured that day. Chain N: the blow lands on the foe aimed at as ever, and N on every other Shocked foe on the field, wherever it stands and whatever the gun's reach, the firer's modifiers riding each arc; no strike is aimed at a conductor, so its Shock neither doubles the N nor is spent (Shocked in his words the same day: “when targeted and struck by a weapon, double the damage recieved and remove Shock”). Then one other foe at random, neither the target nor already Shocked, is Shocked: “Damage all other non-targeted enemies on the field that have Shock, then Shock one other random non-targeted enemy that doesn't already have Shock.” What it sells is a conversion of single-target burst into area, and it costs the setup, a Shock and the arc, “so it needs to be worth the two spends”; the charge it leaves is the next arc's conductor or an ally's doubled strike (“setting up a bigger chain next time”). The decision it makes: which charge an aimed strike cashes, each one cashed a conductor fewer for the next arc, and when to arc; over rounds, since Shocked never fades, the field charges, which the foes' Cleanse and Ward answer. A fourth footprint beside Spread, Penetrate and Splash, a status in place of a geometry, numbered as they are; each is read from the strike's own foe, so an arc spreads nowhere and a foe an area reaches never arcs, and a foe both beside the target and Shocked takes one blow, the area's (Ryan, 29 October: “Chain is an AoE shape, so it doesn't proc on spread/penetrate/splash procs, right?”). A playstyle, “the thinking man's AoE”, and Aphelion's lean (the maker row of section 7): most Shock and Chain setters land in its content, any maker crossing over as usual, the Arc Fault Cannon the first carrier; the amps that ride every blow ride every arc (the catalogue line). The charge's foe is a draw, and the draw is its cost (Ryan, 29 October: “This random is ok by my books. Thematically, it's lightning, and it should be harder to control”): it is free reach, it sets itself up, and it serves single-target and area damage alike, while the player steers it by aim, the target and any foe already Shocked never drawn, so “random is an appropriate cost, I think, to make it a bit less reliable”. It lands no damage and shows as it lands, before the next spend, so it is a setup the next spends read, where the ping's random blow was noise (below). Claude's lean, a pick steered by aim (the foe beside the target, or the next in strike order), retired the same day; what reopens it: a play test where the draw reads as noise rather than lightning. Retired 29 October: the arc that doubled and spent a conductor's charge, built the 28th and read as a plain 4 on today's kits (another machine's doubled blow spent nearly every charge before the arc fired), and with it “nothing charges itself” (Ryan, 28 October: “Don't let it charge itself”), which his pick of the self-fuelling look reopens: the arc's target is never charged, the charge landing on another. Retired 28 October: the kill and repeat (Ryan: “a no-go, its unbalanced at best and janky at worst”; it was also the Kite's identity on a chassis, which keeps it), and the ping, an on-hit on a random foe for half, since a random target is noise and a half is area by strength. Built 29 October (the conductor from its brief, retired, git show 0cea0ee:docs/briefs/chain_conductor.md; the charge and Shock's reading the same day). Read 29 October (docs/measurements.md, Chain on its form of the 29th): Chain 2 is worth about 0.8 vanilla points over the sixteen big fights (the Arc Fault about 4.8, inside tier one's band), nothing against a lone massive foe and about 2 on the formations, where it outruns a vanilla 6 (3.4 with a spread setter); the charge carries it, since the team's aimed strikes cash each charge within the round and the arcs land on a few spends in a hundred, so the charges never pile up on today's kits. Chain N is each weapon's own number, priced as Spread's is: on the Arc Fault, a 4, the keyword reads about 0.75 vanilla points at Chain 2, 0.9 at 3 and 0.95 at 4 over the sixteen (2.2, 2.6 and 2.8 on the formations), a point of N pricing only the arcs, which are rare while the team cashes the charges, and the charge, which no N moves, the rest; read again when a spread setter and a player who holds charges make the arcs common. The Arc Fault keeps its Chain 2, and the Buzzard its stock, a Chain gun its own setter (Ryan, 29 October: “Keep the Buzzard stock”).

Decided

The blow's order: the firer's modifiers on every blow, each foe struck read for itself.

Ryan, 28 October: the base for each foe hit (the roll or the flat number for the foe aimed at, the keyword's N for the others); then the firing unit's modifiers and consumers on each strike and each foe, flat then multiplied (Overcharged, hopper loads, Spun Up, the Cassowary's sixth shot, the Kite's doubling: “a consumer from the firing unit should apply to the whole attack”); then the struck unit's debuffs and the consumers that read it, each foe for itself, flat then multiplied (Flak, then Shocked last, read only by a weapon's strike aimed at the foe, never by a blow that only reaches it: Ryan, 29 October; the Backdraft Charge eats the stacks on each foe it hits and pays that foe alone: “If only one unit is on fire, that's the only one that gets spiked”; the Coriolis's bonus each foe's own missing HP at the moment it is struck); then the struck unit's armour and buffs, each for itself (Armoured; Exposed zeroing it); then the final damage. One formula on both ends, and the rule the mechanics audit's 9 and 19 found missing, where the firer's modifiers were read once on the aimed foe and that blow carried to the rest. The Kite's double stays on the printed number with the entry's own reads inside it (an area's N, heat fired, hopper loads, each foe's own missing HP and stacks consumed) and the buffs added once after (Ryan, 28 October: “keep the kite how it is, my version would make it stronger and I'm not sure it needs to be”); doubling after the firer's flat adds, his first reading of the order, is its buff if it ever needs one (“Note it as a buff target if it ever needs one”). A foe's own reads (its missing HP, the stacks a consumer eats on it) land after the firer's multiplies, flat, beside its Flak, as his chain has them (Ryan, 28 October, on the question whether they sat inside the printed number: “The die should show the calculated result after going through the described chain. There should be no ambiguity”), so neither the Kite's double nor the sixth shot touches them. A foe's reads are taken as the die is spent, and every blow of a strike is read before any lands, so a spend never feeds its own read and a kill feeds the next strike, never the same strike's other blows. Built 28 October.

Decided

The whole die is in view in the codex; tapping a die shows the entry showing.

A reroll needs the whole die in view, so the codex, a double-tap on the machine, lists every entry; the die's readout, a tap, turns the entry showing into text, a number a passive moved highlighted there and never its source listed (the tap-hold-drag row, Ryan, 25 and 26 October); passives belong to the machine.

Control and statuses

Decided

Burn never stops healing.

Damage over time soft-counters healing, taking back some or all of what is healed.

Decided

The Pocket Paradox is a meter.

It counts applications of other statuses, never stacks, so tripping it takes several components or a hit carrying several statuses, and it tunes by its threshold. A chance to suppress was binary; a debuff that zeroes damage was toxic against fodder and bosses alike. A trip that Numbed resists still spends the meter, so it never sits primed to try again, and so does a trip on a foe already suppressed (Ryan, 17 October: "Suppressed is suppressed - they lose their next action. It does not stack, it does not carry. That way lies madness"). Its existence never dictates another design: a status that feeds it counts, and the count is tuned for the final ecosystem (Ryan, 17 October).

Decided

Numbed is a buff nothing removes, and nothing touches it.

A buff with a cannot-be-removed line, not a kind of its own (Ryan, 18 October: its own kind was one type too many, "no reason to have a billion types of objects"), which nothing reads, deepens, spreads, cleanses or purges: a stun resistance that effects can change is where other games' combat loops broke. It decays, so rotating stuns is a slope, not a wall, and its slope says spread the stuns. No foe resists by nature: the chip shows who resists and for how long, and a phone has no room for an innate figure. If a sure first stun on a boss proves too strong, this is revisited. A suppress on a foe already suppressed for that action does nothing and numbs it no further (Ryan, 17 October: Suppressed never stacks and never carries, a Paradox's trip included). Built 17 October. A landed suppress numbs a boss three, whatever its size, a final's hero and the finale's line with it (Ryan, 30 October: “Numbed should be by boss, obviously”), a massive foe three as before (“I guess that's ok, maybe”), and the rest two; built that day. What reopens it: a foe that wants a number of its own (his “Maybe it's a per-unit thing?”, section 12).

Decided

A status stacks or renews, as its definition says.

Ryan, 17 October: renewal without stacking must be supported, "a pretty common thing for a powerful or non-number-related buff to do". The engine reads the policy off the status: add, the default, a stack a grant, a timed grant keeping its own end (Ryan, 16 October: "Timed stacks should decay based on when they started. Not stack and refresh."); renew, one grant whose end is the later and whose count the larger (Suppressed, Retaliate; and the untimed one-stack statuses, on which a renewal changes nothing: never stacking and renewing are one state, parted only by a timer). Section 6's table checks its stacking word against the policy. A status either runs down to zero on its own clock or is timed, never both (Ryan, 18 October: "Things either decrease to zero, or are timed, not both"), and the validator refuses rounds on a halving status. Built 17 and 18 October.

Decided

Armoured and Overcharged count in points, and Overcharged halves.

Ryan, 17 and 18 October. Both sides say Armoured, and a stack is an armour point, so the chip's count is the armour ("Easy to read at one glance"); its duration is the row below's. Overcharged is a damage point a stack and halves at the end of each round, rounded down, whatever granted it, until it is gone: a stack fading a round let a source feeding two a round climb without limit, where a halving one settles near twice its feed ("to prevent stacking to the moon and fueling massive ramp strategies"), and Ryan would grant it to more things on that clock. Rounded down, because a status decays to zero or is timed, never both (the row above): rounded up, one stack would never end. The Ammit's Quintessence Tap keeps every Overcharged on it for the battle whatever granted it (Ryan, 18 October: "that's the rules text, it doesn't care where it came from"), never between battles, everything resetting at the hangar; its release valve was to be an enemy silence or purge (Ryan: ok); since 28 October no purge takes the Tap's stacks (the silence and purge row), so should the Ammit "prove stupid overpowered" at single digits, where each stack is worth more, the valve is the to-do's test and nothing else is considered before it. The Overcharge Coil grants 2 (Ryan, 18 and 22 October: +10 for a round was "silly high" for a support action spent the same turn as a combo, and at 1 the halving leaves next to nothing). The halving stays the clock of every status fed by events (Ryan, 22 October): a stack a round climbs without limit under a steady feed, so one clock everywhere would need a cap on every feed by another name. Built 18 October. Reopens if the count moving by halves reads badly on the chip in play, or if a buff wants a run-down the halving cannot give.

Decided

Burn is a numbered keyword, and Burning a damage point a stack.

Ryan, 18 October: "It should be fixed to a numbered keyword, like a weapon applies 'Burn 2' for 2 stacks, irregardless of damage. Also, in the spirit of flattening numbers, burn should only do 1 damage per stack." So an entry with Burn says its stacks (Burn 3: three stacks of Burning on each foe hit), the first numbered keyword; a foe's Burning loses a damage point a stack at its action, so Burn 3 is 3, 2, 1 over three actions. On a machine a stack is a heat point (section 11's scales row). Each piece's number is content's. Built 18 October.

Decided

Stun is the best mechanic in any game, so adding hard control is always weighed first.

Ryan, 13 October: Suppress is prevalent and needs dialling back, and designs kept reaching for a stun or a burn in place of build space. The roster analysis's caps (no hard-control piece below tier two, one locked on a die, one hard-control passive a maker) were struck as artificially limiting (Ryan, 15 October) for a rule: adding hard control is always carefully considered, and other options explored, before it is suggested or committed to. Ryan, 17 October: a cooldown is no price for a Suppress, since Numbed is in effect one (on one foe the landed rate settles near one stun in three rounds however often it is tried); the more mitigation the game adds, the more Suppress must be priced so the other options stay attractive, since scarcity would make its carriers must-picks; without hard control a tank can become required, so damage must stay a viable way to win, Suppress may be allowed more widely if gated by a condition worked for or a price paid, and off-tank mitigation (a Barrier) is part of the answer. Ryan, 18 October, on what prices it: not the damage it deals, which is incidental, since a stun's worth is a turn where the party is not under threat (Darkest Dungeon's lesson, taken less far); so a Suppress is gated by a condition, a setup, a self-sacrifice or its timing, and the player decides whether to build a team that makes it worthwhile. The Paradox is the model. Retired: a Suppress debuff (Claude's), which priced the stun where it is weakest, on fodder, and not where it is strongest, on a boss.

Ryan, 22 October: no Suppress piece sits in any chassis's stock, the Baboon's least of all ("no reason for it to be able to stock a suppress that powers a suppress, that should be a build choice the player has to make"), and none below tier two, by reason rather than cap, so a gated stun is a find and the team that uses it is built for it. The stuns by gate: the funnel-web's web by its timing and the Paradox by setup; the Umbra Inducer by Volatile, the Arsenal's at tier three ("probably") with no condition, its hull price compounding with the Arsenal's other Volatile pieces; the Backdraft Charge, the Arsenal's at tier two, a consumer of Burning (a point a stack consumed, and a Suppress when it consumes two or more, Claude's fill, so one Burn 2 piece opens it), each stun trading the fire's ticks for the turn of safety; and the Suppression Battery, Wainwright & Sutler's at tier two, out of the Clydesdale's stock, Suppressing only a foe carrying Flak (Ryan: the maker's condition), its blow low on purpose. Built 22 October. The exits: a gate that leaves its stun unpicked loosens before the piece is cut, and a stun the runner shows as a must-pick gets a stiffer gate, never a cap.

Decided

The action economy: Rapid is priced by a gate, and each of its wants has a home of its own.

Ryan, 20 October: "rapid is turning into the new Suppress in terms of build ubiquity"; more Rapid pieces will come ("which we can and will do, but it will feel samey regardless"); "action economy is often a chase in these styles of games. Commander actions are one obvious area." Rapid sold five wants in one keyword and was the only component-level source of any of them, the Suppress pattern: another look at the die, a second action on one machine (setup then cash), spends counted (the line frame's combo, the Cassowary's sixth shot, the Paradox), a small effect that costs no turn, and tempo lent. The direction is the stun row's applied to tempo: Rapid is priced by a gate and never rationed, and each want gets a home that is not a Rapid piece. Another look: own rerolls as a number (a module or a passive), on the machines whose identity reads its die, priced so no module is the universal pick. A second action: the team's order before self-sequencing (an ally's action as the pair: the Dingo's Shock to the Kite's cash); a die mechanic where the sequence is the identity (the Cassowary's stepping); and where one machine must act twice, a price in a banked resource (rage, the hopper, Spun Up, Overcharged, Kestrel's heat), a cooldown burst or a delayed action (the bomber's mines, the web). Spends counted: counters count blows, not spends, so strikes and area pieces are their fuel. The cantrip: combined entries, two effects on one spend (a vent that shields), with nothing to chain and nothing to limit. Tempo lent as a status is Rapid by another name, ranked last. Beyond the dice, commander actions on map-turn cooldowns (section 8): action economy nobody can stack.

Three rules of thumb for every design, rework and rebalance: a design that wants many blows is pointed at strikes; one that wants order is given a die mechanic; one that wants a second action pays in a banked resource, a cooldown or an ally's turn; Rapid stays for the cantrip, gated (if it kills, on a cold spend, once a round, by a cylinder). The stock pass applied it (22 October): the ungated carriers priced, the Laminar Deflector a tier-two blueprint by Ryan's rule that a Rapid shield is never below tier two and a Rapid vent never below tier three, the Wake Diffuser a vent that shields, every new Rapid gated. The exits: if the gates leave Rapid unattractive on every carrier, they loosen before the pool shrinks, since the keyword is a Kestrel lean and not a pest; if an own-reroll module becomes the universal pick, its number comes down or it moves to passives. The reasoning in full is docs/briefs/README.md's.

Decided

Purge strips a foe's buffs, and the foes carry buffs worth stripping.

Ryan, 12 October: the EMP is the heart of the Science Vessel homage, so a dispel is wanted as a keyword whatever the count. Its reach, widened 28 October ("having more than one purpose for a player purge is important though. Enemies can certainly carry offensive buffs such as primed in addition to kindled on more units, and defense buffs like barrier, ward, Regen, or a delayed heal or something, shouldn't be out of the question. Whatever you think adds healthy depth"): the foes' own buffs (Armoured from a brace, Kindled, Getting Back Up) and the machines' Primed, Barrier, Ward and Regenerating crossing to the foes under the same words (section 6's convention), each granted by a telegraphed special and each with an answer beside the purge (kill, suppress or disrupt the rallier; Expose or breach the brace; kill the reraised twice or mine its slot; Ward the stoker's target; pop a Barrier with a cheap blow; wait out a Ward; burst what regenerates), so a Purge is a decision and never the one key; a delayed heal is a countdown, Suppress's and Disrupt's. Numbed, the buff nothing removes, and Guarded, the parts' rule, stay out of its reach. A keyword on any entry aimed at a foe: on a capstone's blow (the jellyfish's EMP) or alone on a debuff piece, Indirect, one at tier two by lean. Its name is Purge on both sides (Ryan, 20 October: "Use Purge for both, for now"), Strip the hangar's verb. No player silence: Suppress holds a special and Disrupt turns one, and the stun discussion kept control scarce. The set, its carriers by domain and the ladder they ride are git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md's, for Ryan's marks. Built 28 October with the ladder, its piece the Null Field Emitter (Aphelion's, tier two, an Indirect debuff in no stock, a blueprint found); read on pick share.

Decided

Silence mutes a machine's keywords, Purge takes what a die face bought, and a foe's Cleanse takes the team's marks off its own: the enemy's questions to what the tiers buy.

Ryan, 28 October, on Claude's analysis ("Agreed on silence, purge, and cleanse"). What the tiers buy is options and stacks (section 3's row, the measurement), and nothing on the enemy's side questioned either but killing faster, so these three are aimed at the archetypes rather than at everyone, each a domain's lean (section 9's levers row), each a special and never a rider on a blow (a tax with no decision), each a one-round window shown a round ahead with answers on the die: it costs time and never ability (Ryan, 17 October). Silence (Ryan, 27 October: it "makes sense to only disable (positive) keywords, and not touch buffs"): the machine it lands on is Silenced, its components' keywords muted through its next round, printed and granted alike (a Rapid acts once, a Spread lands one blow, a Suppress suppresses nothing, its own Cleanse a plain shield), its buffs counting and its stores filling as ever; cast with no condition, aimed at the armed; a Ward turns it away and an ally's Cleanse lifts it, the team's answer. Retired 28 October: buffs alone (25 and 26 October, built that day), a one-round purge that returns and so a duplicate; and the muting of 21 October that took Volatile's price with it, which Volatile as a cost (26 October) leaves nothing to mute. Purge (Ryan, 27 October: "not that threatening with the new mechanics we introduced, regarding resource bars and active buffs ... I think that would let purge be an actual mechanic and not limited to rare bosses"): the machine loses every buff a die face bought, every stack, for good; what a store, a form or a passive grants (the Armadillo's seal's armour, the Badger's plate, the line frame's stacks) goes for the rest of the enemy's turn and comes back at the start of the player's turn after its start resolves, the cooling, the roll and the round-start passives first, "a mild punishment"; the stores stay (the store row); the Ammit's kept Overcharged is its passive's own ("overcharge never decays and cannot be purged"), noted for testing at single digits where each stack is worth more (the to-do). Aimed at the buffed, cast where a machine carries one; a Ward turns it away; a Last Act never purges, since a death blow lands in the player's turn and would hold through the whole enemy turn after. Its placement is its threat: a special lands at the purger's place in the strike order, so a front-rank purger strips before the hitters and a back-rank one after them, and the Swap moves it. Carriers by lean the Waste's casters and the bosses, a tier-two and tier-three question, read on the buff pieces' pick share and the Cassowary's row (its Spun Up bought and carried) once a purging pack exists. A foe's Cleanse (Ryan, 28 October: "a domain lever - debuffs are hard here", the Marsh's): a healer's second job, a special on one of its own carrying a mark that takes every mark the team laid (Burning, Flak, Cryo, Vitriol, Shocked, Exposed, Webbed, the Paradox, the Drone), never Numbed (the buff nothing removes) and never Suppressed (a cure that un-stuns an ally before it acts would kill the stun answer the 22 October discussion kept scarce); weight one, never twice running; its answers kill or suppress the cleanser first, spend the Shock before it acts, burst. One word for one effect on either side (section 6): Purge, Cleanse and Silence on both, Dispel a move's name (the Augur's, a purge special) and the engine's id. The front's card warns of a purge among the leaders (the mission map). Built 28 October with the ladder (section 9's recolour row), the silence's mute from the 21st's code; the first cleansers the ladder's Spectre and Wraith, and no foe silences or purges until Ryan marks the carriers (the to-do). Exits: the duration and the carriers' weight if one bites too hard, never the exemptions. Bench: one verb for keywords and buffs alike, nothing permanent (the domains could not differ, and the stack question needs the bought buffs gone).

Decided

A store is a capped resource with a bar of its own.

Ryan, 21 October: "All stores have bars, since they are resources with a cap. A bar is under the heat display ... depending on how it fills. Being silenced does not prevent a unit from filling or using its stores (balance lever if silence doesn't hurt enough). If a bar grants an active effect, that effect is displayed as a buff. That effect can be silenced. If it would be purged, it effectively gets silenced for a turn instead." So a store (the Muster, the rage, the hopper, the Cassowary's shot count, the Musk Ox's magazine) is no buff: no Silence or Purge touches its filling or its use, and only an effect it grants is a buff. Since 28 October a silence touches no buff at all, so a store's effect sleeps under a purge alone, until the start of the player's turn after its start resolves (the silence and purge row). The shape follows the fill: the Muster a smooth bar in a segment a charge; the rage boxes up to its cap of five (Ryan, 22 October: "5 for the nemesis is fine"); the shot count its pips; the hopper boxes up to its cap with each load's armour a buff, as the Badger's rework has it; the line frame's combo a smooth bar with a tick for each power. The Ammit's kept Overcharged is a buff and no store, and since 28 October its passive's own, which no purge takes (Ryan: "overcharge never decays and cannot be purged"; noted for testing at single digits). Built 21 and 22 October; the hopper's cap and plate wait on the Badger's rework.

Open

Buffs and debuffs in place of the word "status".

Polarity belongs to the effect, not the carrier: burn is bad on anyone, a damage boost good on anyone. Two kinds, buff and debuff, and no third (Ryan, 18 October): Numbed is a buff nothing removes; a banked one (Rage, the hopper, the Muster) is no status at all but a store, a capped resource with a bar as heat and the magazine are, and what a store grants is a buff (Ryan, 21 October: the row on stores). A Rapid component's switch-off is not a debuff and never cleanses. Ryan, 12 October: an Arsenal cleanse that moves a machine's debuffs onto a foe waits on this, since only a debuff that means something on either side can move; since 13 October one does, Burning, a heat tick on a machine and a hull tick on a foe (section 7).

Decided

Corrupted stays its own debuff.

It acts like Volatile, a spend that costs hull, but a foe puts it on, a cleanse takes it off and it ends with the skirmish, where Volatile is innate to its component (Ryan, 11 October).

Decided

Armoured lasts one turn of the other side, from every source today.

On a machine until the round's end, every stack at once; on a foe, from its brace, until its next action (Ryan, 16 October: "If it's effectively braced, it should be braced too"), so each guards against the other side's next turn of blows and neither lingers. A defensive buff that lingered, a stack fading a round, would stack into a wall (Ryan, 12 October), and armour is the dearest stat there is (section 10's prices). A longer Armoured is not banned: it needs a drawback of its own. Built 16 October.

Decided

A rally lands at once and rides each foe's next move, and the intents count it.

Ryan, 26 October, on the mechanics audit: "howl is more clear if it gives kindled for the next action, which could be the turn its granted. Makes it more difficult, certainly, but that's not a bad thing at this point. Kill the wolf if you can reach it, or if you can't, take out as much fodder that is going to hit you this turn, or get swung on hard"; and on how the player reads it: "The intents should take into account incoming buffs. They should have a pulsing version of the incoming buff as a preview chip, so the player knows it's incoming and that's the reason for the increased damage." So Kindled lands on every other foe on the field the moment the rally does: a foe acting after the rallier hits harder this turn, one that has acted carries it to its next move, and that move spends it whatever it is (a special gains nothing, a Suppress that takes the move takes it, a countdown carries it to its blow), the clock Armoured keeps on a foe (the row above). Every intent counts the rallies on their way from the round's start and drops them the moment the rallier is killed, Suppressed or Disrupted, so the enemies' numbers stay exact (section 2's randomness row); a foe whose blow a rally lifts wears the coming Kindled as a chip on a dashed edge, its opacity pulsing on the gauges' clock (Ryan, 26 October: "Similar in concept to the health and shield bars"), never told by a hue alone. Kindled is the foes' Overcharged, an add before the move's factor: "Rules parallel on both sides, so that the player doesn't have to know two formulas", and "One formula means one damage formula followed by the whole game. Not that we need to display a formula. The text is irrelevant. If it's unintuitive that holy multiplies after, that's a problem either with the move itself, or a problem with how we display attacks." The form it retires: Kindled live from the next round, granted once the enemy's turn was over (13 October), which kept the intents exact by keeping a rally out of the turn it was announced in; the test it failed is clarity, a howl that lifted nothing until a round later. Built 26 October, the swarm's add moved before the factor with it (no number moved: every swarm move's factor is 1); the pulse's swing is a placeholder, judged running (RIFTGATE_PREVIEW_DEBUG=1). The exit: the runner's bands reading the Meadow's or the Peak's cells as a wall for the rally's sake.

Decided

Retaliation punishes the striker with a debuff; thorns need a drawback of their own.

Retaliate is a buff on the machine whose work is the debuff it leaves on whoever strikes its hull. Thorns, damage paid at the blow, scale with enemy blows, strong against a horde and useless against a boss, and on a tank they stack into a wall: dangerous rather than forbidden, a thorn is balanced only by a drawback it carries and never rides a megatank (Ryan, 12 October). Retribution is the Arsenal's strongest suit (a lean, section 7); a retaliation elsewhere needs a fantasy of its own. Shocked doubles the team's next weapon strike aimed at the foe, so the tank sets up kills, and does nothing else: the damage a Shocked striker once paid on every blow was thorns left in, on a tank, and it went. What reopens a reward for being struck (Ryan, 20 October, the Whip Scorpion's Siphon): the machine spends its own turn to be struck, deals nothing that turn, and the reward comes later and at random, so it is paid for in tempo, not the old discharge riding free on a turn the tank took anyway.

Decided

Each foe picks its own target as the round opens: at random by default, by an aim where its threat calls for one.

Shown and committed. Random is the baseline the numbers are balanced on, so they read the same whatever the player's composition; an aim serves a foe's threat and the response it asks for, on any foe: the Black Mage goes for the hottest machine, and a late-game rank and file might go for the lowest hull or pick its area move to hit the most. The foes pick in the order they strike and never over-commit: a machine the blows already picked would wreck, through its shield and armour, is passed over while another stands, Slice and Dice's rule. The pick is smart as it is made and never after (Ryan, 26 October: "there's a difference between setting the intents smartly, and using the preview to change intents"; Slice and Dice's foes avoid "a unit that is already prescribed to die, as of game state at the intent pick moment"): during the player's turn it moves only by a Lure, by its machine being Phased, or by a Disrupt whose foe finds its machine gone or Phased, or whose old move aimed at no one machine, so a brace turned into a blow picks afresh ("otherwise changing the ability should still keep its initial intended target"; "If disrupt would change a buff to an attack or something ... it should still retarget"); a foe whose machine has fallen when it acts strikes the next machine in line then, whatever its aim ("I disagree with breaking the next in line rule even for special targeting rules like black mage. More unpredictable info"). The round preview never moves one: "don't get stuck in a preview loop where a tank lures a bunch of foes, the lure predicts it to die, so then the subsequent attacks on it get retargeted, and then it looks like the lure didn't work". There is no taunt stat: redirection is a spend that shows on the intent (Lure), and there is never a dodge chance. Ryan, 24 October: "if every enemy has a targeting rule, it's impossible to get a true baseline that's agnostic of player composition", so "Take away, balance, then add where appropriate"; enemy AI rules the player feels they must learn add overhead and little depth, and a foe goes far on "this guy hits hard", "this guy protects his allies", "this guy heals", "this guy debuffs" and "this guy is going to kill my unit". And, the same day: "It's the baseline that needs to be random, not everything, otherwise we have nowhere to go other than adding stats to make harder enemy types. The targeting needs to serve the type of threat and the response required", while "sometimes a treant just hitting hard randomly is more than enough". Slice and Dice's foes target almost purely at random a larger party, and it "feels plenty fair and challenging". An aim is the curve's targeting lever (section 3). Retired: a sense on every foe, the exposed weighting its default, which read the team's shields and armour into every foe's numbers so that no baseline was agnostic of the player's composition, and the codex saying what each foe goes for. Built 24 October.

Decided

A foe's blow may land on every machine.

Ryan, 15 October: a gap the foe kit refresh filled, the storms first (Root Storm, Quake, Bone Storm and Cleave, each one blow on every machine at the factor its three used to share). A move tagged all ("Every Machine" in the codex) lands each of its strikes on every live machine in turn, left to right, with its riders on each, so no aim and no Lure moves it; its arrows reach every machine, the forecast counts it on each, and the plaque names it once. Its number is what each machine takes, so a move with it is priced well under a single blow from the same foe. A shield saves only the machine it stands on; the answers that stop the whole blow are at the striker: kill it first, Suppress it, Disrupt it.

Decided

Every threat shape has two answers or more, and none has all.

Lure, Suppress and Disrupt touch a telegraphed move; shield and armour take it; Breach beats the buffer by design, and a blow at every machine beats Lure. Cleanse stays while riders persist.

Decided

Damage redirection is common, never a tank's lock.

Lure today. A draw by an ally (an interpose) comes back only on a locked piece, never as a core mechanic (Ryan, 12 October): Guard was built and taken out because a standing draw made one machine the target of everything, which is the lock; a conditional draw on a locked piece passes (the Onryō's Pall is the proposal, the Warrior's Cover its mirror on the foes' side).

Pinned

A deterministic reflect as a boss effect.

Decided

Phased: cannot act, take damage or be targeted, and only before it acts.

Ryan, 20 October, on Claude's ruling: "cannot act, take damage, or be targeted" is the cleaner wording, and it holds until the round's end; granted only to a unit that has not acted this round, so the status always costs the action it protects (his first form, invulnerable now and the next action lost, offset the two halves). Not targetable means allies cannot vent or shield it either and a foe's intent on it re-picks, which moves the intent lines as a dock should without a lure; it still cools by its own cooling, so an overheated machine phases with its problem intact, and ticks and overheats still land. The one rule gates every grant of it: the Swallowtail's Mooring Screens, and Aphelion's planned pieces.

Mitigation, heat and injury

Decided

A shield absorbs its number, and the excess passes to the hull.

Ryan, 14 October. Under the old rule (a standing shield blocked a whole blow, however small) a small shield entry was secretly "negate one blow", worth the biggest blow it met rather than its number, and the content kept rediscovering it (small stand-up shields; a screen on every ally negating four blows for one spend). Now a shield is an honest number, a breach ignores it outright (the shield stands), and the one-blow block is a priced, single-target buff of its own (the Barrier, below). What follows: shield answers the big blow by its size, not by standing; the Peak's telegraph wants a shield sized to it or a Barrier; the Buzzard's regen and the Puffin's overshield are hit points. Shield returns only at the field rate out of fights, so withdrawing is no reset. Built 21 October.

Decided

Barrier and Ward: the block is a buff, one machine at a time.

Ryan, 14 October, the upside of the honest shield. A Barrier on a machine means the next blow a foe lands on it deals no damage, so it never injures, until the round's end; its riders land, and a blow of nothing leaves it standing; a blow it takes shocks no striker. A Ward means no rider a foe lays lands on it that round, a blow's or a move's of its own, breach included; a blow's damage lands (Ryan, 25 October: "Should stop all riders, even from non-damaging attacks", after the mechanics audit found it passing every special by, the Peak's Eruption, Fira and Pyroclasm, the Drake's Stoke and the Summoner's Diamond Dust among them). Both up is the old whole block at twice its price. They answer different shapes, the Barrier the Peak's telegraph and a boss's blow, the Ward the Waste's breach and the Peak's scorch, so a team carries what its fronts ask. A Barrier is never granted to every ally at once: four blows negated for one spend is the exploit the shield rule removed. The Ward lasts the round because riders come in volume where the big blow comes alone (Ryan), and its worth is thin while few domains lay riders on machines, which the foes' fresh start may change. Ward is the buff's word, so the Oblation Ward is the Oblation Mantle and the Votive Ward the Votive Screen (Ryan, 15 October). Ryan, 17 October: Barrier and Ward want the same care as Suppress, and if a Barrier grows common the foes need more damaging riders to carry past it (damage over time, on-hit effects, a countdown's blow, a Barrier's intended prey). The carriers since the stock pass (22 October): the Blast Door (Minerva's) grants the Barrier and the Decoherence Veil (Aphelion's, a working name) the Ward, self or ally, tier one, in no stock. Built 21 October.

Decided

Armour is a flat number off every blow, set by role, and a blow it covers is nothing.

Shield answers the big blow, armour the swarm, and straight reduction stays the rule on both sides: a blow at or under the armour does nothing, a bigger one loses the armour, never a damage floor. Ryan, 21 and 22 October, at the single-digit scale (section 11), where a point of plate walls a whole class of blow. Base armour is priced by role, never by size ("I would price it based on role"): a tank 2, a heavy worker 1, a car, a hover, a flyer or a glass cannon 0; timed armour (a brace until the round's end, the Armadillo's seal until it opens) lifts a unit well past its base and is meant to wall ("armour numbers that are only reachable conditionally should allow complete blocks"). A standing point is the dear one, since it walls one more blow size for the whole game, so module armour is scarce, +1 at most, tier two or three, and paid in a big stat. The forms tried and the test each failed, so a new one is free to be proposed: a cap at half the blow (no wall anywhere, and not sharp enough at low armour for Ryan); the same cap with a Braced state walling ("Might as well have no base armour and the flat armour stack as an ability"); a window that stops a blow up to the armour and nothing at twice it ("armour is useless against a boss"); the armour squared over the blow ("the math is cryptic. You need a chart to understand it"); a percentage a point (it rounds to nothing at single digits); a saving throw (randomness the player cannot see or shift; Ryan: no); base armour folded into hull with Armoured the only armour (the Elemental's plate by nature goes with it). What answers a wall is the pack, not the rule: a breaching move (the breach row) and a rally's Kindled lifting a small blow over the plate, while a walled tank takes only its share of the blows unless it lures (a foe without an aim picks at random, section 5's aim row), so tanking is lure plus plate. On our side Flak and Overcharged add before armour, Shock doubles before it, Exposed sets it to nothing, Burning and Vitriol pass it. What reopens it: the tank rows reading as free wins against packs with no breacher, or the bracing foes' cells reading as walls for a basics team, in the runner's bands.

Decided

The Boar keeps its spill, written out, and Breach is not its word.

Ryan, 12 October: the spill matches the Boar's brief and serves "a passive opens design space": it gives flexibility, which the runner cannot measure; if it proves weak it is buffed. No keyword the player learns while it is the Boar's alone (a second source granting it would make it one): the engine runs it as Spillover, a keyword the passive grants every weapon the Boar fires, written out on the die (the star, the passive's line in the die's readout, no chip). Ryan, 15 October: "All its damage or subsequent strikes spill over": the killing strike's excess and every strike after it carry into the foe behind. A carry past a kill stays the Boar's: a multi-hit weapon strikes its one target, because that is intuitive (Ryan). Breach is not the spill's word: it is one rider on both sides (the breach row). Built 15 and 22 October.

Decided

One breach: the blow ignores the shield and the armour, and the whole blow reaches the hull.

Ryan, 22 October: "Is there any reason to merge breach and pierce to simplify it into one anti-tank threat?" Yes, and it is merged. The whole roster goes to one front (section 3), so a rider that beats shields and another that beats plate would sort the makers' leans by domain, a tax on a roster that leans one way at a front it cannot avoid, where one straight-through threat is answered in play: the Ward turns it away, the Barrier eats one, the kill order puts the breacher first, and a seal is not spent while it stands. The rider sits on the move, not the foe, so a foe threatens armour exactly as far as its breaching move comes up, by the content's three dials: how often (a move's weight), when (below half, the finisher going through) and how far ahead (a countdown); the tank reads it each round off the intent chip, where every rider of the blow shows by its mark after its number, a numbered one with its stacks (Ryan, 2 November: “Enemy intents with a rider need to show that rider as part of the intent chip”; the breach was a “!” after the number until then). Set aside: Pierce N, a numbered rider ignoring N armour, which let a lance beat base plate but not a seal; a breach beats the seal whole, so sealing is a decision about who is still standing. Foes carry no shield, so the same word on our guns ignores a foe's armour: the Overclocked Railgun alone carries it (Ryan, 22 October), Kestrel's answer to plate. What it costs: the Waste becomes the anti-mitigation domain outright, the Barrier and the Ward its dear answers; plate keeps its showcase in every swarm that does not breach. A breach's whole blow counts toward an injury, so the breacher is also the injurer. The foes' carriers: the Waste the most, one mid foe in each other domain by fantasy, bosses mostly not, so the seal keeps its job against the big single blows. Built 22 October.

Recommended

Shields are a buffer, and heat is the second resource.

A typical pack wears a shield down in two or three rounds; the Barrier is the timer now, one blow and one turn; heat is a team problem.

Decided

Machines cool at the start of the round and overheat at the end of the player's turn.

So a hot machine can vent first, and an overheat is the machine's own doing on its own turn: with volatile, the one way to die on your own turn. A withdrawal ends the turn too, so running hot and leaving is no way round the burn. A foe adds no heat outright: its Burning lands at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, and its Scorched heats only what the machine aims at a foe (below). An overheat is hull damage for every passive. Cooling stays well under a weapon's heat.

Decided

Heat bites where it is carried; a cold build pays elsewhere.

Ryan, 13 October. Heat is the one cost that competes with damage on the same die (a cooldown is the other, printed on the pieces that carry one: section 8), and what makes a cool gun against a hot one a choice (rolled against flat is a preference, never a price: the effectiveness row of section 11, Ryan, 28 October); priced per piece but never felt, it is decoration. Before the stock pass it was barely felt outside the Peak; the stock pass's loops lowered the maxima so the hot kits bite and the cold ones stay cold (22 October, docs/measurements.md). The test, Claude's placeholders until play: a build that runs hot overheats by about its third round of firing without a vent, so a formation fight forces a cooling turn or two, by vent or by cool weapon, and a short fight forces none; a cold build is a healthy build and pays somewhere else, in a short reach, a lower number or fewer strikes, never for free (a rolled range is flavour priced at its mean, Ryan, 28 October), and collects in the long fights; the Peak's Burning and Scorched press a resource the hot builds already feel. Ryan's caveat: heat is not judged from a lack of content, so whether it bites is judged once the roster has hot builds to feel it. A cool weapon is also soft cooling (Ryan, 13 October): spending it instead of a hotter one is a cooldown turn, so heat builds are a spectrum from a kit tuned to its passive cooling that never vents to a hot kit that buys its heat back with vent turns. Heat is a budget (the maximum plus cooling times the rounds) against a demand (what the damage-optimal spends would add); the efficient build ends near the maximum, which is good play. The worst case for balance is slack: a kit with the hot build's damage that ends the fights that matter with budget unused never paid for its damage, and everything converges on it. What the runner reads, per row, spikes rather than actions: peak heat as a share of the maximum and the rounds above four fifths of it; overheats and the hull they burned; forced choices, where the best-damage entry would have crossed the maximum; the damage forgone to heat (near zero on a hot kit is the worst case); and the slack left at a fight's end. The levers are the maxima, cooling, the weapons' heat and the vents' numbers.

Decided

Foes heat machines only through debuffs: Burning and Scorched.

Heat applied straight is so polarising that it needs several avenues of counterplay, or it spirals. Ryan, 12 and 13 October: direct heat goes, and two debuffs carry it, the player's words reused and acting differently on a machine. Burning, the harsher, adds a heat point a stack at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, then loses a stack, so the player can vent the heat already there or cleanse it: two answers. Metal does not burn, it heats: one word with two effects by body, the hold saying which, and the first debuff that means something on either side, which an Arsenal cleanse moving debuffs onto a foe would need (section 7). Scorched, the softer, makes every weapon and equipment the machine aims at a foe run a point hotter a stack until the end of the player's turn, when every stack goes, so a shield, a vent, a buff or a cool weapon plays round it; the die shows the hotter heat. Both cleanse. A move on every machine takes the softer, since four machines ticking at once is the spiral direct heat had (Claude's fill, Ryan's to move; which foe lays which is content's). Burning on a machine has its own glyph (Ryan, 13 October): Burn's flame filled solid with the thermometer cut out of it, apart from the overheat's bare thermometer and Primed's hollow flame. Heat reads adds before multiplies, as a blow's damage does, so a multiplier on a gun's heat multiplies Scorched's extra too. Retired: Scorched losing a stack a turn, which piled up under several scorchers. Built 13 October. Ryan, 24 October: heat needs to be more threatening, and because the heat debuffs can be countered, with multiple release valves (a vent, a cleanse, a cool spend round Scorched, the Ward, the caster killed or suppressed), they can be significantly buffed; a foe whose jam is heat tries to heat when no machine is hot, and one may aim at the hottest to overheat it, as a boss would try to execute an easy target. The first cut doubles every foe heat stack, its riders numbered as the player's Burn is (24 October; docs/measurements.md): the cold teams feel the Peak now and the hottest starters are walled on the greedy bot, so Ryan's play judges it, and a wall that survives a player working the valves brings the stacks down a step. Exit: the Buzzard's loss (the old direct tick paid its shield every round) and the Peak's numbers are Ryan's play to judge.

Decided

An injury is one threshold and one die roll on bare hull, and it lasts the battle.

The one permanent cost of a bad fight, answered by shielding before a big telegraphed blow. The threshold is a share of maximum hull on purpose (Ryan, 11 October): asymmetric balancing, so the tank and the glass cannon both have a place, and full greedy aggression risks something against its reward. The threshold may move and the rule be reworked; it goes only if it stops serving that purpose, never because a fight's numbers barely feel it.

Pinned

A low-hull berserker or last-stand entry, an anti-spiral.

Recommended

Terrain in a skirmish is a fight-wide condition the machines feel.

Never a bonus to foes. Nothing acts on it yet.

6

Die faces and iconography

What the player sees, and how it reads on a phone.

The die

Decided

A die entry reads at a glance from fixed places.

One standard face for every component (Ryan, 2 November: “the faces are crowded and they don't need to be ... We need a standard format, using combined or layered icons, that works for every component across the board and doesn't crowd the die”): one main mark top centre says what the entry is and holds what it delivers, whom it is aimed at stands under it (Ryan, 3 November: “I also wonder about moving the number inside of the icon and putting targeting underneath, since the targeting icons are narrower”; the targeting inside the mark with the number under it, built the day before, retired beside it: the number read second and the marks crowded round the arrows), its costs run along the foot as a mark and a number each, no word and no sign (“For costs (heat, shield, hull), don't spell out the word. Use an icon + number. We don't need +/- symbols either, the player knows the direction the cost goes”; the cooldown one of them, its clock: “Cooldown should be a cost in the bottom section as well”; the hull a machine's body, the burst it wore first refused), its place on the die top left, a chip per keyword down the right edge, a star chip for a unique effect; what else a spend does sits small beside the main mark (a status's stacks, a vent's blast), and a dark entry is blank with its clock and turns along the foot. The main marks (Ryan, 3 November: “The weapon faces don't really read weapon to me, nor the equipment faces”): a weapon is a sight, a ring with four heavy ticks crossing it, in a little and out to its edge (Ryan, 3 November: “The crosshairs need either bigger sight lines, or have the sight lines extend inwards of the circle”; the slight ticks standing off it alone retired), its blow inside and, for a weapon that strikes more than once, its strikes after it (2×4) (the ring with ticks, retired on 2 November as a small container round an arrow, “too amateurish”, comes back larger holding the number alone, its side ticks stopping short of the widest number; the ring broken for its ticks and a second ring round it benched; the chamfered frame open at each side's middle that replaced it retired, failing to read as a weapon; a blast, an uneven star, benched, reading as a seal; swords are the intents'). Every number a main mark holds is one size, the size the widest fits (Ryan, 3 November: “Rolled damage was just cut in another session so you can measure max width just with the multi-strikes text”): a strike's blow and its strikes, as wide as the Storm Petrel's blow and zap, the sight's ring sized to hold it, and a number steps down only past that width (a two-digit blow struck three times); a number shrinking to fit every entry went with the ranges. Equipment is gear, a cog of eight teeth, holding what it grants or lays (the module, a diamond with its tips cut, retired, failing to read as equipment; a chip with its pins benched: docs/looks/die_marks_candidates.png); a shield is the shield as it was (“shield is pretty perfect at the moment”), its number inside in the shield's colour; a vent is a plate cut as the die is, its number inside in heat's colour, three waves of steam rising off its top (Ryan, 3 November: “If the vent was simplified to just wavy lines, it could be combined with shield as a combo face”, then “Plain vent looks weird that close to the number, maybe it needs a chamfered box back”; the heat sink with its fins retired, it could not combine; the waves standing either side of the number retired, they read as braces round it; the bare steam over the number retired, too close; the steam inside a taller plate benched). An entry that shields and cools (the Wake Diffuser, the Sutler's Hoist) is one mark, the shield with the steam rising off its whole top (Ryan: “although I don't know how to represent both numbers then, maybe as shield | cool in their respective colours”; “Shield-vent should have the vent lines over the whole thing, not just half”): the shield's number left and the cooling right, each in its colour with a bar between, so the two part by their order, his shield | cool, as well as by hue (the steam over the cooling's half alone retired on his word; two small marks side by side and a shield split down a wavy side benched); the Hoist's cooling is that number, no longer the star. A reload cools nothing, so no steam: its shells and the ammo it loads are its whole mark (“Reload doesn't need a primary icon for a single face on a single machine”: the shells are its ammo's mark wherever ammo shows). Equipment prints no number (“Equipment don't generally need numbers”): its gear holds a buff's status, its stacks beside, or a debuff's first keyword that does something to a foe in content's order (so the order picks it; a reach, an area and Rapid stay on the edge), or the star when what it does is a rule of its own, and the edge never shows it twice. The sight and the gear are drawn wider than the shield, a ring reading smaller than a plate as wide, and the shield as wide as it always was, clear of the place's numeral. Exposed's chip is a reticle open at its top, kin to the sight: they part by structure and colour, and if they blur on the phone Exposed gets a new mark. The targeting marks are one width (“standardize the width of those with the width of the 3 hex symbol”), drawn in the HUD's own lines after the first round read as amateurish (“These are the right direction, but look too amateurish. Try to clean them up, change whatever you need to with main or targeting icons to find something that fits the rest of our visual design”), every one hex cells sharing their edges, a lit one a target it may take: for a foe, the enemy's formation from above, three front cells and two nested behind them, the front lit for Direct and every cell for Indirect (Ryan, 3 November: “The direct and indirect icons need redesigning, the arrow looks cheap and not actually clear what it means”: the tracer and the lobbed trajectory retired; side views of the shot striking the front or dropping behind it benched, reading as a plug; two full ranks of three too tall at the standard width; Every Foe's chip keeps its squares, so the two never share a picture; docs/looks/die_polish_candidates.png); for a machine, three cells in a row; the box under chevrons and the solid arrowheads retired with that round. Every main mark wears a lining, a thin line inside it (Ryan, 3 November: “I liked the previous iterations inner lining, that's the kind of thing that moves it out of amateurish, extra little flairs”, after a round without it), the sight's four short arcs inside its ring on the diagonals, clear of the number; and the die's frame is dressed to match (“The actual chamfered outer box of the die can be adjusted too to match whatever we're doing inside, so the whole thing doesn't read cheap line drawing”): a lining inside its border, its cut corners drawn heavy, short accents on its square corners (a rule over the costs benched, crowding the foot). Retired on 2 November: the reach frames as the weapon's whole mark (the crosshair, the arc) and the three hexes holding a piece's glyph (“The wideness of these is janky”). Whom a piece spent on machines reaches belongs to the main mark, never a chip (Ryan, 2 November: “Ally and self chips - those shouldn't be chips, those are targeting rules. They need to be on the main icon”, after the squares of the self and ally chips went unread; under it since 3 November, on his word above): three connected hexes, the middle its own machine and the two beside it its allies, a filled one a machine it may be spent on (Ryan, the same day: “just 3 connected hexes: center filled for self-only, left/right filled for ally only, all filled for self or ally”), on every shield, vent, reload and buff, the default included, as a weapon's reach is the formation under its sight; a reach over every foe or every ally is a keyword's chip (Every Foe, Every Ally: the area row). Retired on 2 November on his word: SELF and ALLY written under the glyph, and the candidates for the aim drawn as a frame round the mark (a ring, a hex; docs/looks/die_icon_candidates.png) with marks of their own for a buff and a debuff (“We're overthinking it, I think”). The place (Ryan, 15 October: “I don't think it's the size that makes it subtle, so much as the font and color choices”) is a grey Roman numeral in the quotes' condensed italic, so it never reads as a stat; it counts the die in its order, fixed components first, then the hardpoints, as the die's readout numbers its entry and the codex its list, and shows which hardpoint holds what, a reroll that lands on a copy, and a stepping die's next entry. A shield or a vent that also grants a status (none does yet) wears it as a chip on the edge, its number inside its mark. The kinds (Ryan, 20 and 21 October; one Equipment since 2 November): Direct, Indirect, Equipment, Shield and Vent, the blank a sixth drawing and no kind; and the glyph has a grammar. The main mark says what the entry delivers and the mark under it whom: the formation's front lit, Direct; all of it, Indirect; the three hexes, a machine; a weapon's, a shield's and a vent's number inside it, and for a buff and a debuff alike one equipment mark, the gear, with no number (Ryan, 2 November: “At one point I suggested merging buff/debuff into one 'equipment' face type”, after the arrows they wore read as push and pull: “those arrows look like push/pull to me”). The foes' intents are a register of their own (Ryan, 2 November: “Intent markers are different. They use different icons, i.e. swords. The arrow can be changed but not to the equipment one, that doesn't make sense”): their buff and debuff arrows stand until a new pair is drawn for them. A debuff is equipment spent on a foe, any entry aimed at a foe that deals no damage (the iconography is what matters, not the engine's label); it prints no number and deals no damage under any effect, its heat runs as a weapon's (Scorched heats it) and its keywords are its whole effect; a gun whose blow is nothing right now prints "0", so "0" is a gun that will do nothing here and no number is not a gun. The glyphs are placeholders, judged on the phone, running. One star stands for any number of rules, so stars never need merging (Ryan, 12 October): the die's readout lists every rule an entry carries, a number a passive moved highlighted there. Built 21 October; the number inside the mark, the linings and the formation built 3 November.

Decided

The die shows the number one blow will deal.

A range if rolled, one number if flat. Dragged over a foe it shows that foe's number. The game does the arithmetic; the player reads it. Its heat is what the spend will add, every modifier in (13 October, so a Scorched machine's die shows its weapons running hotter, and the Kite's its double). A debuff shows no number, and a gun whose blow is nothing here shows "0" (Ryan, 21 October; the row above).

Decided

Every entry is a component, a blank or a destroyed component.

Nothing fills an empty hardpoint, so a full, undamaged machine is reliable. Shown text never says "face" (the player sees no polyhedron); Face is the engine's word for one entry. Whether it says "die" is open (Ryan, 13 October; section 12).

Decided

Keywords are a list, at most three on an authored entry.

Upgrades may add more.

Decided

Gameplay first; the vocabulary is trimmed once the content is complete.

Ryan, 16 October: the keyword budget goes, since it got in the way of good ideas: "We need to build good gameplay, and then if our vocabulary is too big, trim the fat. We're not at the fat trimming stage yet, not even close." The form retired, about ten keywords in all judged per die on a phone, returns as the trim once the content set is complete. Variety still comes from combining keywords with numbers, reach, heat and passives, as in Slice and Dice. For the trim: whether an effect is a keyword or a rule written out is a matter of how common it is and whether something can add it to a component during a skirmish (Ryan, 12 October); what a player must learn is the keywords, the printed rules and the statuses together, so moving an effect from a keyword to a printed rule saves nothing; Rapid and the Nemesis's roll again are one niche with two words (Ryan, 14 October). The merges across the two sides still open are section 12's.

Decided

Three rerolls a round, never onto the entry showing; a copy of it may come up.

These are not really dice, and a reroll that lands where it was reads as a bug, so the entry showing is skipped. Its copies are not (Ryan, 15 October): skipping them too was abusable, since a die of one key entry and copies of one filler sent every reroll from a filler onto the key, so a copy comes up at the odds its copies give it, and the entry's numeral shows that the die moved. Rapid's free reroll works the same way, so copies of one Rapid component chain, each switching itself off (a valid, if silly, build); every Rapid piece carries a limiter, a cooldown, heat or Volatile (section 7), which keeps a chain finite. One exception, a design one-off (section 7): the Cassowary's Spin-Up spins its whole cylinder, itself included (Ryan, 19 October), since a revolver's spin may stop where it was. The player must be told; die size is a balance lever. Retired: Scrambled, a foe's debuff that let a die's next reroll land on the entry showing (Ryan, 24 October): a reroll that lands where it was is what this row removes, and nothing had laid it since the Whisperer, whose Corrupt it was, left with the families on 9 October; the foes' interrupts on the die are Disable and Corrupt.

The vocabulary

Decided

A keyword and the buff or debuff it creates share a name.

Ryan, 16 October, a global convention: "unless there's a really good reason not to, if a keyword creates a buff or debuff, the keyword and the chip should have the same name." So a foe's brace leaves Armoured, the machines' status (Ryan: "If it's effectively braced, it should be braced too").
