The rule binds the words the player reads to their chips; a foe's effect in content (rally, revive once) is no keyword and takes its chip's name. The good reason to differ: one status from many sources keeps its own name, as Overcharged does from the Overcharge Coil, the Soulcutter Talons and the Grievance Reliquary. Still open (section 12): Kindled, the same effect as Overcharged on the other side; and Reraise, which takes a name of ours under the homage rule. Built 16 October.

Decided

A foe has HP; a machine has hull.

Ryan, 22 October: "hull is the label for a machine, not for a goblin." The shown text says HP of a foe and hull of a machine, the vocabulary has both, and heat stays one word, since only machines carry it; the content's and the engine's field stays hp, its comments changing as they are touched. Built 22 October.

The two tables below are generated from content by tools/pages/bible_tables.py (Ryan, 16 October: both maintained in the bible): rerun it after any change to content/*.json, before the bible is republished, and never edit them by hand. A status's effect is its vocabulary codex, the text the player reads; its kind, stacking and duration are its vocabulary entry's design.table, the stacking word checked against the engine's policy on the status (section 5's stacking row). Unique means only a chassis passive or a locked component grants it, or on the foes' side only one foe's moves; global means anything else. Both are counted from the content as it stands, never declared, so a status unique today turns global when a second source lands. A count in brackets is planned content.

Keywords

KeywordWhat it doesUnlocked componentsLocked componentsPassives and modulesFoe moves
On our components
BreachA breaching blow ignores the shield and the armour: the shield stands, the armour counts for nothing, and the whole blow lands. On a foe's move it reaches a machine's hull, and if what gets through is a quarter of its maximum hull or more it destroys a component; a Ward turns it away, and the shield and armour then take the blow like any other. On a weapon it ignores a foe's armour.100—
BurnKeyword with a number. On a player's weapon or equipment, Burn 3 means each foe hit gains three stacks of Burning. On a foe's attack the same: each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Burning on the machine it hit.520—
ChainKeyword with a number, a weapon's. Chain 2 also lands 2 on every other Shocked foe on the field, wherever it stands, where the target takes the roll: the attacker's bonuses land on that 2 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. No strike is aimed at them, so their Shock stands. Then one other foe, at random, neither the target nor already Shocked, is Shocked.100—
CleansePlayer keyword, on shield and vent components. Whoever the component is spent on also loses every Burning, Scorched, Silenced, Disabled and Corrupted status. A rapid component's own switch-off is not a status a cleanse touches. On a foe's move of its own, the same word: every mark the team laid (Burning, Flak, Cryo, Vitriol, Shocked, Exposed, Webbed, the Paradox, the Drone) comes off the most-marked of its own, never Numbed or Suppressed.210—
CryoKeyword with a number. Every foe the entry reaches gains that many stacks of Cryo (Cryo 1: one stack). Any machine's hit with a weapon on a foe carrying Cryo spends every stack and cools the hitter 2 a stack.010—
DisruptPlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. The foe does its other move instead, this round, and its new intent shows at once. A foe with nothing else open is not moved.100—
Every AllyKeyword, a shield's or a buff's. Spent on a machine it may be spent on, the entry lands on every machine it may be spent on, each taking all of it: every other ally for one that goes to allies only.010—
Every FoeKeyword, a debuff's. Aimed at a foe in its reach, the entry lands on every foe in the fight, front rank and back, as it lands on its target.010—
ExposePlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. Every foe the entry reaches and leaves standing is Exposed through the next round: in Direct reach of every machine, and its armour counts for nothing. A second Expose refreshes it, never adds.020—
FlakFlak N: each foe hit gains N stacks of Flak.220—
LurePlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe, a weapon or equipment. Every foe the entry reaches turns on this machine for the round, intents and all; reach and area decide who and how many. A lured foe whose move has no target keeps doing it.130—
OvershieldPlayer keyword, on shield components, rare. The shield may raise its target past the target's maximum; shield past the maximum fades at the end of the round, or as the fight ends. A shield die spent on a machine already at its maximum is never wasted when it carries this.010—
PenetrateKeyword with a number. Penetrate 3 also hits the other rank of the target's column, which takes 3 where the target takes the roll: the attacker's bonuses land on that 3 as on the blow, then the foe's own statuses and armour. A massive foe fills its column, so it is hit once. On a debuff it carries no number.101—
PurgeOn a player's entry aimed at a foe, a weapon or equipment, every foe it reaches loses every buff it carries but Numbed and Guarded, after the blow, so a foe the blow kills stays down. On a foe's move of its own, never a rider on a blow and never a last act's, the machine it lands on loses every buff a die face bought, every stack, for good, and what a store, a form or a passive grants until the start of the player's turn, after its start resolves; its stores stay, and a stack its passive keeps stays. A machine's Ward turns it away; a foe's Ward is one of the buffs it takes.100—
RapidAfter this component is spent the machine acts again: the die rerolls for free onto any other component (a second copy of this one included, so copies chain). Rapid carries a cooldown of one, so the component is dark for the rest of this round, unless it prints its own cooldown; one that prints none pays heat or hull every spend instead. Any component may carry it, a vent or a shield as well as a weapon.241—
ShockPlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. Every foe the entry reaches and leaves standing is Shocked: the next weapon strike aimed at it deals double. The entry's own blow never spends the shock it leaves.210—
SpilloverThe Wild Boar's rule, granted to every weapon it fires by its Juggernaut Drive and run by the engine as a keyword: when a blow kills a front-rank foe, what it had past the kill carries into the foe behind it in the same column. Nothing stands behind a massive foe. No component carries it; it becomes a keyword the player learns on its own if a second source ever grants it.001—
SplashKeyword with a number. Splash 1 also hits every foe round the target, those beside it in its rank and the other rank of its column, each taking 1 where the target takes the roll: the attacker's bonuses land on that 1 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. On a debuff it carries no number.120—
SpreadKeyword with a number. Spread 2 also hits the foes beside the target in its rank, each taking 2 where the target takes the roll: the attacker's bonuses land on that 2 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. On a debuff it carries no number: its effects reach those foes, and it deals nothing.231—
SuppressPlayer keyword. The target skips its telegraphed action this round, then gains two stacks of Numbed (three on a boss or a massive foe). A foe may shrug it off at 10% a Numbed stack; no foe resists by nature.310—
SwapPlayer keyword, the Dingo's Combat Translocator's: the spend takes two targets, a foe and then another foe or an empty slot, and the two trade places; no damage. A massive foe does not move. Drag the die to the first, drop it, then tap the second; drag it again to move it, or tap the ground to set it back.010—
WebThe funnel-web's Web Bomb, locked: the foe hit is Webbed, no damage. After its action the web bursts, Suppressing it and the next foe to act beside it.010—
Printed on our components (the star)
StrikesAn attack's blows per use. A weapon with three strikes lands three separate hits: each rolls its own damage and carries its own effects, so a rapid-fire weapon procs often (the Skua's strikes share each roll, halved and never below one). Against a shield, each strike is its own blow, so once one breaks the shield the next reaches the hull; against armour, every small strike loses the armour, so many small hits are the wrong tool for a heavy target and the right one for a soft one. Foe attacks have strikes too.220—
Vents allAn attack that fires the machine's whole heat as it is spent: the heat gauge is empty after the shot, and every step of heat fired, the step printed on the entry, adds the component's bonus to the blow before the passives. The shot's own heat cost still lands.010—
Overcharged a killA rule printed on a weapon (the Soulcutter Talons', locked on the Ammit), with no word of its own: every foe it kills gives its owner so many stacks of Overcharged (two), which the Ammit keeps for the battle.010—
Grows with missing HPA flat blow that grows with its target's lost HP: it adds its bonus for every step of HP the target is missing, both numbers printed on the entry, read as the die is spent. No roll is involved, so the number is known before the spend, and the die shows it while held over a target.100—
GatedA keyword printed with a condition, such as a Suppress on a foe carrying Flak or on a foe carrying two Burning or more: it lands only on a foe that meets the condition, read as the die is spent and before the blow's own riders, so a spend never opens its own gate. Held over a foe where the condition does not hold, the die shows the keyword's chip struck through.200—
ConsumesA rule printed on a weapon: its blow consumes every stack of the status it names on its target and deals a point more for each stack consumed, before the multiplies. The die held over the foe shows the number with the stacks in.100—
Burns its firerA rule printed on a weapon: spending it sets its own machine Burning, the stacks it says, after the blow. The machine's Burning heats it at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, then loses a stack, so a vent makes room for it and a cleanse takes it off. Fuel for a machine that wants heat, a price for one that does not.100—
The hopperTwo weapons that work together. One fills its machine's hopper, a load for each foe its blows reach, after the blow. The other fires the hopper: each load it takes adds its bonus to the blow, up to its most a shot, and the loads it takes are spent. The hopper lasts the skirmish and shows on the machine; the die shows what a shot would fire.020—
BracesA rule printed on a component (the Breaching Plow's). Spending it Armours its owner, the stacks it says (an armour point each), until the round's end: the armour stands behind the blow through the enemy's turn.020—
Rolls againA rule printed on a component (the Nemesis's Knuckle of Fury). Spending it with a stack of the status it names spends one, and the die rolls again, a plain roll as the round opens, so the machine acts again and the same component may show again. Unlike Rapid nothing switches off: the stacks are the limit.010—
ResetA rule printed on a weapon: if its spend kills, every entry dark from a cooldown comes back, itself included, and its machine acts again, the free roll never landing on the entry just spent; a team reroll may bring it back up, and every kill resets it again (the Kite's Pinion Driver).010—
PullA rule printed on a component (the Lammergeier's Lorentz Contractor, a debuff): every mine on the field gathers into the slot it is spent on, in laid order, and a foe standing there takes them now. Spent on an empty slot, it gathers them there.010—
DroneA rule printed on a component (the Lammergeier's Syrinx Drone, a debuff): the foe carries the Syrinx Drone. If it already carries Exposed, the spend is Rapid; otherwise it gives the foe Exposed, never refreshing it.010—
SprayA rule printed on a weapon (the Skua's Flechette Pod): its first strike lands on the foe it is spent on, and each strike after it falls on a random foe in reach at that moment, the same foe again allowed. A kill clears its slot at once, so a hole burnt in the front rank opens the foe behind it to the strikes still to come.010—
RegeneratesA rule printed on a component (the Storm Petrel's St. Elmo's Regenerator, a lure): spent on a foe, its own machine gains Regenerating, the shield it says at the start of each of the rounds it says after this one, a grant of its own each time.010—
An ally's actionA rule printed on a buff (the Giant Petrel's Immelmann Booster): spent on an ally whose die is spent, the team pays the rerolls it says, banked or fresh, and the ally's die rolls again, never onto the entry it showed, and it acts again. Spent on its own machine it grants what it says.010—
Rolls its dieA rule printed on a buff (the Giant Petrel's Reheat Uplink): the die of the machine it lands on rolls again, never onto the entry showing. A die already spent rolls nothing.010—
Rerolls a killA rule printed on a weapon (the Giant Petrel's Spindrift Salvo): if its spend kills, the team gains the rerolls it says this round, once however many fell.010—
CooldownA rule printed on a component: once spent, its entry goes dark for that many rounds, this one included, and the die shows it dark with the turns left; the die can still land on it, and nothing in it acts. Rapid carries a cooldown of one unless the component prints its own, and "no cooldown" lifts even that. No cleanse lifts a cooldown.020—
On foe moves only
Breach (a foe's)The blow ignores the machine's shield and armour: the shield stands and the whole blow reaches the hull; a Ward turns it away.———25 (the Antlion Queen, the Balloon, the Barbed Needler, the Behemoth, the Black Mage, the Bomb, the Death Knight, the Dread Knight, the Great Needler, the Grenade, the Lancer, the Magma Elemental, the Needler, the Old Man of the Desert, the Piercing Eye, the Pilgrim, the Pit Antlion, the Reaper, the Sandworm, the Warrior, the Wyvern, the Zombie Knight)
ScorchFoe attack keyword with a number. Each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Scorched on the machine it hit (Scorch 2: two): every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe runs hotter.———19 (the Balloon, the Behemoth, the Black Mage, the Bomb, the Cinder Tortoise, the Drake, the Fire Lizard, the Grenade, the Magma Elemental, the Magma Lizard, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Red Dragon, the Rotbloom, the Salamander, the Summoner, the Wyvern); 4 of them a move of its own
Burn (a foe's)Keyword with a number on a foe's attack: each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Burning on the machine it hits (Burning, above).———3 (the Drake, the Rotbloom, the Wyvern); 2 of them a move of its own
DisableEnemy keyword. The component the target's die is showing is switched off through the target's next turn: if the die lands on it, it comes up blank.———7 (the Alraune, the Basilisk, the Cockatrice, the Gorgon, the Leshy, the Prising Eye, the Rotbloom); 5 of them a move of its own
CorruptFoe attack keyword with a number. Each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Corrupted on the component the target's die is showing (Corrupt 2: two): it deals damage to its owner whenever spent, a bite a stack.———7 (the Barbed Needler, the Gorgon, the Reaper, the Rotbloom, the Summoner, the Thief, the Wraith); 1 of them a move of its own
StealEnemy keyword. The component the target's die is showing is switched off through the target's next turn, as a Disable, and the foe takes it: its next swing lands that weapon's number, a rolled one at its middle, and anything that was no weapon is a fake, for 1. A Ward turns it away.———1 (the Gilgamesh)
DrainEnemy keyword. The foe regrows what the blow took from the hull, no more than the machine had, so a shield starves it. It lays nothing on the machine, so no Ward turns it away; a Barrier's blow deals nothing to drain, and Burning on the foe takes back what it heals.———2 (the Vampire Lord)
SilenceFoe move of its own, never a rider on a blow. The machine it lands on is Silenced: its components' keywords are muted through its next round, printed and granted alike; its buffs count and its stores fill as ever. A Ward turns it away.———0
Purge (a foe's)A foe's move of its own, never a rider on a blow and never a last act's: the machine it lands on loses every buff a die face bought, every stack, for good, and what a store, a form or a passive grants until the start of the player's turn; its stores stay; a Ward turns it away.———0
SwallowFoe move of its own, never a rider on a blow. The machine it lands on is Swallowed: out of the fight for the rounds it says, or until the swallower falls. It never takes the last machine able to act. A Ward turns it away; a Lure turns it, and a Suppress or a Disrupt stops it, as any move.———1 (the Floraison); 1 of them a move of its own
MergeFoe move of its own. Every other foe on the field of the kinds it names is taken into it, gone without falling: it regrows the HP they had left, and its next move hits harder for each one. Thin them first; a Suppress or a Disrupt stops it, as any move.———1 (the King Gel); 1 of them a move of its own
Self-DestructA blow that spends the striker: after it lands the foe is gone, whole, and nothing brings it back. The blow is the foe's whole life, so it is announced like any other and the answer is to kill it first or to shield what it has picked.———3 (the Balloon, the Bomb, the Grenade)
Every MachineA blow at every machine: each of its strikes lands on every machine in the fight in turn, so no aim and no lure moves it, and the number shown is what each machine takes. A shield saves only the machine it stands on; the answers that stop the whole blow are aimed at the striker: kill it, suppress it, disrupt it.———14 (the Augur, the Balloon, the Bone Wyvern, the Cinder Tortoise, the Elder Treant, the Fallen Weapon, the Grenade, the King Gel, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Old Man of the Desert, the Phantom Train, the Rotbloom, the Vampire Lord, the Warrior)
CountdownA move that gathers before it lands: announced as the round opens, it lands the stated number of rounds later, its warning chip counting down the whole time. Nothing hits while it gathers. Suppress holds it a round, Disrupt breaks it off, and killing the foe ends it.———13 (+1 planned) (the Antlion Queen, the Augur, the Balloon, the Behemoth, the Fallen Weapon, the Great Needler, the Lancer, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Phantom Train, the Pilgrim, the Reaper, the Red Dragon)
Follow-upA move that names the move its foe announces next round. The first lands, and the second is the next round's intent, so the warning comes a round ahead: Red Capote, then Andalucia. A Suppress on the first, or a Disrupt that turns it, and the second never comes.———3 (the Cinder Tortoise, the Gilgamesh, the Matador)
GrudgeA blow that grows with the fight: a point heavier for every foe the team has killed this fight, its intent showing the number as it stands. Kill order answers it: the grudge-bearer first, the rest after.———1 (the Pilgrim)
Last ActA blow a foe lands the moment it falls, shown on it by the skull and its number. It lands at once, before anything else, even when the foe was the last one standing, so shield up before the kill rather than after.———2 (the Behemoth, the Dread Knight)
PartsA foe in parts: a core and the parts that stand with it. While its parts stand (all of them, or any, by the foe) the core is Guarded and takes no damage; a fallen part may grow back after a few rounds; when the core falls, its parts fall with it. Choose what to focus: a part to open the core, or the core to end them all.———2 (the Fallen Weapon, the Obsidian Tyrant)

The screen

Decided

Minimal chrome.

Flat vector overlays in a HUD colour the player picks from three presets (green, amber, blue), never a meaning's colour: not the enemy's violet, heat's red-orange or harm's red. No unit names, rank labels, passives or help text on screen; enemy health shows. The reinforcements waiting show by rank (Ryan, 15 October: wanted; built 22 October, a first cut to judge on the phone): in the formation's right gutter a plaque at each rank's height, an arrow pointing in over the count, and between the ranks a tall one for the massive foes that take a whole column; a rank with none waiting dims at nought while any foe waits, so a mine is laid against a count; the header's REINF stays the total. The plaques stand fifty wide over the last column's margin, their counts large, and a tap pulls out the waiting foes' list beside them, each kind by what it steps into with its picture and its count, the plaques lit while it is out (Ryan, 2 November: “reinforcement icons need to be bigger and more obvious. Ideally we can tap them to pull them out and see the queue”); the pool is drawn at random, so the list is what waits, not an order.

Decided

One chip block under each unit, wrapping into rows.

The chips run in rows as wide as the unit's place (a foe's column, a machine's spot), kept on the screen, with room for three rows under the machines clear of the controls in either layout (Ryan, 2 November: “chips can go off the sides of the screen if there are too many ... Need to wrap rows, which means there needs to be space for 2-3 rows of chips under the player units specifically without bumping into the UI”; one row that ran off the edge retired). A foe's intent (action and number, not target: Slice and Dice's model, with its blow's riders inside the chip) and its statuses; a machine's statuses (every one, however long it lasts, so the Ammit's battle-long Overcharged reads there too, and in its codex with its count, kept for the battle: Ryan, 16 October; built that day) and the forecast's warnings (the shield-break warning went on 26 October, Ryan: “now that shields don't prevent all damage. It's clutter, the bar previews are more than enough”), recomputed after every action, its gauges pulsing on one clock where they will change, a foe's HP among them, and a foe the round kills wearing the machines' destroyed chip (the round preview's row, below). A buff on its way, a rally's Kindled lifting a foe's blow, wears its chip on a dashed edge, its opacity pulsing on the gauges' clock, until it lands (Ryan, 26 October: "a pulsing version of the incoming buff as a preview chip", "Similar in concept to the health and shield bars"; the row on rallies, section 5). A unit's open readout draws its arrows; the eye button draws them all.

Decided

The round preview shows everything the round will do but chance and the player's own choices.

Ryan, 26 October: "It should account for every single thing that will happen other than 1) random active effects, which I don't think exist and 2) player decisions such as spending dice faces. Die faces only apply to the preview when hovered over a valid target", and "showing healing on the enemy side as part of the preview is incredibly important for decision making": a foe's heal is set before the player acts, so it is no choice of the player's. So the rest of the round is played on a copy of the fight, every rule as it runs, and the gauges, the warnings and the foes' bars read it: a heal, a death before a foe acts and the last act it sets off, a web's burst, a rally, a self-destruct. Ryan, the same day, on the rolls the engine does make: "None of your random effects are relevant ... Those are all game state changes during the player turn that the preview should recalculate after", and on a pick drawn again when a foe's machine falls, "No such thing should exist. If a machine phases, the targeting recalculates immediately, showing the new game state. Same with everything." So a pick made again (its machine fell before it acts or is Phased, a Disrupted foe's machine gone) is the next machine in line, left to right, Slice and Dice's rule ("it just targets the next ally in line"; Ryan, the same day: "Yes let's build next in line"), which the player can tell without the preview, and the preview shows it with a second arrow, dashed, the foe's own staying solid on its pick, the intent unmoved (the targeting row, section 5; Ryan: a dashed arrow in its place "masks the true intent", "We either need to show both arrows, or do like Slice and Dice and just preview the damage, not the target cascade"). Retired the same day: the pick drawn again from a stream of the fight's own, which the copy shared so the preview knew the draw; randomness the player could see only through the preview, against section 2's rule that randomness is something the player can see and shift. A stun's roll against Numbed, an injury's entry and a strike at random among several are the die's spend, settled as it lands and read in the next preview, so the copy leaves them out, counts a rolled blow at its lowest roll, and the reinforcements are the next round's. Every status the round will land wears a pulsing preview chip, on a machine or a foe ("All incoming chips should appear as pulsing opacity versions"), beside the skull when the round kills it ("It should be in addition ... some of those chips might be the cause, like adding burn to your own units"), and every warning chip pulses the same way, the skull first ("the skull is just another predicted chip, like any other. It just symbolizes death"); the recorder in Effects notes every grant the copy makes. A web's burst against a Numbed neighbour stays the one roll of the enemy's turn, its stun shown only when sure ("it can't show an incoming stun unless guaranteed"). A foe the round kills keeps its intent ("the intent line is still relevant for effects and watchers. Especially if the intent is tied to its death") and wears the machines' destroyed chip, so a Burn hit enough to kill shows the fall at once. Retired: the forecast summed by hand (the blows aimed at each machine, then a foe's ticks), which missed whatever it was not taught, a heal first among them; the bot still reads it, by measurement (27 October, docs/measurements.md: the greedy reads some 390 forecasts a round, and the copy costs twelve times a hand sum, so a round on the copy would run seven times as long and the matrix a day), Ryan having left the call to Claude ("Act on the bot however you think"). Built 26 October. What reopens it: the copy's cost on the phone (about 2 ms a refresh on the desk machine); for the bot, a cell whose band the greedy misreads for a heal or a last act, when a memoised copy, one play a decision point, is the first thing to try.

Decided

Numbed's chip has bracket corners, the mark of what nothing removes; a status chip has a box.

A Paradox chip shows its count.

Decided

Tap, hold, drag.

Tap a die for the entry showing, double-tap or hold it to lock or unlock it, drag it to spend: a tap reads a die as it reads a unit (Ryan, 30 October: “can we just change lock/unlock to hold and double-tap, and tap to inspect for dice? That way it's standard across the board”), its readout a toggle, one open at a time with the units'. A die's tap lands once the double-tap window has passed with no second tap, the die lit from the touch meanwhile, so a double-tap only locks and nothing flashes (Ryan, 30 October: “Is there a reasonable double tap that wouldn't flicker the popup?”, then “Yeah, agreed to all” to the beat it costs a read, since a lock comes every round and a read grows rarer as the player learns the kit), a fifth of a second, under Android's own 0.3, which read as lag on a single tap (Ryan, 2 November: “double-tap buffer on die is too long and feels laggy on single tap. Lower to 0.2 and I'll try again”); a unit's tap lands at once, its double-tap opening the codex over any flash. The readout lists that one entry, a mark on the die a line with its unique effects last, each line headed by the mark it says, the die's big glyph on what the piece is, a keyword's chip on its line, the star on each unique effect, whom it reaches in the words the die writes under its glyph (Ryan, 2 November: “any icon on a die face NEEDS to be represented in the popup, star icon before the unique effect for instance”), and the whole die is the codex's (Ryan, 25 October: “only show the visible component with slot number, name, and effects listed in a vertical list, with unique effects last”; no flavour for now). It turns the die's iconography into text and lists no source (Ryan, 26 October: “This popup doesn't show all effects on it, it just turns the iconography into text, basically”; listing a passive's change “reads to me like we're expecting the player to do math”): a number the live sources moved off the component's own is coloured instead, green where the change is good for its unit and red where it is bad, never judged for the player, its arrow on it for the way it moved, since hue alone does not read, and each number on its own (Ryan, 26 October: “Don't put the number in a chip, just change the font colour and add the arrow”; “Skua should have down on the damage and up on the count”). What a keyword means is the keywords list's, in the pause menu (Ryan: “We need a keywords list in the pause menu”), and a term in a readout opens its entry on a tap (the terms row, below), so the popup carries no explanation (“yeah this is wordy”). What reopens it: the popup or the list judged on the phone. Tap a unit for its state in the same format (Ryan, 26 October: “showing resource bars with numbers and modification up/downs if they exist, a list of all chips on the unit, and for enemies their current intent in the same format as the die hold press for a component”): its gauges with their numbers, its stats as the codex has them, every chip by its name and count with what it does a tap on its term away (Ryan, 2 November: “unit popups don't need the chips descriptions spelled out now that we can tap keywords”), and a foe's intent a line a mark under its field chip, the machine it is aimed at named, its colours the unit's. The tap toggles it and one unit's is open at a time (Ryan, 29 October: “Change the hold-press on player units and enemy units into a tap toggle, only one unit popup allowed at a time”): a tap on another unit moves it, a tap on nothing or the end of the turn closes it, it follows the fight while open, and it is translucent so the arrows it draws show under it (“Popup backgrounds need to be slightly translucent so that targeting lines can be seen underneath”; 2 November: “Increase the opacity of the popups in skirmish a bit”); it takes every tap that lands on it, a term's aside (Ryan, 2 November: “i managed to reroll when trying to click a keyword on a unit popup since it was underneath. Popups intuitively should prevent triggering things behind them, even other popup triggers”); a machine's rests on its gauges (“Tighten the bottom of the player unit popups to the top of the unit's bounding box”). Retired: a readout open only while the finger stayed down, a unit's (29 October) and then a die's (30 October: its terms could take no tap, and a die's tap locked where a unit's reads); a die's tap landing at once, which flashed the readout open on every double-tap's lock; and KEYWORDS, a button always on screen opening the list (26 and 29 October), a stop-gap once a term opened its own entry (Ryan, 30 October: “I asked for the keyword button as a stop-gap, if we have contextual help that can get repurposed or removed and just live in the menu”; “And yes remove keywords button”). Double-tap a unit for its codex, the base state between fights (the fight's effects live on the readout). A readout opens above its die or unit, clear of the thumb; a die's stays opaque over the log.

Decided

Every special, a boss's blow and a blow at every machine are announced on a plaque.

A JRPG message window in the foes' sixteen-bit register over their rows: the invasion at the interface layer, never HUD chrome. The replay holds for it (a second, a placeholder), once per move, so a storm of three strikes says it once. Plain blows and the player's components get none. A blow at every machine earns one (Claude's fill, 15 October, Ryan's to move): it reaches everyone at once, so it is named before it lands.

Decided

The HUD is a feed being analysed, not a print.

Rare, varied one-shot events (scans, telemetry, tears, signal drops, a die recomputing), never a rhythm and never competing with the pending-gauge pulse. Nothing else moves while the player thinks.

Decided

The skirmish's controls sit where the player sets them.

Ryan, 29 October: “Leave the layout as an option,” the setting Skirmish Controls. Middle puts the log, dice and Reroll between the two sides; Bottom puts them under the machines, which then stand right under the foes in one scene. Middle by default until play says otherwise; in both each die stays in its machine's column and Reroll far from End turn. A quick switch beside the intent eye moves them mid-fight (Ryan, 29 October: “a quick-switch that changes the control layout position”), saved as the setting is; the eye and the switch sit side by side inside the log's glass.

Decided

What the player sees of content.

The codex, the flavour quote, a passive's name and description, a module's description, a foe action's text. Internal text lives under design; planned drafts never show; tags show in the codex only. content/vocabulary.json defines every term and generates docs/vocabulary.md.

Decided

A term in shown text reads in its colour with its icon, and a tap opens what it means.

Ryan, 30 October: “in codex and help text, we should be highlighting keywords in their colour when applicable, and even including the iconography. Pretty standard fare for game clarity”; “Tapping a keyword is exactly what we need”. Every term the keywords list defines (a reach, a keyword, a rule on a component, a status, and since the bosses' slate the keywords a foe's move carries, Every Machine, Countdown, Last Act and the rest, under a heading of their own) reads bold in its group's colour (the colour row, below) with its icon before it, wherever shown text names it by its name or a keyword's verb form: the holds, the readouts, the codex and the list itself; a name that holds a term's word (the Web Bomb, the Pocket Paradox) stays a name. A tap on a term opens its entry beside the word, over any screen, its own terms opening theirs in its place; a tap anywhere else closes it. The words take their colour first, his call to judge (“Try bold in the colour first, and I'll judge”), though the darker colours read lower as text than the plate's quietest grey (heat 3.7:1, the blue 3.4, the red-red 3.0 at their worst; docs/measurements.md); a debug build's KEYWORD INK sets the words in ink, the icons alone coloured, to compare. A die's readout stays open as a unit's does, so its terms take a tap too. What reopens it: his read on the phone.

Decided

Few machines against a horde is the fantasy.

The answer to every foe striking each round is design (area damage, lure, mitigation, foes that misplay), never a cap.

Open

Teaching and icons.

A gesture tutorial and an icon cheat sheet in the pause menu, the reroll rule first; a licensed icon set (game-icons.net, CC BY) for the glyphs; terrain in the skirmish shows as the field's dress (29 October).

Colour and legibility

Decided

Semantic colours are fixed; the HUD picker changes chrome only.

HUD green #60ffb0, amber #fff21a, cyan #5cd8ff: three presets, the green minty since 30 October so it parts from the amber for red-green vision, where #33ff55 and the amber were one yellow (Ryan: “Move to the minty, yes”; docs/measurements.md), and no free picker (Ryan, 30 October: “lets just stick with the presets then”), since a free pick can land the chrome on a meaning or go too dark to read on the map, and the menus drawn in it with it. A register a meaning, every state with a glyph too, so no two marks part by hue alone. The enemy's is the rift's violet, for everything a foe does (Ryan, 30 October: “keep purple for enemy activities”): its rings and the band where a machine could attack from, its warning zones and tags, its intents and arrows, its plaques, the boss's reveal and what a foe did to a machine, light #c8a4ff on a deep #2a0e4a edge, which reads on every ground and plate where the red it replaced fell short (docs/measurements.md); the packs' routes wore it first (29 September), carried by a striped two-violet field. Heat is a red-orange, #ff4000, for what heats or hits harder (Ryan, 30 October: “Lighter, less red oranges sometimes look green to me. In isolation that's fine, but when green can mean 'good,' that can be a problem. That's why I'd rather redden the heat colour”). Any other red is placed case by case (his words: “either a slightly more red-red, the violet, or the heat orange-red”): harm to a unit is a red-red, #ff0000 (a number moved against its unit, a kill or a wreck forecast, an entry that cannot pay, a stop with no room, a foe Exposed, the gate's integrity low), a dead thing its dim #7a1400, and a cost wears its resource's colour, Volatile's hull cost the hull's. The shield's blue (#2f63d8) is what protects or holds: a shield, armour, a ward and a barrier, and the control that holds a foe (a stun, a lure, a web, a disrupt, the Paradox), kept where it was: under red-green vision the violet loses its red and reads as a light blue, so a cooler, lighter shield would have landed on it, where today's stands apart by being darker (Claude's lean, agreed; the control joined it on Ryan's word, 30 October, no hue his eyes keep apart from the rest being free). Green means one thing, a number moved in its unit's favour, the red-red the other way (#5cff6a, in the holds and the codex, never without its arrow; Ryan, 26 October: “If that means using green, this is the place for it”; “Positive and negative are always relative to the unit, not the player”, so a foe hitting harder is green); no other green carries meaning, and no coloured text sits on a map tile. Each term the keywords list defines wears one colour on the die, its chip and in text, its group's (Ryan, 30 October: “standardize the keyword colours. Use whatever is appropriate for each keyword. We don't need a unique colour for each keyword, obviously. Just groups that kind of make sense. Neutral white or grey or whatever is also available, even though it's text colour. Stay away from HUD colour obviously”): heat's for fire, heat and what hits harder (Chain beside Shock), the blue for what protects or holds, the violet for what only a foe does, the red-red for harm (Exposed, a destroyed component), the hull's for a price in hull, and ink for a shape, a reach or a rule; never the HUD colour. A debug build's COLOURS page sets these by wheel or hex, so he tries them himself and his picks land as these values (Ryan, 30 October: “Might as well put in switches for everything, actually, and I'll tweak everything and give you my take”), and since the scheme settled it holds only what is in play (the same evening: “Make sure the colours tab is simplified to only what we need”): the presets, the enemy's four violets (the violet itself, the bright its marks breathe toward, the intent field's deep and the edge under its lines, the routes and the rift's bars and tears wearing the violet) and the meanings; white, the neutrals and the plaque's look are settled and out of it. The HUD colour on text is decoration: flair, titles and subtitles; gameplay text reads in ink or a semantic colour (Ryan, 26 October: “Really the only HUD coloured text should be decorative stuff, flair things, maybe titles and subtitles like we have it. Actual gameplay text should be in a sensible colour”). Retired: the enemy in red (pure #ff0000 from 29 October, #ff1e1e before), which stood 3.0:1 on a chip's black for a protan and 5.6 from heat's orange for a deutan; heat's orange #ff6000, light enough to read as green to him; and a term's colour chosen apart on the die, a machine's chip and a foe's (Suppress blue on the die and the chrome's on a foe, Exposed the chrome's on the die and the red-red on a foe), most keywords in the HUD colour. What reopens it: his read on the phone, or the rings and the routes failing to part there by their structure.

Decided

No two marks differ by hue alone.

Ryan is colourblind (the type unstated), so his colour words are suggestions. A red or an orange is red and green alone, never blue, brighter and darker included (Ryan, 29 October: “I think the reason that I'm struggling with the reds is that you're moving the blues as well when I ask for brighter/darker and so on. Reds and oranges, compose them using RG only, not B”): past pure red the green steps 30, 40, 50 and 60 are each distinct to him, and past about #ff8200 an orange reads as yellow or green; even a lighter, less red orange can read as green to him, which is good's colour (Ryan, 30 October: “In isolation that's fine, but when green can mean 'good,' that can be a problem”), so heat leans red. Each mark carries structure too: an edge, dashes, motion, a glyph, a weight. What fails is a colour over its ground, never two UI colours apart, since every mark carries an icon (Ryan, 30 October: “the issues I'm looking at with colour are not differentiating between HUD elements. It's certain colours, over the BACKGROUND that's the issue”), so a new colour is scored by protan, deutan and tritan simulation (Machado) against the grounds it is drawn on (the field's, the map's, a chip's plate) and picked by the worst case.

Decided

Lean hotter than seems right.

Every effect judged on the phone has come back hotter, thicker or louder, never quieter: start at the exaggerated end and come down.

Decided

Judge an effect running, never from a still.

Every effect ships a switch that fires it constantly (RIFTGATE_FEED_DEBUG=1 is the pattern), and "faster" or "slower" is confirmed before it changes.

7

Mechs and the builder

A loadout is a real identity: MechWarrior's build depth, simplified, with no weight classes and no gated kit. The words: a component is one piece of kit and exactly one entry on the die, a weapon, a shield, a vent or equipment (the row below); a hardpoint is a free slot; a chassis's fixed components are built in and use no hardpoint; a module sits inside the machine and is never spent.

Machines and the die

Decided

Chassis come in any shape and differ by plain numbers.

The silhouette is the model and the footfall, and it decides what a machine crosses (section 4's ground row): tracks, walkers and wheels wade water at two, a hovercraft crosses water and a flyer water and walls, so a team is picked for the map by its crossers as well as for the fight.

Decided

Four squad slots; a run starts with three machines and buys the fourth.

A real choice between splitting up and staying together.

Decided

The die is the components.

Fixed components plus hardpoints, one entry each. A small die is consistent; a big one carries more but shows each entry less often (a thin deck as a chassis stat). Fixed components are never removed or bought open, because restriction makes builds fun.

Decided

Hardpoints are untyped.

The die is already the slot limit (a shield entry costs a gun entry on the same roll), and a niche the player can leave is an invitation.

Pinned

What a hand holds: locked, or a hand that takes more.

Ryan, 12 October: a gauntlet on the Nemesis's hip cannon loses too much immersion; tabled, the fists back as the Nemesis's locked piece. The shape he meant, if it returns: a hand as a superset, never a restriction. A hardpoint on a hand takes anything a mount takes and also what only a hand can hold (a gauntlet, a sword, a handheld rifle, a shield on the arm); a mount takes no hand-held piece; nobody loses a build and a chassis with hands gains the fantasy. It pays only with enough chassis with hands: almost every Arsenal chassis, many of Aphelion's, few of Minerva's and Wainwright & Sutler's, none of Kestrel's. It also raised a word: most players read Melee as a sword (the tags row).

Decided

A chassis's design is its passive, its numbers and its fixed components.

The stock in its hardpoints is generic kit the player replaces, so a passive must work with generic kit, and no machine has one straight-line build.

Decided

Toughness is not slots.

A tank is tough through its numbers and passive, never through a locked component that is only a stat line (plating by another name); its locked component is what only it can do, and a locked shield that does something passes (the Puffin's Backwash Ionizer). If a machine proves both unkillable and hard-hitting, the fix is its hull, not its slots.

Decided

One passive, one idea.

Nameable in a phrase (an upside and its cost count as one), at most three rule lines: a double-edged niche definer (the Kite) or a plain multiplier fantasy.

Decided

A chassis's identity must open build space.

Its passive, numbers and locked components together, never a convenient hole filled or a nice-to-have. A passive that only feeds its own fixed components adds nothing: it might as well sit on the components. The interesting part is how the player gets there: the Nemesis banks rage on any hull damage, so overheating on purpose, or anything that hurts it, becomes a build. A map rule has its place as a passive, but on its own it is a weak identity. Ryan, 15 October, keeping two of the roster analysis's tests: a new chassis earns its place by a new read (a state nobody reads), a new product (a state nobody produces), a new price, or a change to what a kind of piece does, since niches can and should overlap across a larger roster and the test reads the mechanics (a mechanic is its pattern in its context, never one trait it shares, such as a random target: Ryan, 16 October, "if you say 'can't do this thing because that already kind of does it,' then you quickly run out of things"); and a passive aims to be fed by, and to feed, other makers' pieces, ideally two routes in and two payoffs, an aspiration and never a pass or fail.

Recommended

A passive multiplies or redirects what components already do.

A verb several components share belongs on them as a keyword (so overshield is one).

Decided

A passive may break a rule, as a one-off.

That is the point of a passive: the rule bends for one machine and holds for every other, so the player learns the rule once and the exception where it lives.

Decided

A component is a weapon, a shield, a vent or equipment.

A weapon deals damage; equipment deals none (Ryan, 26 October: “Equipment is the label for all 4 forms of buff and debuff faces”): a buff is spent on a machine and grants one of the machines' own buffs, a debuff is spent on a foe, its keywords its whole effect. A shield and a vent are kinds of their own, spent on machines, each saying whom it reaches. The words drop “damaging weapon” and its caveat “regardless of damage dealt”, which is implied (Ryan, 26 October: “Much more clean and intuitive”), so a trigger reads one of three ways: damaging a foe with a weapon, hitting a foe with a weapon (whatever the blow dealt), and hitting a foe with a weapon or equipment; and a rule over what a machine aims at foes reads every weapon and equipment aimed at a foe, so a buff, a shield and a vent stay out of it (Ryan, of the Kite: “every weapon and equipment that targets enemies. Pretty clear and lets it have a backup plan of buffing allies if it chooses”; the Cottonmouth's drum, the Lammergeier's mines, the Cassowary's shots, Scorched and Thrust Overboost the same). A piece may be two kinds on one machine: the Whip Scorpion's vents blast a foe, so they read Vent, Weapon there, and the blast is a weapon's hit, doing all a weapon's hit does, resolved by the code every weapon's hit runs through (Ryan: “Vent blast should obviously proc cryo, that's the point of tagging it with weapon. It shouldn't need exceptions in code, it should just work now”). The die's readout shows the kinds and tags a passive or an effect gives a piece, and the codex the piece as built (Ryan: “hybrid shouldn't go in the codex, since that shows unmodified components”; “If a passive or other effect alters labels or tags, the same rule applies”). Retired: a weapon as anything spent on a foe, a debuff included, and equipment as anything spent on a machine, which needed “damaging weapon” and its caveat to say which hit a rule meant.

Decided

A component is intact or destroyed, for the whole battle.

Ryan, 25 October: "Cracked is gone." The mild state went, and with it the mechanics audit's finding that a crack made a Volatile piece better (its hull price a keyword, stripped with the rest). An injury destroys the entry the die lands on, at a quarter of the maximum hull. The threshold is set by what it asks of the player, never by a loss rate or a win rate (Ryan, the same day: "It's not about win rate, it's about forcing player choices and balancing compositions"): half, set to keep the two states' losses, was retired within the day, since a machine could lose but one piece before it fell (docs/measurements.md). A wreck loses only what was destroyed, as any machine does: its price is being wrecked and out for a battle (Ryan: "That's pretty big"), and if it needs to bite more, the levers are the recovery time or a cost. The Honey Badger's "never destroyed" holds every injury off until its identity session moves it to a module (the open Badger row). What reopens it: a fight that wants a mild injury again.

Decided

The codex shows a machine between fights; its readout shows the fight.

The codex has base numbers, each with the change the loadout and the battle make in a bracket beside it, the change coloured with its arrow and the base in ink (Ryan, 26 October: “the base number, with a bracket containing semi-permanent changes, if any, also coloured with arrows, such as from modules or battle effects like from fracture rewards”; “just go Base (+/- change)”), never a combat effect. Under the numbers and the passive it lists the active effects, what else the machine carries between fights (“non-chip effects granted not as part of the passive, such as modules, auras, battle effects from fracture rewards”); then the die in one column, each component a row: its name, its type as its main icon shows it (“Face type is either Weapon, Shield, Vent, or Equipment. Equipment is the label for all 4 forms of buff and debuff faces”) and its tags, then three lines, its attributes, its keywords and its own rules, at its own numbers (“Remember codex shows base numbers on the components”), and a foe's moves the same way. What reopens it: the plate judged on the phone.

Decided

Content stays a draft until Ryan signs it final.

design.stage says so on components and modules; none is final yet.

Components

Decided

Every component is exactly one entry.

A passive with no entry is a module.

Decided

Volatile is a cost printed beside the heat, a shield cost stands beside it, and an entry a cost locks cannot be played.

Ryan, 26 October: Volatile goes "from a keyword with a chip to a cost, alongside heat. Simplifies things. Also opens the option to pay for things in Shield as well", which "can be coded in if you're already in there. It's pretty clear, if you don't have the shield, the face cannot be played, and overshield does cheat it out at the cost of using two actions for one effect." So a hull cost (Volatile 3) and a shield cost print on the entry in the heat's row, in the die's readout and the codex the same way, and no chip says them: the gauges' preview shows what they take. The hull cost may wreck its owner ("I would allow it"); the shield cost is taken first, overshield included, and an entry short of it is locked: the die shows the cost struck through, as a dry gun shows its ammo, it has nowhere to land, the drag finds no target, the bot passes it over and END TURN pulses. "This will be the first case we have for a face that needs a condition to be used, so that locking catch and UI will need to be built. I think that's a good thing as it will encourage the design space if the support is there for conditional faces, as advanced pieces": one funnel in the engine (Skirmish.shield_short, read by every target list) and one mark on the die, for the conditions to come. Retired: Volatile the numbered keyword with a warning chip (28 September, numbered 22 October). No piece carries a shield cost yet; its pieces are content's to propose. Built 26 October.

Decided

Rapid and volatile may ride on any component, and Rapid always carries a limiter.

So a shield can cost hull, and a vent can give the turn back. Ryan, 15 October: every Rapid piece carries a limiter, a cooldown, heat or Volatile, so no chain is free; most pieces meet it with a cooldown of one, printed on each (Ryan, 2 November: “Rapid does not have a native Cooldown, it needs to be displayed always”: the switch-off that Rapid itself once carried retired, each cooldown a cost the die shows by its clock), and a piece without a cooldown pays heat or hull every spend. The validator holds content to it: a built Rapid entry that prints no cooldown carries heat or Volatile. The Cassowary's Spin-Up (Ryan, 19 October) carries none of the three: its limiter is the cylinder and its odds, since its spin is a true roll that lands on itself one time in six, to be spent again (Ryan: "I think we can allow the high-roll for the revolver spin, landing on itself, as an exception to the global rule"), a fifth of a spin more a spend on average, and every other way back to it in a round runs through the other chambers' switch-offs or the team's rerolls (a combo piece, not a typical Rapid one); the exit is that a stepping-die piece that ever chains for sure gets a limiter of its own. Heat limits a chain only while nothing in it cools, so a Rapid vent of any kind is a free way out of the heat system, and Ryan is very wary of one. Ryan, 20 October: the Wake Diffuser, the one Rapid vent, lost its Rapid and became a vent that also shields, the action-economy row's cantrip by density; a Rapid vent, if one is allowed to exist at all, has no place in the early tiers, never below tier three, and a Rapid shield is never below tier two ("not nearly as toxic as rapid vent"). Volatile carries its bite as a number (Volatile 3 bites 3 hull), so each piece's price is its own, a numbered keyword as Burn is; the form it replaced, one constant bite on every entry, priced every piece alike.

Decided

Support and status entries everywhere, Aphelion's most.

Stock is picked by design and balance, never to give a component a home.

Decided

A locked component is never a blueprint.

Being powerful and unique is why it is locked: no hardpoint stocks it and it has no tier. Its power still reads at its chassis's depth (Ryan, 21 October: components grow in power as tiers go up, so a locked piece on a tier-three chassis is tier two or tier three in power, and the Giant Petrel's Reheat Uplink may outdo the Overcharge Coil, a tier-one piece).

Decided

A shared basic kit.

One plain component a job, one maker each, stocked across makers, while everything else a maker makes carries something of its own (a keyword, a reach, strikes, an odd number): plain entries differing only by number and name are no decision. Tier zero (Ryan, 12 October): what comes off the assembly line, every basic's blueprint owned from a run's start. A tier-one chassis stocks basics beside its locked piece where it has one, since the rule underneath is the budget's: a tier one shows its identity with basics alone. The Arsenal makes none. The eight: the weapons Direct and Indirect, rolled and flat (the Twin Autoguns and the Ankle Mortar rolled, the Rivet Gun and the Laser Carbine flat and both Direct, the Carbine warm, so the kit holds no flat Indirect basic yet); the shields and vents split self only, the bigger number (the Canopy, Minerva's shield; the Bleed Valve, Kestrel's vent), from self or ally, the smaller (Aphelion's Halo Shield, Wainwright & Sutler's Coolant Jacket), and none for allies only. The Radiator Sail is Aphelion's ally vent at tier one. A basic never copies a real thing's name (Trench, never Trench Gun).

Decided

One stock pass brought the kit in.

Landed 22 October (its brief: git show a550c2e:docs/briefs/stock_pass.md); the content holds its table and its numbers. What it settled beyond the table: the old close weapons came down to Direct's band; the Rivet Gun became a basic, on the Armadillo; the Zenith Emitter folded into the Laser Carbine, its idea free to return as a tier-three Aphelion piece (the briefs README's bench); the Coolant Dump and the Blowout Stack retired; the Stingray and the Baboon stand at tier one on basics, though both read as tier two until Aphelion has more chassis, and the Baboon may stock a shield again. Kestrel's holes were Claude's to fill (Ryan, 21 October: "stock changes as you see fit"): the Cyclic Cannon, a Direct multi-strike gun; the Transonic Rifle, the flat step between the Laser Carbine and the Railgun; Trim Tabs, a tier-two module, one free reroll of its own die a round paid in a stat; the Overclocked Railgun kept, and the Buzzard stocking the hot steps.

Decided

The Laminar Deflector takes Rapid, and the Entropy Condenser Volatile.

Ryan, 12 October: the two plain pieces that were not basics took identities and left the basic pool's way. The Deflector is a shield with Rapid, Kestrel's tempo: shield, roll again, act; the Condenser a vent with Volatile, the Arsenal's way: its biggest number paid in hull. Ryan, 20 October: a Rapid shield is never below tier two, so the Deflector is a tier-two blueprint that no chassis stocks; its Rapid stays.

Open

The basic kit's names.

The two renamed basics keep working names, the Canopy and the Bleed Valve, until Ryan's name pass, and the Airburst Shell, the Flak Cannon and the Decoherence Veil with them; the Blast Door's old name went to the Barrier piece.

Decided

The Faraday Hull left the Armadillo, its shock kept and its discharge gone.

The Hull read too strong against rank and file, and the discharge was the larger share (measured on the lone Armadillo; docs/measurements.md), so the discharge went first. Ryan, 12 October: retribution is the Arsenal's thing, and the Armadillo is armoured, not a porcupine. Ryan, 14 October: the Hull moves to "a new unit that still wants to get hit, but actually has to worry slightly about its own hull", a lighter Minerva tier two, and the Armadillo becomes the form changer (landed 20 October, the Armadillo row). Nobody carries the Hull: its idea is the echidna's if Ryan takes it, a session left open for now (Ryan, 20 October; docs/briefs/minerva_hedgehog.md), and the Retaliate buff keeps the engine's shock.

Tags

Decided

Tags carry no rule of their own; modules and passives read them.

Shown in the codex as the piece is built and in the die's readout as its machine shows it, a passive's change in (Ryan, 26 October: “If a passive or other effect alters labels or tags, the same rule applies”). The set: Ballistic, Missile, Beam, Plasma, Flame, Arc, Blade, Melee, worn by each piece's fantasy (assigned 22 October, the stock pass's table): every weapon and debuff wears its tags and a shield, a vent and a buff none, and the validator holds every component to the set; five locked pieces the table did not reach (the Pinion Driver, the Lorentz Contractor, the Syrinx Drone, the Fovea Designator, the St. Elmo's Regenerator) wear none yet. Melee means short range: a flamethrower may wear it, and it reads as "this doesn't shoot far", not "a sword". Ryan, 12 October: most players read Melee as a sword, not a shotgun, but the word stays: the words of range (Close-quarters, Point-blank) are too long, and no hand-held subclass is needed. Narrowed to contact it would hold locked pieces and hardly a blueprint, so a module reading it would only feed fixed components: of the seven pieces that wear it, five are locked and two unstocked. Only the screen's lunge reads it now.

Decided

Tags are read through soft synergies.

One tag makes a state another tag reads: "beam weapons deal extra to burning foes" beside "missile weapons add a stack of burn" pulls toward mixing, on one die or across the team. A module or passive may name two tags, a cheap way to widen it. Components never read tags. No threshold or count that makes one blueprint the build ("with three melee weapons"; a bonus only one tag's pieces feed): those are lynchpins that dictate a build, the player farming one blueprint to make it work. A count every piece of the tag feeds, where nothing a tag grants feeds that tag (the gunship: each weapon fitted raises a number by its tag, Ryan's, section 7), is no lynchpin and passes.

Decided

A tag earns its place once two things read it.

Decided

A chassis passive reads a tag only if it opens build space.

The Nemesis's melee bonus was the model (any melee, paid for by any hull damage) until its rage replaced it on 12 October; no chassis passive reads a tag today.

Modules

Decided

Modules carry stats and stay simple.

Stat lines up or down plus at most one rule: a small number and a stat line, never a wall of text. No parity with each other, and no universal must-pick (every chassis fitting the same module flattens the game); pick share in the runner guards it. Four archetypes guide, unenforced: high stats and no text, low stats and good text, massive stats and bad text, negative stats and amazing text.

Decided

Module slots are a chassis number, never bought.

Two; the Clydesdale three.

Decided

Platings are modules.

A hardpoint carries something mounted, and armour slapped on does not track.

Decided

The Muzzle Velocity Booster and the Tactical Databank are mirror images.

One raises its rolled weapons' highest roll, the other their lowest, with no stats. Neither touches a flat weapon, so on a machine of rolled weapons they measure the same within the noise and on a flat one nothing (docs/measurements.md): the mirror holds and neither needs a stat line. They part by matchup, the Booster where a spike crosses a kill, the Databank where steady damage beats armour and long fights, so the choice reads the front and the kit, which is the asymmetry wanted. A flat increase to damage was rejected: good everywhere, so never a choice.

Open

The catalogue again as soft-synergy pairs, on the tags assigned 22 October.

Why it reopened: without a rule the Ram Frame measures the best module on every machine (section 10's prices), the universal must-pick the first row on modules forbids, and the two "bad text" modules measure as losses on nearly every machine, so their stats are not paying for their text. Decided 22 October (Ryan, on Claude's second proposal) and built: the Ram Frame and the Artillery Rangefinder are mirrors, as the Booster and the Databank are, each a reach read, the Frame +1 on Direct blows aimed at the front rank, the Rangefinder +1 on Indirect blows aimed at the back; the Frame's armour line is gone, and both pieces' stat lines come from the pricing run. The Melee read proposed first fed only locked pieces, as the tags row says. The rest of the catalogue is the open part.

Makers and names

Decided

Five makers at most, each an identity through what it builds.

Never a battlefield niche; a voice and a history, never marketing. Ryan places machines and components under them. The table says what each maker tends to build, a guideline and never a rule (Ryan, 12 October): a machine may cross it while it still reads as its maker's. The name families hold. A profile is an identity, never an exclusion (Ryan, 13 October): a keyword or a mechanic fits wherever it fits the piece's fantasy, so a Wainwright & Sutler spider paralyses because spiders do (Aphelion would hardly build one), and statuses are Aphelion's strength, never its alone. Such leanings are marked Lean in this document, and a Lean is never a reason to refuse a piece.

MakerBuildsVoiceNames
Minerva Heavy WorksTracks (nearly all of them), heavy wheels, quads, perhaps heavy bipeds and one flying brickA maintenance manualRugged mammals
Kestrel DynamicsSmall and mid flyers and hovercraft on big boosters, all the fast jets, nothing bigA flight-test logBirds, raptors first
Wainwright & SutlerWheels, quads and a heavy on six or eight legs (Ryan, 28 October), perhaps a helicopter, no bipeds but the Cassowary's chicken walk; old-West iron, simple and straightforward: indirect fire and areaField notesThe frontier's working animals and pests
AphelionThe deep-space programme come home: hovercraft and bipeds, aquatic; it flies on thrusters and space-age rockets rather than rotors (the Stingray's flush fans are its own silhouette)Haughty jargonPrimates, insects, sea creatures
The ArsenalThe Council's house: creepy walkers first, borrowing other makers' shapes; hot plasma, big single hits, burn, self-damaging kit, retributionLiturgyFigures who punish, from any culture: justice, vengeance and retribution, Hell's wardens and avenging spirits among them; never war
Decided

A nickname is a real Earth animal a player can picture.

Living or extinct (a fossil when it dodges the trope: Dunkleosteus over Megalodon). The Arsenal names its machines for figures who punish, never animals, so a name tells the player which side of the makers built it: a mythic name on a city maker's machine would blur that read, which is the reason and the whole of it (the invasion brings the genre's high fantasy, and the occult is the Council's). No vehicle has an open cockpit.

Decided

Each chassis has a dull maker's designation.

It names the job, never a stat, in its maker's shape (EX-45XLE Combat Miner, K-7 Mk III Interceptor, Pattern 1 Combat Walker, Perihelion Flight 7 Support Frame, Arsenal Instrument IV), shown small above the nickname. Each maker's word for the thing is its own ("Frame" is Aphelion's, "Instrument" the Arsenal's): a lean of the designations.

Recommended

Claude's designations are proposals for Ryan to move.

Every designation Claude filled, the Kite's and the Clydesdale's among them, stands until he moves it (Ryan, 21 October: "Designations as you see fit"); content holds them.

Decided

Names are ours, or a knowing homage.

No chassis takes a machine name from BattleTech, Heavy Gear, Warhammer 40,000 or Halo. Iconic names are never used, known ones are judged case by case, coincidences are kept. Ryan, 21 October: the two passes the roster carried close. The Sparrowhawk (a BattleTech aircraft, a coincidence) is the Kite, the light raptor that twists on its forked tail, and the Goshawk (an obscure nickname's) the Buzzard, the broad-winged one that soars and stays; the ids stay, since nothing the player sees reads one. Out with them: the Shrike, the Merlin, the Gyrfalcon and the Peregrine (BattleTech's), the Albatross (BattleTech's and Halo's) and the Condor (Halo's); a name too specific to picture (the Hobby, the Saker) is out too. The Butcherbird, the strafer's name at its go, turned out a BattleTech 'Mech's that evening, so the strafer is the Skua (Ryan, 21 October); a plain Owl is Heavy Gear's, so Kestrel's owl is the Snowy Owl.

Decided

Component names follow each maker's vocabulary as a guideline.

Minerva blunt, Kestrel air and speed, Wainwright & Sutler plain military beside something silly, Aphelion physics nonsense, the Arsenal medieval and occult, sanctified on the face and sinister on reflection; general words (cannon, laser) belong to no maker. A name is a piece of kit, never an ability; its pseudo-tech may contradict itself; it approaches a real thing without copying it; the codex never explains the joke.

Decided

A maker leans toward keywords; it owns none.

Ryan, 13 October: a maker's keywords are identities, never exclusions, and a table bucketing keywords by maker as rules is a poor guide to design. A maker leans toward the keywords its pieces most often carry, and any piece carries the keyword its fantasy calls for, whoever makes it. The leanings: Minerva toward Spread, Lure and Armoured (heavy industry holding a line); Kestrel heat as a resource, rerolls, strikes and risk, Overshield and Exposed, with Rapid its gated cantrip (Ryan, 20 October: "the maker didn't really have an identity before other than the look, and now we are indeed looking at heat, rerolls, multi-strikes, and risk"; 28 October: "Kestrel holds Exposed"); Wainwright & Sutler Flak (its signature status, Ryan, 19 October; 28 October: "W&S holds most of the Flak"), Penetrate and strikes, Suppress on its Battery (indirect fire, volume, the on-hit); Aphelion Disrupt, Shock, Chain, Cryo, Cleanse and Primed, and the Paradox (the status house; Ryan, 28 October: "Aphelion holds a lot of the other status effects - shock, chain, cryo", so most Shock and Chain setters land in its content); Minerva most of the armour buffs (Ryan, 28 October); the Arsenal Volatile, Burn ("Arsenal gets a lot of the Burn"), Overcharged and Retaliate, and the talons' kill rule and rage (pain, fire, retribution). Ryan, 28 October: "Crossover can and should happen, to increase cross-maker diversity when building. Leans, not rules, not rails." The merges across the two sides still open are section 12's questions.

Decided

A machine wears its maker's livery.

A repaint is a palette swap; extra colours are free Sponsorship unlocks (section 10).

Decided

A prototype hangar comes before the economy.

Repaints and loadouts tried on every machine, the loadout carried into battle for play tests. The harness fits loadouts and modules today, and the quick campaign's hangar a run's (section 3's quick campaign row); a repaint is a debug switch (RIFTGATE_PAINT).

The chassis

Each chassis's maker, tier, size, numbers and kit are content's (content/chassis.json, the hangar's Codex tab); the rows below keep why each machine is what it is.

Decided

The cars trade strengths.

Their map rules are passives (the Prairie Dog is never hunted, the Dingo acts twice), and each car's weakness is the other's strength. A decoy every hunter picks, the Prairie Dog's first passive, stays out: it scraps the machine unless it may kite, and kiting is a dangerous road. Alone, a map rule is a detachment tool on a die that cannot detach (docs/measurements.md, 13 October), so each car fights by its locks: the Dingo's Megataser Harpoon Shocks, the pin the setup for an ally's blow (Ryan, 14 October: a taser shocks), and the Prairie Dog softens with Flak (its row below). The want of a fast machine that fights alone stays open.

Decided

The Buzzard splits sustain from burst; the Clydesdale's big die earns its reroll; the Wild Boar dives rather than tanks.

Decided

The Stingray is Aphelion's hot status amplifier.

Cooling 0 and a hot fixed gun keep its Exothermic Suite fed. Its strength at high heat and its double dip through Primed are settings to tune, and its burns feed the Baboon's meter on purpose.

Decided

The Puffin is Kestrel's shield escort, and its shields read the ally's heat.

Ryan's go, 21 October, on his worry that it was toxic and boring (a big overshield and a vent on the key ally every round, whatever that ally did). Its passive, the Slipstream Projector, grows every shield it spends on an ally by a share of the heat that ally carries, so it multiplies what shields do: Kestrel's heat as a resource, converted for others (the Buzzard converts its own). Its fixed Backwash Ionizer is the big one through it, allies only. The cooling it gave went as the toxic half, which also fought the heat the passive rewards. No cap: the ratio bounds it, the die limits shielding itself, and overshield fading at the round's end is the safeguard. The decision it makes: the hot ally or the threatened one, and the team's order, since an ally that fires its hot gun before the Puffin spends is shielded for it. A shield specialist is a niche; a shield-bot is not. Tier one since 21 October, to balance the line as the Kite moved to tier two. What reopens it: a larger share of the ally's heat if it plays as a trickle; the Peak's Burning and Scorched become shield through it, the domain that should fear an escort. Its rounds: git show b4dfbe5:docs/briefs/kestrel_puffin.md.

Decided

The Kite at tier two: a flying accident that kills to stay alive.

Ryan's direction, 18 and 19 October (the data read it as a tier two, and double damage and double heat "feels thin and abusable": a cool hard-hitting gun doubled was a free lunch), and his go, 21 October. Its passive doubles every weapon it fires (the printed number, buffs once; section 5's blow row keeps it so and notes its buff if it ever needs one), and every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe runs a flat heat hotter whatever its own and goes dark two rounds, a buff on an ally its way out (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), and each attack that killed makes its shots hotter for the fight; its locked Pinion Driver, its four in two shots at half, one from each wing tip (Ryan, 29 October: "the same total damage as before, same everything. Just in two shots. A minor change"; both go at the one foe, armour comes off each and Flak adds to each, so an armoured foe takes less, and a shot after the kill lands on nothing), on a kill brings everything back and the Kite acts again, and a team reroll back onto it chains it again with every kill, with no limit (Ryan, 24 October: "Let the Kite reset infinitely. There are other ways to nerf it that aren't more rules exceptions"): the kills, the team's rerolls and its heat are the brakes, since every shot pays the passive's flat heat and every kill a point more for the fight, and if the chain proves too strong the levers are its numbers, never a cap. The flat heat answers the cool-gun abuse, since every shot pays it; a d3 (the die minimum loosened for it) makes every dark entry felt, the team's rerolls its fix; a paper hull (the scales row's one-off) caps how much it fires. Its niche: the trash chain, and a steady doubled gun on the boss; the caster-killer is the Needletail's Exposed, on which the doubled shot loses nothing to armour. The old finisher left for the pool as the Coriolis Accelerator. No shield on a kill in the first cut: getting out is going dark while the Puffin and a Barrier cover it, the kill-shield the lever if it dies too often. Its runner rows are its team rows, since alone it loses what it used to win. The passive's four effects are its one-off over the cap of three. Its brief: git show 95d4ed3:docs/briefs/kestrel_kite.md.

Recommended

The Baboon is Ryan's brief.

Long arms, stubby legs, part orangutan and part troll, its suite on its back, control through statuses; numbers and kit filled in for him to move. Its passive is decided (the Pocket Paradox); its flavour text is his to write. Its move to tier two is in session (docs/briefs/aphelion_baboon.md).

Decided

The Nemesis: "Taking hull damage banks a rage for the duration of the skirmish."

Ryan, 12 October, in place of the Crucible's melee bonus a hull hit: all hull damage, a blow, a bite of its own, an overheat, since that opens the most build space and reads the simplest. Its fists spend the rage (below). Rage pays for actions only, so the die stops collapsing onto the fists: they took two thirds of its spends with the Crucible feeding them, half with rage.

Decided

The Nemesis's fists are the Knuckle of Fury; the gravity piece is shelved.

Ryan, 12 and 13 October: fists that spend rage to roll again are not gravity, so the Nemesis keeps the word knuckles, in the Knuckle of Fury (Ryan's name, a Bruce Lee reference), with no Burn ("it has no place"). The gravity piece, a tier-three blueprint with an area effect and Volatile, is shelved with lots of potential and comes back later; the Gravity Knuckles entry stays unstocked at tier three until then (as a blueprint it measured a sidegrade on other machines, and the Nemesis's best build only because the Crucible fed on its bite). Ryan, 12 October: no hard guard against a build that breaks the game. It is not a PvP game, enough stands in the way that a busted machine needs support, and a piece that is generically that good can be rare or dear to build.

Decided

The Nemesis's signature: rage that keeps its fists swinging.

Ryan, 12 October: its locked fists spend a rage when they strike, and the die rolls again instead of being spent: "it's not a choice, you just get rapid", with no cooldown, since the rage is the limit, which reads far more berserk. The roll is a plain roll as the round opens, not Rapid's reroll, so a fist may show again, the very fist included. Rage buys actions, never damage: a content call for this machine. A rage a hit, since rage per damage taken would barely differ and would cost the player a half rage to track. The levers if it should hit harder: the fists' number, or two rage a hit (Ryan, 13 October: the fists as they are until play, without Burn). An optimised build feeds it more in a group (the Tithe Coupling); a lure build waits on content and on a bot that values being struck. The roll again shows (Ryan's ask): the Rage chip bursts as its stack goes and the die flips back with the reroll's own flip and sound. Banked up to five (Ryan, 22 October).

Decided

The Honey Badger's hopper: its claw fills it, its fragmenter fires it.

Each foe the Chainsaw Claw's blows reach puts a load in the hopper, and the Bullion Fragmenter fires up to three loads a shot, each adding to the blow. Its spread turns a crowd into ammunition for the boss, where it was weakest, and the round holds decisions: the claw before the fragmenter, fire now or bank, reroll toward the pair. Two entries, so it is not a ramp and payoff in one; the cap a shot keeps a full hopper a supply for several shots rather than one burst. As built: its identity session reworks it (the open row below).

Decided

The Breaching Plow armours the Boar.

Ryan, 12 October: "Give the plow braced. It fits." (Braced is Armoured since the one-name convention of 16 October, section 6.) Spending it Armours the Boar until the round's end, in place of the Glacis, an armour line meant for front-rank blows only, which measured no different from plain armour and so was hidden base armour (docs/measurements.md). Charge and brace is a decision; plate that is always there is not.

Decided

Locked components may work together.

A chassis's fixed pieces that feed each other are design space of their own, kept in mind for every new chassis: what only this machine can do, in a sequence the player plays round by round. Several machines grow through a fight (the Buzzard, the Nemesis, the Ammit, the Stingray, the Badger), which is fine for now, a symptom of scaling being easier to design (Ryan, 12 October); what matters is that a machine's pair fits its identity.

Open

The Honey Badger's identity: a scrappy machine that keeps on coming.

Ryan, 12 October: scaling threats are fine for now, but scaling is not the Badger's identity. In session since 15 October (docs/briefs/minerva_honey_badger.md, his calls there in his words): the hopper is the identity, a load for each foe a spend damages, so it leans to area and detachment beyond "bruiser"; the loads it holds are armour, shown as a buff, up to a cap; the claw loses Spread and strikes twice, the chase spend against a boss; "never destroyed" becomes a module at tier two or three. Open: the claw's number, the swarm fill at tier one, the fragmenter's bonus a load and the module's line. The last stand and campaign insurance are on the brief's bench.

Decided

The funnel-web: an ambusher that binds and bites.

Ryan's go, 18 October (its brief: git show bfa8cf2:docs/briefs/ws_funnel_web.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier two.
