Its passive: its blows on a foe showing no intent on it lay Vitriol, a share of the foe's current HP a stack at its action, so it opens on a boss, never finishes a foe and never sets off a last act. Its locked Web Bomb is its stun, kept under the stun row (Ryan: it fits the kit): the webbed foe acts once, then the web bursts, Suppressing it and the next foe to act beside it; no cooldown (on one foe Numbed is in effect its cooldown, and a stun is priced by a gate, here its timing). Its two uses are the decision: web the foe left of the threat and the threat skips now; web the boss and its blow lands now, it is held next round and shows no intent then, a window. Per hit, because per spend the stacks never happened without Rapid, and strikes as bites give multi-strike pieces a home (the Maxigun its first chase piece); the payoff is the team's windows (an ally's Lure or Suppress mid-turn, a bare-hulled tank), with Vitriol the reward and the Paradox a bonus, never a reason (Ryan). Set aside: fangs, a missing-HP finisher, which closed the role on itself and repeated the Coriolis Accelerator's pattern. Its weapon hits lay the Vitriol, as its text says (Ryan, 25 October: "That's not a global passive rule, that should be a vitriol specific rule, and documented in the passive text"; "The important part is that it was a damaging weapon"), so the web feeds the ambush only through the window it opens. What reopens it: the ambush condition must bite (a soft limit on a boss, a hard one alone and in swarms; the runner reads the share of its blows that bite and the web's share in formation fights) and a window's Vitriol must be worth the spend that made it; it waits on elites in fights to matter outside the boss fight.

Decided

The Cassowary: the gunslinger whose die steps instead of rolling.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_revolver.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier one, the maker's damage dealer. Its passive (Ryan's wording, 12 October): "Whenever its die rolls, it moves to the next entry instead, in sequence." Each fight opens on a random chamber (Ryan, 18 October: "like a revolver spinning, the only random factor we're able to inject in an otherwise deterministic chassis"), so the plan is adjacency, not sequence, and a team reroll is its dial: its one source of randomness, seen and shifted. Every sixth weapon or equipment it aims at a foe counts double, so the player steers which chamber takes it. Its locked Spin-Up spins the whole cylinder, itself included (his exception to the reroll rule, a design one-off), and primes its next weapon; its limiter is the cylinder and its odds (Ryan: a combo piece, not a typical Rapid one): a spin lands on itself one time in six, to be spent again, the high roll he allowed, so a spend averages a fifth of a spin more. Squishy on purpose (Ryan, 19 October: "we're packing a lot of firepower in it"), so the maker's tender deal holds no front, accepted: the Clydesdale moved down a tier is the lever if play tests want one. Only a weapon spends Spun Up, equipment leaving it standing (Ryan, 26 October: “Spun up only interacts with damage, so change it to Weapon only for now until balance says otherwise”); retired: a debuff spending it for nothing, a bonus that is only damage taken by a piece the die grammar (section 6) lets deal none. Its passive's name (the Indexed Cylinder) and its designation are working names. What reopens it: a stepping-die piece that ever chains for sure gets a limiter of its own; if the pips or the next-chamber chip clutter a phone die, the counts retire to the die's readout. No model yet; the bot turns the dial and plans no cycle (the to-do).

Decided

The Cottonmouth: versatility of reach, every weapon through the drum.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_fan_boat.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier one, the fan-boat: the maker's versatility in a third form (Ryan, 19 October: the Clydesdale's is loadout, the gunship's stats, this one's reach), so it belongs at tier one, where it plugs holes in many draft compositions. Its passive, the Adaptive Munitions Drum (Ryan's name): every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe leaves the drum as one Indirect, flat shot with its own keywords (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), punching through the back rank or spraying the front, so a column or a row is the decision on every shot, with full information and no state to track. The drum is the passive's rule, not an entry on the die (Ryan: "the deck gun isn't actually a locked component"), so the machine locks nothing. The fixed number is what makes any shape balanceable, and the price (a big gun is wasted on it), with the light hull and the heat. It spreads statuses well, which steps on Aphelion's toes and is fine (Ryan: "Status spread isn't a monopoly, nor is it a complete identity"); beside the Baboon it is a Paradox engine. Set aside on the way: everything Direct through a deck gun (free reach once danger close went), the magazine, the echo, the sponge, the sum and the hopper (the brief's bench). Its hole to read: every status shell becomes a two-or-three-foe status, so the gate principle holds per piece. Its shape is the shot's whole (Ryan, 28 October, on a Spread piece fired through it): the drum's Spread 2 or Penetrate 2 replaces the piece's own number, never the higher of the two (“that's gaming the build”), “it forces the weapon into its own shape and then adds any keywords and effects it doesn't already have”. Ryan's picture for its model, not yet built: a big rotating set of barrels with the guns mounted invisibly inside, future-tech, a hover hull under a drum and the one machine whose hardpoints show no mounts.

Decided

The Dingo gains a Combat Translocator: two foes or spaces trade places.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_dingo.md): "Dingo winch is perfect. We could increase it's flexibility by allowing lateral movement as well, unique from the Minos. Target two foes/spaces and swap them? Useless against a boss, sure, not sure if that matters." Two foes, or a foe and an empty slot, trade places, no damage (the Swap keyword): distinct from Minos by pattern (Minos turns whole ranks over involuntarily; the Dingo chooses two). It exposes a caster to Direct fire, sets the acting order (the spider's web, a stun's timing) and bunches foes for every area piece, so a swap then a shell is a cross-maker combo, and soft control. A massive foe does not move, so bosses are out by rule; useless against a lone boss, which does not matter for a tier-two team piece whose harpoon still works there. A teleporter in the maker's register, not a winch (Ryan: a cable cannot swap two things): "guys this totally isn't just a teleporter", the Combat Translocator (his working name). Tier two with it, a second locked piece reading as a pure team piece. Its interaction, drag to the first target and tap the second (Ryan, 21 October), is the form any later multi-target piece shares; a tap on the ground sets it back, the die flying home, once no readout is open, since the drag back alone was hidden (Ryan, 30 October, agreeing). The bot reads a swap that bunches foes under an ally's area, not yet the acting order; its look is the settled looks row's. It makes the Dingo's die a d5 where it was a d4 (Ryan, 29 October: "I guess that's ok").

Decided

The Prairie Dog's fight half: its Flakshot lays Flak, the maker's on-hit.

Ryan's go, 19 October, on his own shape (its brief: git show f5a085a:docs/briefs/ws_prairie_dog.md): "Flak repeater - if it was a low damage, spread, maybe rapid, weapon with a new debuff: flak, target takes X damage per stack when attacked ... Could be part of a new Flak family of W&S weapons, their signature status." Flak makes every blow of a weapon the foe takes deal a point more a stack, a blow of 0 too (Ryan, 25 October: "Flak and overcharge exist in part to get past armour"), halving each round (Overcharged's clock, so a steady feed settles and a boss never ramps); on-hit only (Ryan: "on-hit only"), since a tick at the foe's action is Burning with another chip. A flat on-hit is the kind Ryan named missing on 14 October; Shocked stays the multiplier one blow spends, and the two pair. What it gives the maker: its many small kinetic blows get a status that makes often matter, with routes in from any spray or multi-hit piece and payoffs on every small blow. The decision it makes: lay before cash inside a round; spread it across a rank for the area pieces, or stack it on the one foe the Maxigun will chew. The pivot (Ryan, 20 and 21 October, under the action-economy row): the Flakshot Repeater lost Rapid, since the squad's setup-then-cash is the team's order and a gated Rapid bought the Dog's solo loop alone; Flak 1 (Ryan, 22 October; 2 the lever if 1 reads as nothing). The car's fight half went on its passive beside the map rule: the Prairie Dog takes less from any foe carrying Flak, whoever laid it (Ryan: on the passive and not on the piece, so any Flak piece covers it). So one status does both halves: in the squad the Dog softens the rank; caught alone it sprays and the swarm hurts it less, survival rather than kills. Two clauses on one passive is Ryan's call for this car. What reopens it: the halving clock settling too high on a boss (a cap), or the maker's small blows making Flak a must-pick (price, never scarcity); Flak's clock is section 12's.

Decided

The Whip Scorpion: the boiler whose vents are its guns.

Ryan's go, 20 October (its brief: git show 82ff916:docs/briefs/ws_boiler.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier two (Ryan, 19 October: so that not both of the maker's damage dealers sit at tier one, and it is the more complex), the maker's sustained damage dealer ("a sustained dps engine"): a future-tech machine that behaves like a steampunk device, its names in the maker's register ("Keep heliographic cause its dumber"). Its passive (Ryan, 12 and 13 October): its vents blast a random foe for the heat they take off it, a weapon's hit that does all a weapon's hit does, its Cryo cashed among them (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row); the heat is its own, so a vent it spends on an ally blasts nothing and an ally's vent on it blasts nothing (letting them blast is the first buff if the row reads weak); the random target is the rider's limit and the player times it (section 2's damage-rider row). Nothing else cools it, so every point of heat stays until a valve opens and the fuel is a decision, never a drip (a passive gain a round went on 20 October as a free blast a round; a small pilot light is the exit if the row reads cold). The Heliographic Siphon, Ryan's shape taken as a lure-all ("Riskier, more rewarding"): no damage, no Rapid (too easy), every foe in the fight turns on it for the round, it is Armoured for the round, and every blow landed on it adds heat, next round's fuel. The counter-profile is the point (Ryan, 20 October): the heat a blow is poor against a boss, which its vents already rip when it keeps its heat up, and good against a swarm; the reward is capped by the heat maximum and one vent a round, the risk the whole formation's blows and the overflow burning at the next turn's end. Claude's objection (no reward for being struck that scales with the enemy's blows) was withdrawn on Ryan's argument: that line struck free offence riding on a turn a tank takes anyway, and the Siphon spends the machine's own turn, deals nothing that turn and pays out a round later at random (the retaliation row carries the condition). The decision it makes: run hot on purpose, whistle at the swarm whose blows bounce off its armour, never at the brutes, and open the valves into the crowd; the Peak's Burning and Scorched are free fuel. What reopens it: the boss cells falling too easily (half the blast), the solo row reading cold (the pilot light), and the lure-all proving too swingy (the rank form, Spread, the bench's fallback). No model yet.

Decided

The Swallowtail: the helicarrier, a muster its pieces spend.

Ryan's go, 21 October (its brief: git show 5d989be:docs/briefs/ws_multirotor.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier three, an assault flyer on four rotors, the maker's first capstone: the helicarrier, "the apex of steampunk-silly military technology" (Ryan, 20 October): "It carries things, obviously, but it also operates as a mobile command, and fire support. It doesn't rain death, but it directs units to rain death, offers in-fight refueling and support, and gives them a lift when needed." Its passive, the Muster Deck, has two clauses. On the map, the machines beside it ride and land as a block (Ryan, 21 October: "They move as a block, if the block doesn't fit, it can't go there"; a landing animation or a redistribution on landing may come later), reach and never a reset, a strategic piece and no movement abuse. In the fight, the Muster fills a sliver every time any die rolls (Ryan: "it takes MANY rerolls to gain a charge", "ultimate tier", "you have to work for it"), a kill by its own guns worth several rolls, each fight from empty: any die's roll plays up the team player, and per fight so the player never farms charges. Retired on the way: the multi-rotor that lent its die (Ryan, 20 October: the more he thought, the more ways he found to abuse it, and it was a boring combo piece) and the equipment slots that replaced the lending (tedious to play and to balance); the hull as the price of its pieces, the command bar (Ryan: "The carrier is not the commander entering the battlefield"), the wreck recovery as a charge, the dock's lure.

Every locked piece has a plain form and a charged one that spends a muster, which goes out unasked whenever a charge is up and it has somewhere to land (Ryan, 22 October: "Don't prime the muster charge. It just gets used if it's up. If it's an option to activate it becomes abusable, player can hyperoptimize their turns without interacting with the die RNG"; "Not using it is the player's issue, and if its always a bad call I think we need to just change the effects"). The Mooring Screens shield every ally, the team shield, honest by its number, where Armoured on all hands four machines the dearest stat and a Barrier on every ally breaks the Barrier row; charged, Phased on every ally, only while none has acted this round (Ryan, 22 October: otherwise "it can be used as a selective protection which is not the intent"), with no cooldown, since it is paid three times (the muster, the docked allies' actions, the whole enemy turn on the carrier), the lever its price, never a clock. The Spotting Flare Exposes, and charged Suppresses too, a stun gated by the price and clocked by Numbed (nothing on the helicarrier is called Mark: Ryan, 21 October). The Sutler's Hoist shields and cools an ally, and charged resets it whole. The Reveille Horn is a team reroll, and charged an ally acting again, the action-economy row's tempo lent, paid in a banked resource and the carrier's action. The Hoist reloads nothing on the Musk Ox (Ryan: a locked pairing would take the artillery's levers). The names are Claude's on Ryan's pick, the chassis's his. What reopens it: the fill reading as farming (the rate), the charged dock as a free round (its price), and three bars on one maker reading badly on a phone. For later balancing (Ryan, 22 October): an ally acting first keeps the charge for the other pieces; he would rather make the forced spend work than add a control for one chassis, so the pieces may be reworked first (Phased on every spend of the Screens, or dropped for the global lure), and the primed form may come back. No model yet.

Decided

The Musk Ox: the artillery, its guns fired from forged ammo.

Ryan's go, 21 October (its brief: git show bc97727:docs/briefs/ws_artillery.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier three, the maker's second capstone and the raining death the Swallowtail leaves whole: the cold price the heat row asks for, on the maker whose kit is cold. Ryan's picture: "a cross between a Big Bertha, a M270 MLRS, and/or a truck-mounted MEADS launcher. Big. Big wheels"; wide, the old cannon on the centreline and the high-tech guns on flank sponsons ("the 'high-tech' W&S design where they tried to resolve their inherent bias against energy weapons"). Its passive, the Desublimation Forge (Ryan's name): its damaging guns spend ammo instead of heating it; the magazine opens each fight low and ramps (Ryan, 22 October, of the first round: "make it a total of 3"), and a vent it spends on itself reloads. Everything else keeps heat on a low heat bar (Ryan: "Scorched and Burn are the only way it really interacts with the heat system unless it opts into heat-generating shields, buffs, or debuffs"). Ammo is a resource beside heat and never a buff, so no Silence or Purge reaches it, and only its own pieces reload it. It keeps three rules heat cannot have: it opens low and ramps, the early rounds earning and the later spending; its old gun pays for its new ones; and a full magazine buys a salvo.

The Shrapnel Recuperator, a cluster cannon whose Flak is the team's and whose refund is ammo for each foe it hits, never a setup for its own salvo (Ryan: "a one-two that's way too obvious"); the refund repeats the Honey Badger's hopper (a crowd as ammunition for the boss) and passes the pattern-in-context rule, since here it prices every gun. The Unrestricted Expenditure, the order to spend everything: the whole magazine as strikes at random live foes, a boss killer when the boss stands alone and the trash obliterated when it does not ("The artillery piece isn't a precision weapon"), steered by thinning the field or firing at a lone boss; the biggest per-hit multiplier in the game, so the foes' Flak multiplies with it and the strike's number is the lever. The quick reload (for testing, a working name): Rapid, ammo back, heat its limiter, so with the foes' heat it threatens an overheat on the low bar. The siege battery keeps its charge, power bought by using components; the Musk Ox's ammo is the price of firing, so spending more for keywords stays the siege battery's. Retired on the way: the reloader gated on an empty magazine, Flak 2 on the cannon (the one-two), the big gun with no refund (the closest to inverse heat), and the Hoist as its loader; the bench keeps ammo carried between fights, an overflow shell, a ranging ramp and shell types. What reopens it: ammo running plentiful (the reloads or the trickle), the salvo multiplying the foes' Flak past its worth (the strike's number), and the quick reload kept or cut once tested. No model yet.

Decided

Wainwright & Sutler's roster: ten machines, three, five and two.

Ryan, 19 and 20 October, the tiers 3-5-2 (if 4-4-2 worked better, the Dingo would be his move down). Tier one: the Prairie Dog, the Cassowary and the Cottonmouth, a scout, a gunslinger and the reach piece, no front, accepted (the Cassowary row). Tier two: the Dingo, the Clydesdale, the funnel-web, the gunship and the Whip Scorpion. Tier three: the Swallowtail and the Musk Ox. Ten, eleven being too many (Ryan, 18 October). Nine are in content and the engine, five of them waiting on models (the Cassowary, the Cottonmouth, the Whip Scorpion, the Swallowtail and the Musk Ox). The gunship, whose fitted weapons raise its numbers by their tags, is a proposal (docs/briefs/ws_gunship.md), free to run since the tags and the rescale landed (Ryan, 21 October: it waited on them, "since that's its whole deal"). What reopens it: a tier one reading as a wall or a free win in the runner's bands; a fourth tier one wanted, which is 4-4-2 with the Dingo down.

Decided

The Armadillo seals: Minerva's form-changing tank.

Ryan's go, 20 October, on his framework of 15 October (its brief: git show 63487a9:docs/briefs/minerva_armadillo.md). Tier one. Its passive, Lockdown: a shield it spends on itself seals it, armour up, its own cooling stopped and hotter each turn; a vent or a weapon opens it; a shield on an ally and equipment leave the form as it is. The seal never stacks (Ryan: the granted armour is tied to the mode); an ally's vent still cools the sealed shell (Ryan, 20 October: allowed). Its locked Cryo Flingers (Ryan: "It launches a sticky cryo canister with a really annoying beep or something, for the lore reason") lure and mark: any machine's weapon hit on the mark cools the hitter, so the tank's output is cooling the team contends for (the Armadillo to reseal, a hot hitter to keep firing), never an ally cooling the Armadillo for free. Ryan marks Cryo for reuse ("probably a healthier way to grant ally cooling than venting directly") and allows it a minor rider later, only if the roster has room. The Faraday Hull and the Lockdown Shutters left it: the Hull's idea is the echidna's if he takes it; the shutters are the look of the seal. The forms refused: the hypertank at tier two whose shutters drew blows (a build of nothing but lures); the blanket draw while sealed ("a free pass for 3 or 4 turns"); team armour or a team shield on opening (an unkillable team by another name); the stored blows cashed as damage ("back where we started") or as rerolls ("that doesn't fit with the theme"); heat a hit so fast a seal lasts a turn (tedious); and the Hull in any form on it (the discharge again). What reopens it: the seal protects nobody without the lure, so the pair's rate is watched in play; the all-in cooling team; and the greedy bot values a lure for allies at nothing, so its runner rows wait on its valuations (the to-do). Its model shows the seal (the seal row, under Motion).

Open

Parked ideas.

Burning terrain; statuses carried between fights; player formations; two rerolls or ten entries if the Clydesdale's passive is thin.

Open

Aphelion's next chassis.

Ryan, 12 October, and his go on the roster analysis (14 and 20 October): a Gundam homage, never too on point (a shield, a handheld rifle and a beam sword make one), its passive a combo buff that grows with every blow it lands until a turn without an attack breaks it ("Every blow counts"; not the Beam tag, which "is just setting it up for a different synergy that it doesn't start with"); a jellyfish flyer in the Science Vessel's register, its width narrowed to soft control, its spread carrying Lure, Disrupt and Purge and its EMP Purge with Disrupt ("neat, good direction, needs refinement"); the Carrier, the Protoss homage, at tier three and designed on its own (the battlecruiser's session is "not related at all"). A Zoids homage on four legs, a fast walker, is a candidate whose maker is open (Aphelion or the Arsenal). A Voltron or Power Rangers homage is one of their single machines, never a combination. Cats and wolves stay out of Aphelion's names. Parked: a large hovercraft; long guns while the engagement range may change; a time-shifter, Ryan's spacetime manipulator ("soft control, moving or delaying hits as a whole identity"), its holes in the briefs README's bench. Each candidate's brief holds his calls and its open questions (docs/briefs/aphelion_*.md, docs/briefs/zoids_walker.md); nothing is built before each session's go.

Open

The Arsenal's next chassis.

Ryan, 12 and 14 October: more Arsenal machines, medieval, demonic, eldritch and sinister, none of them another Overcharge ramp. Misharu: justice, a foe whose blow breaks a shield punished, its payoff Penance, a weaken on the breaker. Mastema: fire that passes from the dead to the living, its locked pieces tools rather than a gun, leaning to fire that runs through the team (its Ewer carries the allies' Burning and Scorched onto a foe). Minos: every foe its weapons reach changes rank, not optional, a power the team works around. The Onryō: a curse on whoever wounds the team (Grudge was a working term from the myth, never the machine's word: Ryan, 31 October), never popped for burst, its kind open again since Flak took the flat on-hit. An Arsenal cleanse that moves debuffs onto a foe (the Scapegoat Brand, a rare after the vocabulary pass) is possible since Burning means something on either side. Each has a brief with Claude's first round and review, unanswered (docs/briefs/arsenal_*.md); nothing is built before each session's go.

Decided

Kestrel's line: the strafer at tier one, the bomber above it, ten in all.

Ryan, 14 October, from the roster analysis's gaps (docs/roster_analysis.md, section 8): the strafer, every weapon it fires striking twice at half, the Kite's bargain in count instead of magnitude; each strike loses the foe's armour, so it slaughters trash and bounces off plate, a detachment the map can read, and riders per blow love it; Kestrel's second tier one, so its tender deal varies. The bomber above it at tier three (Ryan, 15 October), the Lammergeier (its row below). The line decided and named on 21 October (the hub: docs/briefs/kestrel_line.md): three quarters of it flies (Ryan, 20 October), Kestrel's lean heat, rerolls, strikes and risk. Four are in content since 23 October: the Needletail runnable, the Skua since its engine of 25 October, and the Storm Petrel and the Giant Petrel since their engines of 30 October (Ryan: “go for the ready to build ones”; their briefs retired: git show d84ad16:docs/briefs/kestrel_shield_spender.md and kestrel_gun_platform.md). Their models: the Skua's, the Puffin's and the Storm Petrel's are Ryan's picks from five rounds of look tests on his notes (23 to 27 October, docs/looks/kestrel_*.png), built 28 October: the Skua the jaeger, the Puffin and the Storm Petrel on each other's hulls (his swap of 26 October: the research boat is the Puffin, at the light size; the wide hull on the giant boosters the off-tank, at the medium), and the Lammergeier the flying wing drawn first for the Giant Petrel (its row below); the Needletail's two modes (in fast, mark, hover and vector to dodge, out fast: "a fox in a henhouse") and the Giant Petrel as the catamaran are his next session's, from the fifth round's looks.

The Skua (its brief: git show f87b831:docs/briefs/kestrel_strafer.md): the swing-wing strafer at tier one, a flyer parted from the Kite by its silhouette, the seabird that harries in repeated dives (the Butcherbird turned out a BattleTech 'Mech's). Its Split-cycle Breech rolls each weapon once and halves the roll, rounded down and never below one, for each of two strikes, every buff and the foe's conditions counting on each strike and its armour too (Ryan, 24 and 25 October: "Don't roll both halves. Roll, then split", and rounding down, since equal strikes keep the die's "1 ×2" readable: "if it proves weak, we buff it in another way. The synergy it has with Overcharge and Flak alone might make it a moot point"; 27 October, the buff: "rounded down, min 1", so a Twin Autoguns' 1 strikes 1 twice where it struck 0 twice, and the die reads "1 ×2" and never "0–1 ×2"). So with basics alone it is level with a plain gun on an even number, a point down on an odd one and nothing against plate, and every stack of Overcharged or Flak counts twice, on a halved 1's strikes of 0 too. A debuff fires twice too, its riders on each strike (Ryan, 25 October: "Debuff should fire twice regardless of damage. That's kind of the whole point"). Its Flechette Pod sprays: the first strike lands where it is spent and each after it on a random foe in reach, a kill clearing its slot at once, so a hole in the front opens the foe behind it (Ryan, 16 October: "Choose first strike, subsequent random"). Its runner rows (25 October, docs/measurements.md): alone, third of the nine tier ones on the rank and file, quick on the small packs, falling to the swarms on its 20 hull and bouncing off the big foes, the Pod its most spent entry; beside the Puffin with the Overcharge Coil, a flat buff on each strike is its way through plate, the Treant and the Bone Wyvern taken in nine rounds where they took twenty-four. What reopens its numbers: Ryan's play of its start.

The Storm Petrel: Kestrel's tier-one body, so a tier-one body comes from a second maker and the Armadillo is no must-pick (Ryan, 19 October), and a generalist that can zoom off and fight a pack alone. While its shield stands every blow on it loses a little, and its weapons burn shield in place of heat, the target taking a zap of what the shield paid, so a cold gun is how it stands still; its lock lures a foe and regenerates its own shield, self only, never Rapid, the die its brake. The plate walls the packs that strike small and is no wall against a swarm; the lever is the plate's number before the regen's stacking. Built 30 October: the shield pays a weapon's heat as the spend adds it, overshield included, the rest reaching the gauge; the zap is a plain blow after the strikes, through the foe's damage taken and armour, lost on a kill; the plate reads the shield each blow meets; the regen is a timed grant of its own each spend, bought, so a foe's purge takes it. The die shows the shield a weapon takes behind the shield's mark in the heat slot, and over a foe the blow joined to its zap. The bot reads the burn as shield lost (the hull its gap lets through and the shield that would have stood), the zap as damage and the regen as hull kept over its rounds. Its first rows (30 seeds, a direction, docs/measurements.md): alone in the Armadillo's band and above it on the ladder's recolours, at half the rounds; beside the Puffin about half again the Armadillo's pair on the big foes. Its burn on basics is section 12's question.

The Needletail (its brief: git show 9ced5e5:docs/briefs/kestrel_interceptor.md): the spotter at tier two (its gate reads buffs and debuffs, which no basic is). Equipment, a buff or a debuff, is Rapid on it once a round, a cantrip on every maker's setters and buffs, and its Fovea Designator Exposes a foe: in every machine's Direct reach and its armour counting for nothing, through the next round, refreshed and never added (Ryan: "go aggressive"). Exposed is the debuff's word alone: a back-rank foe with nothing in front of it is in Direct reach by the layout, and neither a chip nor the codex says so (Ryan, 26 October: a chip for it "steps on the debuff and is very confusing"; "It's not a condition. It's a layout. It's obvious what can be hit or not. Adding text around that is clutter"). Declined: the flat on-hit half of Ryan's first shape, which is Flak's kind; benched: a clock that waits for the foe to act.

The Giant Petrel: the formation leader at tier three, an ekranoplan, aggressive where the Swallowtail is the supportive mobile HQ, pushing its allies to act and keeping the tempo up: it banks the team's unspent rerolls, and its locks sell them for a reroll, a spent ally's second action, a kill's rerolls and an ally's Overcharged roll, the Petrel never trading its own turn for the ally's (Ryan, 20 October: an ally going again that is not one turn traded for another). Its must-pick guard is the runner's team rows with and without it, the levers the Booster's price, the bank's cap and the Salvo's rerolls. Built 30 October: the bank carries what the player's turn leaves unspent, up to three, while it fights, a roar's cost coming off the whole; the Booster's ally use lands only on a spent ally while the team holds its three, the ally's die rolling by Rapid's path; the Salvo's two come once a spend that kills; the Uplink's roll never lands on the entry showing, and a spent die rolls nothing. The bot's look-or-action valuation: a reroll's worth is one yardstick, a share of the team's average blow; an ally's action is its die's average blow; the price is kept from the looks as the round opens while an action is worth more than they are, the Booster held for an ally to spend first; and since the bot rerolls only as a round opens, a reroll a spend pays or brings later is worth what the bank carries of it. Its first rows (30 seeds, a direction, docs/measurements.md): in Kestrel's four level with the Lammergeier's under the greedy; in Wainwright & Sutler's four over the Swallowtail's on every formation, most of it its own guns, a fifth of an action bought a fight: the team to watch for a must-pick.

The designations are Claude's (Ryan, 21 October: "Designations as you see fit"), in content. The last two of the ten, each with a brief and a first round unanswered, their tiers open: the Magpie (a working name), a support engine leaning to the thief or to its intelligence (docs/briefs/kestrel_magpie.md), and the Snowy Owl, a stealth unit, the silent hunter on the map or in the fight (docs/briefs/kestrel_owl.md). A look parked for either (Ryan, 28 October: "a good candidate for the owl or magpie"): the ornithopter blade, two F-14 pairs on their own pivots that open into an X of four wings and beat, and close along the body into a blade (docs/looks/kestrel_ornithopter.png, a look test, never content until its chassis lands). Names, numbers and looks are Ryan's.

Decided

The Lammergeier: the bomber, its shots waiting in the enemy's slots.

Ryan, 14 to 21 October (its brief: git show 62192a4:docs/briefs/kestrel_bomber.md). Kestrel, tier three (Ryan, 15 October), a heavy flying-wing flyer, big for Kestrel (a Lean bending as Leans do, as the Giant Petrel's does): the bearded vulture, which drops bones from a height to break them. Every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe may also be spent on an empty slot in the enemy's formation (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), and the shot waits there for whoever steps in, with its keywords; a kill clears its slot at once and the reinforcements step in at the round's end, so kill-then-mine is a tempo decision in one turn, and the Marsh's Reraise and the Meadow's Gel halves walk into it. Its locks: the Lorentz Contractor, the pull, gathers every mine on the field into its target's slot and sets them off on a foe standing there, or gathers them into an empty slot as a setup (Ryan, 21 October); the Syrinx Drone, the mark: the next weapon to kill the foe leaves its shot in the slot, Rapid on a foe already Exposed, and otherwise it Exposes the foe, never refreshing it (Ryan: "So no refresh"), so the Needletail's Expose or the Swallowtail's Flare makes the mark free. The readings Ryan agreed (21 October: "The point is to try and cheat out an opening"): the landing waits until the reinforcement step has placed every arrival, then runs left to right; a stun mine takes the arrival's first action; shots still waiting when the arrival falls wait on for the next ("if it turns out strong, that's easy to change"); a Swap and the drone take a foe, never a slot. Worth most where fights bring reinforcements, which the foes' fresh start is to make common; the pull pays in a fight with none. Its stock is stand-ins by kind, which the stock pass's table missed (the to-do). Its model (built 28 October on Ryan's call of the 27th: the chrome chevron drawn as the Giant Petrel's hovercraft goes to the bomber, airborne, "ditching the tandem wing is fine"): one flying wing with four booster drums across its trailing edge, the bay under its body with a lit seam, the Contractor's dish and the Drone's tubes modelled in, the four mounts on the wing clear of the drums (his one note). Its designation reads Flying-Wing Minelayer since; the name is his to change. Benched: the tandem wing of its go, two wing pairs fore and aft, never drawn.

Open

Minerva's roster.

Built: the Honey Badger, the Armadillo and the Wild Boar (proposed down to tier one in the briefs README's tiers, undecided). In session: the Honey Badger's identity (docs/briefs/minerva_honey_badger.md: the hopper lean taken up, its numbers open); the echidna, a lighter tier two that wants to be struck (docs/briefs/minerva_hedgehog.md: Ryan, 20 October, left open for now; the Faraday Hull's idea its if he takes it); the siege battery, Ryan's H-tank at tier three (docs/briefs/minerva_siege_battery.md), parked on his charge brief of 20 October: "using components adds one charge, up to a max. Each of the cannons spends charge to gain power, such as armour piercing at one charge, penetrate with two charge, and so on, and perhaps the cooldown increases with each charge spent as well"; and the battlecruiser, the second tier three (docs/briefs/minerva_battlecruiser.md), designed apart from the Carrier (Ryan, 20 October: "not related at all"). Each a session of its own, proposed in chat first.

Pinned

Transforming chassis.

One reads only with a form-change system, which could be built and is not a priority (Ryan, 12 October).

Models

Decided

Every model is a function in code.

tools/lowforge/models_low.py holds each machine as proportions; Ryan judges renders; a Blender edit lasts only once it is written back into the code.

Decided

Hardpoints are gun mounts.

One hp_n pin each, pointing forward; fixed components are modelled in, with no pin. A shaped component takes the next mount in die order as one of five classes in its maker's metal: barrel, twin barrel, rack, blade (katar style) or dish (shield projectors, buff coils). Armour and vents show nowhere; a spare hardpoint carries a bare pad. A mount holds one component at one place (Ryan, 28 October: "we don't have variable-width hardpoints"), so a stock part is never wider than a fitted one (the Dingo's one Twin Autoguns spread across both nose ports were two hardpoints). A model names the component each pin's stock part is, and the game wears a fitted die against that record, on the map as in the fight, never against content's stock: a stock that moves on shows its class shapes until the model follows (the stock pass of 22 October had left thirteen models drawing kit their machines no longer carried, found 28 October); every model has as many pins as its chassis has hardpoints, a test holding it. What reopens it: mounts of more than one width, a system of their own.

Decided

A machine is drawn as big as its class by mass.

Light, medium, heavy or assault, each chassis's size in content (the Honey Badger heavy: small and scrappy, but heavy machinery). An assault machine overflows its hex, drawn south over north; the codex and the hangar frame every machine whole.

Decided

Models are seen from above.

Anything the player must read goes on top. A launcher shows its missile tile on its front face only; a booster's exhaust is one glow, pale at the centre and deep red at the rim; Wainwright & Sutler parts look bolted on and squared off.

Decided

Settled looks.

The Prairie Dog a closed buggy on a high stance; the Baboon's fists at knee height from behind; the Clydesdale's turret pairs keep their spacing; the Stingray's fans flush, never rotors on booms; the Puffin a long chined boat with its skirt and stripe, its side plates level as repulsors under way and folded up near plumb to amplify its shield in a fight (Ryan, 27 October, on the swap: "really looking like the Puffin"); the Storm Petrel the wide hull on the giant boosters with the vents on their covers, its Riot Field shown as emitters and lit rails and never a plate, St. Elmo's fire the lit port in its snub nose; the Skua the jaeger, a jet's straight nose and wrist-swung hands, never an F-14; the Nemesis a Pacific Rim homage; the funnel-web a turret on stilts in Wainwright & Sutler's military camp that creeps one foot at a time (Ryan's good to go, 20 October); the Kite's Pinion Driver a matched pair, one at each wing tip, streamlined and symmetrical, its nose mounts the hardpoints (Ryan, 28 October), each a ramjet-gun, a drum like the boosters' tapering to a lit exhaust and the barrel forward out of a toothed inlet whose teeth run aft as strakes tapering into the drum (Ryan, 29 October: "something that looks like it gives an extra boost of speed on a kill"); the Stingray's Photosphere Lance on its dome turret (Ryan, 28 October); the Dingo's Combat Translocator a riveted cabinet with two raised prongs and a lit coil threaded on a core where its rear tube stood, totally not a teleporter (Claude's lean, taken 28 October; the coil floated between the prongs, 29 October), and its rear mount on a gimbal on the flat of its roof, left of the harpoon (Ryan, 29 October); the Armadillo's Cryo Flingers a twin canister rack in its hatch, their beacons blinking (the beep; the lean, taken 28 October), the barrels bored, bore and rim in the guns' dark steel (Ryan, 29 October), the hatch's striped collar meeting the deck on a skirt in the livery (Ryan, 30 October, of the stripe run down to the deck: “that's now a lot of hazard stripe in one area. I don't mind the extension, but maybe this is a good place for livery instead”), its stack become a louvred radiator sunk flush in each flank's chamfer above the hind hip, sized to what the slope holds there, and two near-square louvred vents standing out of the back panel low down, all of which throw their louvres open over flaring heat a pair at a time, hold them open together, shut them a pair at a time a beat apart and seal with the ports, and a vent on each flank's chamfer just ahead of the rear ports, a closed dark protrusion that slides out to vent, its thin-slatted faces glowing between the slats to the cameras ahead and behind alike, every vent's glow an orange of red and green alone, #ff7800 at its height (Ryan, 29 October: the stack's rain flap was “more W&S comical than heavy Minerva”; the vents that replaced it “big boxy radiators sticking out at an angle”, “in a darker colour, not the default hull colour”, the tail band square again and its bevel's taper liked, kept in mind for the next pass; “new vents on the sides”, “small rectangular protrusions when sealed” that “either slide out, or lever up using the forward edge as a pivot”, slid since a lever on that edge shows its face behind only, with “a larger footprint to sell”; the glow to “lean way more yellow/orange” from the ember's red, then, golden, “way too yellow”, so an orange under the line past which an orange reads to him as yellow; 30 October, after the radiators had stood as boxes about the tail's back panel and top corners: “Place them flush on the chamfered face above the leg joint, and resize to fit. Split the long rear vent into two squareish ones”, then, asked whether an idea of his was still to come, “nope, i liked what you did”; then “The rear ones can protrude a bit more”, “desync the vent openings, then close them all at the same-ish time”, “Open them in pairs, not one at a time, and leave them open longer once they're all open”, and “slow down the standing back up at the end and start it as the vents are closing. the vents can stay open a touch less long. desync the pairs closing just a bit”), and every weapon it carries running in and out along its line of fire, never pitching (Ryan, 29 October: "they should only move along their axis of firing, in and out"); its seal is the seal row's, under Motion.

Open

Each component class's size per machine (a pin's scale).

Motion

Decided

Every machine has its own idle acts and its own movement, on both screens.

Four acts each, five for the Baboon, the Kite and the Skua (Ryan, 28 October: the Kite's treatment, bigger animations that show off its agility, the Kite's own among them), for the Nemesis and the Ammit, whose turns came as acts (the footwork row), and for the Dingo, whose coil has one; six for the Armadillo, its come-about and its vents; and the sealed Armadillo plays three of its own (the seal row). Foes stay painted pictures that breathe and lunge.

Decided

Machines are piloted, not animals.

An act is what a pilot does: looks round through the head, turrets or sensors, flexes limbs and weapons, test-charges a weapon, shuffles impatiently. Cockpits are fixed, except the Clydesdale's head; the Ammit's head is still by design.

Decided

Heavy and mechanical, with inertia.

MechWarrior or Star Wars, not Gundam or Evangelion. Every part chases its act on a servo slightly under critical damping, timed by the machine's heft; a blow is keyed as a slam that accelerates into the hit and stops dead.

Decided

Acts are loud and never in step.

Each moves several joints and the body at once, with glows hot enough to read across the map on a phone. A machine picks acts at random, never the same twice running, with long rests between.

Decided

A grounded machine stands still between acts.

Only a flyer rides a slow swell; a hovercraft balances like a platform on water, tipping in gusts, catching itself and settling with a heave.

Decided

Turrets turn slowly, not far, and pause before turning back.

At most 70 degrees from the bow, 35 degrees a second, and a hold of 0.6 seconds before reversing; lowforge refuses an act that breaks this. Under way a turret turns with the hull.

Decided

Walkers stomp and plod.

A planted foot never slides; the body vaults over near-straight legs and nods onto each foot, never rolling side to side; a walker turns at the waist and steps round to turn in place.

Decided

A move on the map is one smooth curve with real acceleration.

The machine turns onto its way, accelerates at its own rate, holds full speed through corners and eases to a stop; pace follows the move value, so a longer route takes longer. Drifters (the Dingo wildly, the Puffin tamer) nose in and step their tails out; the flyers and the Stingray bank; every other machine follows its heading. Packs keep quick straight steps.

Decided

An act may name the places it plays.

Travel that leaves the hex (the Kite's loop, circle and figure eight, the Dingo's strafe) plays on the map only.

Decided

Motion is data beside each model.

Joints, acts, gaits and cornering are authored in models_low.py, written to art/models/<id>.rig.json and read by MechRig; no runtime code keys on a chassis id. The hangar page's motion port changes in the same commit.

Decided

Motion is judged running.

RIFTGATE_ACTS_DEBUG=1, RIFTGATE_MOVE_DEBUG=1 and the hangar page's Models tab. Adding joints leaves the geometry identical (tools/lowforge/glbcmp.py).

Decided

Planted footwork for the walkers' turns and sidesteps.

Ryan, 20 October, after the funnel-web's pivot: the Dillo gets a turn of its own, and the bipeds lateral motion in their acts, old or new; more joints never hurt. None were needed: a hip takes the yaw and roll that keep its foot planted and pointing where it landed, the knee and the ankle the rest, the foot flat (_footwork in models_low.py). The acts: the Armadillo comes about, turning its whole shell to look (no head and no waist) in the diagonal pairs it walks in, and holds with the port guns up and the cowl lamps lit; the Nemesis braces for the meteor, a foot back as the whole machine turns on its planted foot under the waist's turn (the waist alone had turned it, the feet planted), and squares up to something at its left, a wide stamp into the stance, fists up, then back round (a sideways pace each way was built first; Ryan: it looked like dancing); the Ammit circles its prey, the snout kept on it, two moves round to one side, a pause and two back (one move each way through the centre was built first; Ryan: two to one side and back is more effective). Ryan's calls on the others: the Clydesdale keeps its look-round and its waist, since by its lore it is not supposed to pivot well; the Baboon's acts are fine and not special, so nothing for it yet. Judged running: the Dillo's come-about is good (Ryan, 20 October), the rest his to judge (the to-do).

Open

Attack acts.

A blow read by its weapon (guns traversing to bear, a recoil, a flash) instead of today's strike, at the cost of an act per weapon kind. A session of its own, the design discussed first.

Open

A render margin round each skirmish machine view.

A travelling act leaves its frame on the phone: render larger and overflow (a fill cost, perhaps flyers only), or keep acts inside the frame. Discuss first.

Pinned

The helicopter style of movement stays in the engine, unused.

Decided

The Armadillo seals on screen.

While it is Sealed its guns sink along their lines into the shell, then each port's iris plug rises out of the port's floor and shuts it just under the rim, in the ports' stripe (the Lockdown Shutters live on as the look of the seal, 20 October; leaves drawn back across the shell showed outside it, so they rest under the floor, Ryan, 29 October), the louvres of the radiators and the rear vents shut flat, the side vents stay home, the cowl lamps and the vents' glow go dark and the whole machine hunkers down on its planted feet, the shell low and its brow dipped: its defensive base pose, from which it plays acts of its own (Ryan, 29 October: "When it's in sealed mode, the animations set shouldn't be the same, its base pose should be hunkered down in a defensive position"), so no open act clips the sunk guns. The flingers' rack stays live, since a sealed Armadillo still lures, and it never vents sealed (Ryan, 29 October: “I don't think a vent animation should run during the sealed form”); it opens in reverse. A model may hold a form while its machine carries the status of the same name; the form may carry the whole machine with it and acts of its own, and held it plays those, else the machine's own less any that moves what it holds, and none while it turns; RIFTGATE_SEAL_DEBUG=1 holds every form and lets it go in turn, to judge running. Three leaves a port, where a camera has more: a phone pays per draw call, and the shutting reads the same at the machine's size. Ryan judges it running (the to-do). Open, its vents stand ajar, each louvre half its throw and each side vent half out of its well, the vent act opening them the rest of the way and the seal shutting them (Ryan, 2 November: “When unsealed, the vents should be open, and then more open during the vent animation”): a model may stand in an ajar pose while a form is not held, its share giving way as an act swings the joint so the act ends where it was authored to. The form reads off the screen too (“armadillo needs indication when a die face will transform it, like a die chip or something. It also needs some highlight or something when it is transforming, and a better indication of what form it's in”): a shield it may spend on itself wears the seal's chip on its die while it is open, and a weapon or a vent the opening's while it is sealed, each read in the readout; Sealed wears the same mark, a seam with arrows driving shut onto it, the opening's thrown off it, so they part by structure; the sealed machine wears a steady hexagon shell in the shield's blue, and as it seals or opens rings close onto it or are thrown off it under the word SEALED or OPEN. RIFTGATE_SEAL_DEBUG=1 plays the shell and the burst with the model.

8

Commander and city

The commander's layer is not built; it waits for a balance baseline.

Decided

The commander's tools do what machines cannot.

Manipulation, mitigation, information, movement: never a fifth gun. A reusable ability deals no direct damage; a one-shot may, and damage one-shots belong on the battlefield (soften a pack before contact), so the machines' dice stay the killing power in a skirmish.

Pinned

The commander layer waits for a baseline balance.

It needs heavy UI work and a place on the skirmish screen.

Open

A command die, or one-shots only.

Recommended

One command die, rolled with the machines' dice.

Its entries are rechargeable abilities that cannot be hoarded: smoke blanking the back rank's intents, a drone screen, focus fire, a scan of a pack's next two rounds. Its size (d4, d6, d8) is a research path, the larger dice carrying a backfire entry or two.

Decided

Command entries come from the hangar, never from rewards.

They would be wrong as loot and would dominate every pick.

Decided

Spending a consumable must not feel bad.

Ryan hoards potions, so spending is the default.

Recommended

One-shots expire when the rift closes, and the pouch holds three.

A fourth forces a use or a discard; a starting stock chosen in the hangar restocks every battle, research raising capacity and quality. Doom Eternal's short loop is the touchstone; saving a full pouch for the boss stays a legitimate blitz, paid for in missed upgrades.

Decided

Each Fracture of the Rupture meter grants the reward pick.

Unweighted by domain (section 4). The meter, the Fractures and the seal were built without the pick on 30 October (Ryan, 24 October: the picks are “designed only in high-level theory, and maybe not warranted at all”), since they pace the battle on their own; whether a prize rides each Fracture is decided after they are played, with the economy pass (section 12).

Recommended

The pick is one of three.

A battle-long modifier on a carried component, a one-shot, or a battle-wide boon (an extra reroll a round). Temporary upgrades amplify the build, never replace it.

Decided

Battlefield abilities belong to the city.

Sprint, airlift, teleport, tractor beam, orbital strike, on cooldowns counted in map turns, legible and impossible to hoard; city research shortens them, adds charges and unlocks more. No command die rolls on the battlefield. A skirmish component's cooldown is a printed property of its own, counted in rounds (below).

Decided

Cooldowns in a skirmish: a printed property on specific pieces.

Ryan, 14 October, after the roster analysis weighed it (its section 7): a cooldown is a printed property on a specific piece, never a general mechanic, shown on the entry as dark with the turns left, so it is paid in the die's reliability and read like any other rule; he designed the funnel-web's web bomb and the Gundam's parry with it the same day. It prices what heat cannot: a price per die rather than per spend, dear on a small die and cheap on a big one, so it falls on the cold kinetics and the big dice that heat ignores, and it is the revolver's native currency. It also fixes a reading (Ryan): nearly every Rapid component is "Rapid, cooldown 1", the switch-off, and the Nemesis's fists are Rapid alone, limited by rage (section 6). No cleanse lifts a cooldown: a price, as heat is. No cap on where a cooldown goes or how many a die carries: Ryan, 15 October, struck the recommended cap of one cooldown entry on a die below tier three as artificially limiting, and the rest of Claude's list (never on a basic) goes by the same reason. The exit: if the counts clutter a phone die or read as hidden, the shape retires to Rapid's one-round form.

Recommended

Some city abilities reach only so far from the gate.

Artillery covers the southern half, an airlift leaves only from the gate, so pushing north leaves support behind.

Pinned

Command entries with a battlefield meaning too.

Rejected for now: it doubles the learning per entry.

9

The invasion: domains and the party

The other world is a JRPG world, and a rift is where its overworld pushes into ours. From their side, our city is the dungeon.

Decided

The JRPG genre invades.

Final Fantasy is the spine and the genre is blended. Its flat sixteen-bit sprites against our gritty 3D machines are the clash the game is built on.

Decided

A rift's domain dictates what spawns.

A biome of the classic overworld with its bleed (the tiles the flattened ground paints), wildlife of one tactical signature, a boss and a hero. A rift rolls one lead and at most one guest, shown by the front card and the bleed, so scouting is the land.

Decided

Four domains, and the answer is tactical, never a damage-type triangle.

Elements are flavour, so which front to take is a build question. The names are working names.

DomainIts signatureThe build must answerWildlifeElitesBosses
The Meadow: grass, forestNumbers that split and swarmArea damage, armour against volume, kill orderGoblin, Dire Wolf, Gel, Mandragora; the Treant (massive)The goblin shaman; the Elder Treant; the LeshyRotbloom, Floraison, the King Gel
The Waste: desert, badlandsFixed damage no shield answers; theft of rerolls and entriesHull and tempo over shields; redundancy on the dieNeedler, Antlion, Cockatrice; the Sandworm (massive)The Old Man of the Desert, the king sandwormGilgamesh, the Fallen Weapon, the Pilgrim
The Marsh: swamp, graveyardHard to keep downKill order, reach to the healer, burstRevenant, Ghost, Zombie KnightThe Reaper, a hunter; the Bone WyvernThe Matador, the Phantom Train, the Vampire Lord
The Peak: volcano, fire caveHard hits on a telegraph, and heatShields for the telegraph, cooling, a lure onto a cold machineBomb, Salamander, Drake; the Magma Elemental (massive)None yetThe Behemoth, the Cinder Tortoise, the Obsidian Tyrant
Decided

A domain reads on the map by structure.

The bleed's tiles and the disc under every pack, so a sprite's hue means nothing. Each domain paints its own tileset (Ryan, 29 October: “They all need their own tileset, first of all. Shouldn't all be painting green meadows”), its water and walls included (30 October: “walls and water certainly should change with tileset, even if they have no functional change”), and each ground its own tone of the domain's, two steps at most in a channel of the compositor's grid and three in all, so the grounds differ without jarring (30 October, of one ground for all: “the different types need different backgrounds. Its very monotone now. It just needed to be less jarring”; of the first build's fills, wildly apart: “the ground can differ where appropriate. Just not wildly different like it was”; and of the Waste's and the Marsh's rough, “a bit more separation from the plains colour”). Water is always the domain's liquid with a rim and ripples, and the walls always a range standing out of the top of its hex into the hexes above (30 October: “The wall objects on all can be larger, or at least taller”), so a hex's rule keeps one shape in every domain; the paint never sets or leans the map's shape (the shapes row). Retired: the first tilesets of 30 October, a fill a ground with marks drawn in rectangles and water and the walls alike in all four, which failed his read (“some of the decorations are just weird or bad”; the Peak wanting “a more volcanic palette”; the Marsh’s that “does not read undead marsh or anything really”). Built again the same day (DomainGround; “Overall the art is a huge improvement though”): outlined pixel sprites, the Meadow's woods, cottages and snowy peaks, the Waste's cacti, sandstone ruins, oases and red buttes, the Marsh a dark bog of dead trees, graves and crags, the Peak black rock split by glowing fissures, a fire temple, lava and volcanoes; the Marsh keeps no violet, the genre's poison swamp, since violet is the rift's intent. The Peak's lava is an orange-red, never the yellow-orange that read as acid (30 October: “it looks more like acid than lava (yes, lava is hot orange-yellow in real life, but this is a painted game)”), and stops short of red, where a red lake hid a pack's red mark from a deutan; since the enemy's marks went violet (30 October) a redder lake would hide none of them, and he kept the orange-red with the fight's backdrop on the same palette (Ryan, 30 October: “lava looks great”). Every colour sits on the compositor's posterize grid and is scored by the three colourblind simulations on the ground it is drawn on (docs/measurements.md). Judged running (the to-do; RIFTGATE_BLEED_DEBUG=1 paints the whole field).

Decided

The Gel is a wall, not an attacker: its halves step up in its place at once, a pair in one slot.

Ryan, 25 October, from play: "when a gel dies, it should spawn the half-gel immediately in place, not wait for the reinforcement phase. Gel doesn't need breach, it's a wall not an attacker." So a kill on a Gel never opens its hole, and its Engulf lost the breach. The halves stand as a pair in its own slot (Ryan, 25 and 26 October: going to the pool "is so anti-thematic"; "bend the 4-column rule for the gels ... Cramming two small slots into a single slot so we don't have to redo all the AoE rules (I.e. aoe treats the double-slot the same as a single slot, but single-target weapons don't)"; "half units should exist as a pair taking the place of one unit"): one slot to the formation, an area blow reaching both, a single-target blow one; full while either stands, so no mine goes in and a drone's half leaves none while its twin stands ("Keep it simple, a half is treated as occupied"); one falling leaves the other the slot alone, and with both gone it is a plain slot ("The split state only exists for the units that created it"). Claude's leans he agreed (26 October): a Swap moves the pair together, a mine waiting in the slot goes off on them as on one (a single-target shot the left half, an area shot both), a web on the left half catches its twin, the left acts first. The twin slot is drawn as the slot's two halves, each half its own view at its pixels' whole scale, so a half is drawn smaller than a whole foe on the phone (his read), and a half alone keeps its own half of the slot until both are gone (Ryan, 2 November: “a half unit should not take the full unit slot when it's other half dies, only when both are vacated does it return to a full size slot”). The halves' round is over as they step up (Ryan: "Halves, or any summons, shouldn't attack the round they're spawned, no"): no intent, no Lure or Disrupt to turn, a stun taking their next action, striking from the next round, so a kill never adds blows to the round it was made in. And "we need to make it clear when something joins the pool": a foe joining the reinforcements (a half with no room, a summon, a foe getting back up) flies off its pack into its rank's count in the gutter, which goes up with a burst as it lands (RIFTGATE_POOL_DEBUG=1 keeps it going). Ryan, 26 October: a summon "should take a slot if available, else go to the pool" (the nearest its summoner's column; "ideally it would take a slot immediate always"), and a foe getting back up does so "in-place, immediately, just like the gel splitting. It makes clearing that column harder, which is a good thing", its round over too. A summoner needs no condition to summon (Ryan, 26 October: "Just a lower weight and the guarantee that they can't do it twice in a row is enough. They should be a priority target, not left because you know they won't summon till the pool gets low"): the Ghost's Raise weighs a special's 1 against its bolt's 2 and never comes twice running, and the condition that counted the pool left the engine with it. An area weapon in hand brackets a twin slot's pair as one slot ("show both halves selected as if they were a single slot"), whichever half it lands on behind the scenes. A half stepping up sets off a mine in its slot, as any way in but the reinforcement step's does, so a Syrinx Drone on a Gel mines its own half. Exit: the Meadow's fights read in play, the Gel as a wall that volume or kill order answers.

Decided

A tier's rank and file are recolours and their own units: a repaint, a name and a bigger number, with a twist where one is marked.

Ryan, 28 October: the roster fills by tier with placeholder monsters or classic palette swaps "so that ramp balance can begin", ideally with more threatening mechanics as well as more stats "so the game isn't just a numbers race, but for now a numbers race is kind of what we need"; a recolour by hue is fine "as long as the colour is sufficiently different and they have a modified name or prefix or something", and no other marker. So a recolour is its base's sprite shifted to a hue the three colourblind simulations keep apart from the base's, lightness with it, named by a prefix or a word of the ladder's (Gel, Bubble Gel, Metal Gel), at the tier's numbers (section 3's row), and carries one twist from the mechanics the engine already runs where Ryan marks one (a rider, an aim, a condition, a special, a countdown, a last act), none where he does not; its tier is content's tier, and since 30 October the compositions are the lists (section 5's pack row), each dealt at the tiers it names, the spawn lists by tier derived from them for the harness, the runners and a boss's escort. Built 28 October: twenty-six recolours, two on each base type, their sprites by tools/recolour.py. A placeholder monster is an entry with no art, drawn as the views' placeholder shape, for a role a domain lacks. Retired: the recolour tier held open against section 3's rule, which is an aspiration now.

Lean

The domains' levers, and their identities sharpening by tier.

Leans by each domain's fantasy, never exclusions: a boss anywhere may carry any, and a guest's members stand in a formation from tier two (section 3's row) so no hard counter steamrolls a domain (Ryan, 28 October). The Waste purges, theft of buffs beside its theft of rerolls and entries, and sharpens by breach on more of its members, bigger fixed blows and a mirage's Barrier. The Marsh cleanses, "debuffs are hard here": its healer's cure and cleanse, reraise, regeneration, a sanctuary's Ward and a delayed heal, hard to keep down and hard to mark. The Meadow silences, the shaman the commander, and sharpens by count, the swarm's add and a wall's armour, numbers that split and swarm. The Peak burns, and sharpens by the telegraphed spike, primed claws and countdowns. HP is the general ramp's number and the damage's shape the identity's (section 3's row); the ladder of units that carries each, and the enemy's buffs, are git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md's.

Decided

The leaders are the party: heroes on a quest, played straight.

In line, stepping forward to strike, never mugging. When a domain's Anathema fills, the commander is the dungeon's boss and its hero comes, in its rank beside its wildlife.

Decided

The party is dealt at the run's start.

One hero to each domain from its bench, shown on a party card (four silhouettes and job names); each Anathema bar names its hero. The deal keeps two front jobs and two back; no job rolls (only the player's dice do). It draws on a stream of the run's seed of its own, so the rest of the run rolls as it would without it (sim/campaign.gd); the party card waits on the mission map.

Decided

The benches.

The Meadow: Warrior, Martial Artist, Tamer. The Waste: Thief, Lancer, Merchant, Gunslinger. The Marsh: the Cleric, the Augur, Rune-bearer. The Peak: Black Mage, Summoner, Mage, Sorcerer, Dragon Heir. Any domain: Blue Mage, Psychic, Dancer. The first two of each are built; the Marsh's Necromancer, the weakest homage, was dropped for the Augur (16 October). Robots stay off (or one late wildcard); flying heroes are hunters with reach. What each brings, in a line (names are working names): the Martial Artist two strikes and a counter; the Tamer a back-rank commander calling the wildlife up every round; the Lancer a Jump announced this round that lands next, breaching; the Merchant blows that take salvage off the tally; the Gunslinger a line blow into our back rank; the Augur (Clair Obscur's Sciel by archetype, Ryan, 16 October; a working name, the omen-reader's job) an Omen named at one machine that lands two rounds on, and below half every omen due on every machine at once, the countdown and the every-machine blow on one move; the Rune-bearer an execute on a machine below a quarter of its hull; the Summoner its summons, each a move rather than a companion, from the Norse world its rune circle opens on (Ryan, 15 October: names not the series' own, full Norse): Skaði's frost and Surtr's fire in its final, in Shiva's and Ifrit's places, and Fáfnir its promoted form's, in Bahamut's (his suggestion); the Mage a second caster's kit; the Sorcerer shadow for fire (corrupt-all and heat-all on one hero); the Dragon Heir the pair in one body, massive below half; the Blue Mage learns the components used against it; the Psychic the old die attacks in a child's hands; the Dancer another action for the wildlife. Hero-plus-beast pairs (a summoner's esper, a dragoon's dragon): the hero human-sized in the back as the brain, the companion massive in front as the hitter, and killing the hero first makes the companion dumb; a pattern for a later hero, not a rule. The heroes' tropes are to come from beyond Final Fantasy (Ryan, 15 October: the heroes need to focus on tropes that are not the series'), balancing the series out a little and never purging it (Ryan, 16 October): the Lancer stays the dragoon, way too iconic to lose; the Warrior stays the plain fighter, Dragon Quest's hero being ambiguous; and the Thief takes Persona 5's Joker under its plain name, for the extra tongue in cheek. The rest is proposed in docs/heroes_proposal.md. The Cleric is the homage to every white mage and priest, the Priest folded into it (Ryan: their kits would be much alike; the Cleric his pick, the genre's generic word), and the Martial Artist stays for now.

Decided

The Cleric carries Dia, Cure and Holy; promoted, it readies its allies.

Ryan, 15 October, of the five moves written for it. Cure is the question a Marsh build must answer, reach to the healer. Reraise went from its final, since getting back up is the Marsh's dead's (the Zombie Knight carries the same move, the Vampire Lord gets up once), and came back as its promoted form's one move more at the finale, cast on its allies, the other party members, never itself (Ryan, 16 October: appropriately threatening): a healer that readies the party to get back up is the finale's own escalation. Its first pick is a party member, the heaviest hitter among them. Protect left its kit.

Decided

A hero is human-sized in its final and massive at the finale.

In its final, a chibi party sprite: fighters in front, mages behind the wildlife, so killing the healer is a reach problem. At the finale its promoted form (the class change) is its own entry, in the boss register: what it holds of its own is what the class change adds, its size (2), its armour and one move more, and the rest is its hero's, its name included (Ryan, 21 October: the same names, flagged if they feel thin), since section 3 wants a new foe, never an old one with a bigger number. A job costs two drawings. Its one move more: the Cleric's Reraise and the Summoner's Fáfnir, whose mechanics are open; the other six are open (section 12). Its art is its hero's until the boss register's is cut. On the map a hero only ever commands the boss unit, which patrols a hex a turn whoever commands it, so a hero carries no map move and no hunt of its own (Ryan, 28 October, of the Thief's: “it's a boss so it doesn't need it”); a hero that walks the map would bring them back.

Decided

The finale is the dealt party, promoted, at the capital.

The classic four (Knight, Ninja, White Wizard, Black Wizard). A hero beaten in its final leaves its column to boss-tier wildlife of the domains still standing. The Convergence is the party's level, and its last tier wears the final dungeon's cosmic register, home of the Lovecraftian dial. The stock pass's bands read it a wall for every rung on a team of three (docs/measurements.md). It stays one for a stock trio, since it is fought at a run's end by a squad of four with a run's kit, and it is read once the campaign runner fields built teams (Ryan, 23 October, agreeing with Claude's lean; the runner's player fits nothing it finds today, section 12's campaign question). It reopens if a built squad of four is walled there too.

Decided

Three bosses beaten set a domain's final: every rift it leads is its final.

The third boss beaten turns every standing city's rift the domain leads final at once, its hero the boss the mission select shows, and every rift it leads after is dealt final (Ryan, 26 October: "If the final is set, then all open rifts should change to show the final as the boss in the mission select"; built that day), so no rift opened before then brings a plain boss after. Four finals must be impossible, so there is always a choice. A final counts only when the hero dies and its fight is won: the Thief's Flee stays, its price shown before the fight, and the catch pays (kill it first, or Anathema stays full). The battle is won when the hero's fight is won, its escort down with it, and a team wrecked after the hero falls has lost it (Ryan, 27 October, closing the mechanics audit's 26 the engine's way: the whole escort must die, since a final's hero is the smallest boss and dropping the escort would make the easiest fight easier still; the text that said "whatever is left of its escort" was written while the fight could still be left after the kill). His reason: "it removes the requirement for any boss to be a super tank to be difficult. A buffer or healer like the white mage or whomever now becomes a real threat if you have to also take out its entire escort. And the player must be careful to not rope in other packs when engaging", the packs whose rings cover the boss joining its fight as section 4's boss row has it. Each domain needs three distinct bosses before its hero, drawn without repeats, from the genre's iconic bosses (the tiers row below); the engine already draws from a list, so the slate lands as content alone. The slate's twelve landed 30 October as placeholder kits, three a domain (the tiers row below), and the stand-ins went where Ryan put them (24 October; 30 October: “they are rank and file after all”): the Treant, the Sandworm and the Magma Elemental into their domains' formations at tiers one and two, two moves each as the rank and file carry at tier one, and the Bone Wyvern into the Marsh's elites.

Decided

A boss scales to the battle's tier: its numbers and its escort.

Ryan, 25 October: "I think we need a way of scaling bosses to tier. We don't need to add moves to start, just scaling stats and upgrading the escort. Else we'll end up designing ourselves into a corner, unable to change how many battles in a tier, how fast anathema rises, general scaling, and so on." On 26 October, after hearing section 3's row against it: "Scaling bosses doesn't feel nearly as bad to me and seems like a fine compromise for the sake of run diversity" (the row now carries the exception), and "Make sure that boss escorts are composed of units from the tier. I expect they will be hand tailored at some point though." Built for a domain's boss, a final's hero and the finale's line alike: their HP and attack by the tier, and more escort, drawn from the domain's spawn list at the tier (the lists by tier since 28 October, the highest tier's under it standing in while it has none; never massive, the columns being few), a named escort kept for the hand-tailored ones to come; every number a placeholder. A run keeps its bosses unique where the content allows ("which we should try to"). The ramp's share waits on the ramp (the curve's form). Retired: the slate tiered, each domain's three bosses one a tier (Claude's lean), which repeats a boss when a domain is fought twice at a tier, leaves the finals untiered, and would hold a final for the last tier against the spawn flow ("you'd have to wait until tier 3 with full anathema? Doesn't make sense"). What reopens it: a boss that reads as the same fight at every tier, whose next levers are riders on its blows or extra attacks (Ryan, 26 October), or escorts that want hand-tailoring, each boss's own in content. An escort composition at a tier, when one is written, is the boss's hand-tailored escort in place of the tier's units round robin (the hook, 30 October); none is written.

Decided

Anathema is the Rupture meter's weighted tally.

Each kill by its menace, never capped, every battle however it ended (built 30 October, section 3's tally row); the count of three bosses still decides the final, judged against the tally and the game's length when Ryan reads both.

Decided

Every foe move wears the genre's name where it has one.

Protect, Shell, Cure, Fire, Fira, Flare, Meteor, Holy, Steal, Mug, Flee, Stone, Self-Destruct, announced on the plaque. The engine's words and the player's components keep ours.

Decided

One rule set for foes.

Each has an aim. Rank and file carry one or two moves at tier one and up to three at tiers two and three, the twist the third (Ryan, 28 October, on the ladder's move counts: a numbers race with a twist is the ladder's point, so the Antlion Queen's swallow, the Spectre's and the Wraith's hallow, the Death Knight's grave guard and the Wyvern's dive stay, and the Red Dragon trims to three, its Stoke the one to go); a boss or hero three, the third a signature opening below half. A special never fires twice running, replaces the blow, and is an engine effect never keyed to one foe. Ryan, 24 October, of these and the move counts: "The rest is fine." A condition reads the foe's own side (below half, hurt, its kin standing, its queue, one of its own hurt or not readied) or what its move needs (a machine carrying a buff, for a silence): it keeps a move from doing nothing or saves it for its moment, and a move without one fires on its weight. None gates a threat on the player's state (Ryan, 24 October: every special needing a condition "was never a rule I made, nor does it even make sense at all to have", and "I don't want to overcomplicate the enemies"). Retired: the gates that read the player's state (a machine at a heat, a machine showing a weapon, a reroll left), which held back the very threats they gated, the Drake's Stoke never in a first round or against a cold team, the Black Mage's Fira all but silent and the reroll gate never closing (docs/measurements.md, 24 October). The enemy mitigates by armour or recovery, never by a shield of its own, and every foe on the field can be reached by a die the player has, every round it stands there; a foe that leaves the field (a flee) says so on the plaque before it goes, the test for the Thief's Flee and the Needler's Scatter, both planned (the to-do); a move with a countdown (the Lancer's Jump) gathers on the field, reachable, its warning shown and its blow landing the round its count runs out (15 October). Its interrupts attack the die, and its front rank is the lure that reach answers.

Decided

The roster is a working set.

The foes get their fresh start (a hunter foe, roles, formations) in a session of their own, proposed in chat first. Ryan, 13 October: the compositions and the engagement rules together set the fights' shape (section 4's pacing row), so the fresh start is upstream of the area keywords, the reach premium and any chassis that reads a rank.

Decided

The roster comes in tiers, and size, rank and complexity are apart from the tier.

Ryan, 15 October, his terms sharpened 23 October: an elite or a boss may be any size, the rank and file may be massive and stand in either rank, and a front-rank foe need not be fodder. The rank and file are the genre's tropes (goblins, skeletons, ghosts, drakes, slimes, wolves), simple and advanced. An elite is a spawn of one or more unique packs outside the usual spawn list, a mini-boss tier (Ryan, 23 October): a visible curveball that demands the player adapt, by a mechanic on the map (a dangerous hunter, a fast blitz on the gate, a sudden swarm, an aura or a battlefield effect such as shields that stop recharging or terrain changed) or by being a harder fight that threatens the gate while it holds the team, which is what a tier-one elite looks like. It is a miniboss event in the middle of a battle, guaranteed rather than one of the random spawns, to break up the flow, add depth and drain the player's resources with a high-pressure situation every battle is sure to hold (Ryan, 24 October). Early battles bring about one, later ones more, and an elite is worth a larger chunk of the Rupture meter and Anathema than a pack. The bosses, three a domain, are a who's who of iconic bosses from across the whole genre, and of its signature monsters iconic enough to earn the place (the Tonberry everyone hates; the Behemoth for sure; the Adamantoise probably); no story's final boss (no Sephiroth, no Paintress, no Lavos). The finals are the heroes, homages to the genre's heroes, spread beyond Final Fantasy. A massive foe of any tier fills both rank slots of a column.
