The four built massive foes (Ryan, 23 and 24 October): the Treant, the Magma Elemental (Ryan: a golem implies a master) and the Sandworm are rank and file, the Treant a rank-and-file trope not special enough for a mini-boss; the Bone Wyvern is an elite unless a better setup comes. A beefed-up alpha or a named homage of the Treant or the Sandworm fits the harder-fight elite, under a name of ours (section 1): the Elder Treant, and the Leshy, which may be a foe of its own for diversity; the king sandworm is the Old Man of the Desert, Dune's own words for its worm but plain words, which the homage rule lets stay (Ryan, 24 October). A boss's mechanics stay inside the skirmish until there is a good reason to let one bleed onto the battlefield (Ryan, 24 October: it still stands, for now); an elite's may work on the map by definition, the Reaper's as a deadly hunter (Ryan, 24 October). The slate is docs/bosses_proposal.md, its twelve landed 30 October as runnable placeholder kits, seven on Ryan's word (“build what can be built, and specify exactly what you need to flesh the rest out, including any holes”) and five on his yes (“Yes to all waiting bosses”), each without a picked render drawn as a rough placeholder, a reminder (“It can be a comically rough entry”): every boss he marked in (Rotbloom, Floraison, the Fallen Weapon, the Matador, the Behemoth), the Cinder Tortoise (the Adamantoise, his probably and then yes), Gilgamesh on Claude's lean (a boss or a domain-agnostic elite is open, section 12), and the King Gel, the Pilgrim, the Phantom Train, the Vampire Lord (Neclord by archetype) and the Obsidian Tyrant with its rider (Black Tyrano's) on Claude's kits; each carries three moves, the third opening on a condition or following another, and a point of armour or two (the set of 2 October). A massive foe may stand in a spawn list (24 October), so the three stand in their domains' formations now the slate has taken their boss slots. Built 30 October: the elites' moment is a Fracture (the pacing row; Claude's lean, agreed), so the event is guaranteed and every team meets it at the same point of progress however fast it plays; a domain's elites are compositions led by a foe flagged elite, spawned at the rift in content order, each once a battle; the first five land as runnable placeholder kits for Ryan's marks (the goblin shaman's warband, the Elder Treant's grove, the Leshy's hunt, the Old Man of the Desert's rising, the Reaper alone), their numbers their own and never scaled by the tier; the Bone Wyvern joined the Marsh's elites at tiers two and three once the slate took its boss slot; the Peak's elite and a Waste hunter wait on his marks. The quick campaign's elite slots take them in turn.

Decided

The boss mechanics run inside the skirmish.

For the bosses' slate, each an engine effect any foe may carry (the one rule set row), never keyed to one boss: a countdown (a move gathers, its warning chip counting, and lands that many rounds later; Suppress holds it, Disrupt breaks it off); a boss in parts (Ryan: parts that regrow, or stand, until the core is killed, so the player chooses what to focus: the core is Guarded while all its parts stand, or any, a fallen part may grow back, and the core's fall takes its parts); a last act (a blow the moment a foe falls, before anything else and even as the fight is won, so the shield goes up before the kill); foes helping foes (a cure on the most hurt of its own, armour on every foe through the next round). Built 30 October with the slate's first seven: a follow-up (a move names the move its foe announces next round once it lands, so a warning comes a round ahead, the Matador's cape before its flurry and the Cinder Tortoise's shell before its quake; a Suppress or a Disrupt on the first breaks the pair, never by the roll); a steal (Gilgamesh's: the entry a machine shows is switched off and taken, and swung back next round at its own number, a rolled one at its middle, a fake for 1 when it was no weapon, so what the die shows is a decision when he comes for it); and a swallow (Floraison's: a machine out of the fight for two rounds or until the swallower falls, never the last machine in the fight). Built with the last five: a merge (the King Gel's: every Gel on the field taken into it, regrowing what they had left and lifting its next move a point for each, so thinning the Gels first is the answer; its court is its own escort, an escort that names its boss); a drain (the Vampire Lord's: a blow that regrows what it took from the hull, so a shield starves it, since one of shield and hull walled the slowest trio whatever its HP, the shield no answer to the heal; no Ward turns it away since it lays nothing on the machine, Burning its race, as section 2's burn never stops healing); a grudge (the Pilgrim's Karma: a point heavier for every foe the team has killed this fight, so kill order turns over); a condition that reads a fallen part (the Obsidian Tyrant's countdown of five to a firestorm on every machine, opening once its rider falls); and a move used once a fight (the Vampire Lord readies its rise once, where the Zombie Knight's line may ready again). The swallow is hard control on the team, the first: answered before it lands by a Ward, a Lure, a Suppress or a Disrupt, and after it by the kill alone; what reopens it is a swallow that reads as helplessness in play (section 2), the first lever a blow's worth of damage on the swallower freeing its machine. The first last act in content is the Behemoth's Meteor, the first bosses in parts the Fallen Weapon, its two eyes Guarding its core, and the Obsidian Tyrant, its rider on its back. None leaves the skirmish (the tiers row). The greedy bot values each; every weight is a placeholder for the runner, and the bot values no swallow, since it prevents no damage, so a boss cell reads the bot there.

Open

Foe roles.

A rank is a foe's only role until Ryan proposes a system.

Pinned

The genre's superboss.

If one follows the party through, it is an optional fight past the Rupture, never the finale.

10

Meta and resources

What a run earns and spends, and what outlives it. The numbers in sim/campaign.gd are placeholders until the economy pass.

Decided

Two currencies, split by what earns them.

Salvage (materials), from kills and loot, makes things. Favour (the Council's fee), from won battles docked for gate damage, buys services and stock. A loot detour earns salvage but risks Favour. A resource per domain would let the map solve itself, so there is none.

Decided

A kill is a kill.

Every foe killed pays salvage however its fight ends, tallied only at the battle's end, so there is no count to farm against. Kills are to be weighted by danger, like the Rupture meter. Hit-and-run farming is accepted: a withdrawal gives the enemy its hit, the survivors return whole, and the fight cost a map action. Weighted by menace since 30 October (two salvage a point): the reinforcements pay a trickle and the rift's bounty, the field at the start, the elites and the boss, is where the salvage is, one of the three prices on a stall (section 4's stall row).

Decided

Loot lies on the map.

Wrecks and alien remains, common, seeded with the map, claimed by ending a move on one. Each holds salvage plus components or, in the rarest kind, a chance at a blueprint (modules too, never a research token). A tap shows its kind and likely contents, never the contents: the decision is the detour. Not built.

Open

The loot's shown names and density.

Working names Derelict, Carcass, Relic; never "chest".

Decided

Only a blueprint builds.

For salvage, without limit; any other component is one copy. No chassis blueprints (unlimited machines flatten the roster), and a locked component is never a blueprint.

Decided

Tiers are depth, and a maker never maps to a tier, with one exception.

A chassis's tier is complexity and a component's is power (below). Any maker may field a tier three. The one exception, a chassis rule only (Ryan, 13 October): the Arsenal fields no tier-one chassis and makes no basics, because it is the Council's trickle (section 1), and it is never strictly better, or it becomes the endgame chase; its tier-one components exist, and the Nemesis stocks them under "a chassis stocks only below its own tier" with no rule changed. A run starts with three tier-one machines, four squad slots and the basics' blueprints (tier zero).

Decided

A chassis's tier is complexity, a component's power; modules are flat.

Ryan, 12 October, agreeing Claude's lean: a chassis's tier is a sharper passive and more locked pieces, a higher ceiling and a lower floor, never more power, so the starting machines stay viable all run; with unlocked components free to move, a new chassis is a new sandbox if it is compelling enough. A component's tier is power: blueprints are the growth curve and the basics are meant to be replaced. Modules are flat, their worth in fit and their count capped by slots. Blueprints of tiers one to three are found, or their components come on a bought chassis of tier two or three. A tier-one machine's stock is basics beside its locked piece, Slay the Spire's opening (Strikes and Defends beside a card of its own); most tier ones lock one, and a tier one shows its identity with basics alone, with a locked piece or without (Ryan, 15 October, keeping the roster analysis's test). Ryan, later on 12 October, agreeing Claude's proposal: a component carries its tier in its entry, and a chassis stocks only below its own tier (the validator holds it), so tier one needs no special case and a tier-three component is only ever found, free to be a real heavy hitter or a game changer. Tier three holds the most unique effects and the most raw power, tier two most of the synergy pieces, so no build has to farm tier three for its capstone. Ryan, 24 October: tier three is for "highlight, chase pieces", and four machines in all tier-three gear is not "a healthy endgame", so the run deals it as a chance (by the rift's tier, in git show 85e28fd:docs/curve_proposal.md). A bought chassis brings one copy of each component it stocks, free upper-tier pieces to play with, never their blueprints (owning a component never teaches building it, 7 October; only a blueprint builds, above).

Open

Modules as a priced stat bundle and a priced rule?

Ryan, 12 October: what if a module were a random stat bundle and a random rule, each priced? Claude: possible, and the prices (below) are the tool. Generated to one budget, a rule's price taken from it and the rest spent on stats, no module is bigger than another, only shaped differently, so an offer of three asks which fits rather than which is best, the decision rolled stats lack. The holes: a rule's price is an average over machines (the Booster is worth nothing on a flat kit and a great deal on a rolled one), so fit decides, which is the point; the generator must not pair a rule with the stat that cancels its cost; generated modules need names; and a module that is a single item, never a blueprint, keeps a lucky shape from being farmed. A middle way: the catalogue's rules, authored and named, each with a stat line generated to the budget. Discuss first.

Open

More turnover in the roster.

Ryan, 12 October: making machines easier to scrap would turn the roster over. The holes: turnover by punishment costs the player a favourite; turnover by attraction (offers worth taking) costs nothing. Wrecks are rare while a team of three wins nearly every fight, so the foes' damage against the team's durability is the open axis that sets the wreck rate; the time a wreck sits out (one turn) is the other lever. Permanent loss stays refused (wrecks are recoverable). Discuss first.

Decided

Every stat and passive has a price, measured.

Ryan, 11 October: the way into the module economy, and the same for components. The method: every machine alone, greedy, each bump given to every machine against the same run without it, the mean change in win rate read in hull; the runs are in docs/measurements.md, read as bands. What the first prices said, which the design leans on: armour is the strongest line a module can print and flattens past a point; a shield line is worth less than its number; heat and cooling lines are filler except on a hot machine; a rule is worth what its kit makes it; a passive ranges from nothing alone (the cars', the Badger's, the Puffin's, which work on the map or on allies) to the Kite's, worth a chassis; a module's tier tracks its worth no better than a chassis's. Caveats: one greedy bot, a win rate that saturates, and a foe mix of many small blows that flatters armour. Components by knock-out runs, then tier ladders (the to-do).

Decided

Offers, never trees.

After a won battle the host maker offers three things, one of each kind, a chassis, a component and a module, all from its catalogue, and one is taken free (Ryan, 13 October; the component a single copy, never a blueprint, confirmed 22 October), so a machine is on the table after every win against better kit; the boss drops a research token that unpacks into a choice of three blueprints of its domain's theme at the Convergence's tier, so choosing a front chooses the build path, tier-three blueprints come from late bosses, and host-maker offers shape the roster by the fronts chosen, on purpose. Blueprints come from the tokens, from a rare and dear one on the shelf, and from the rarest loot (the loot row above); Salvage builds copies of owned blueprints. Procurement sells components and chassis for Favour, refreshed each turn, a chassis on the shelf more often than not, since a maker whose city is never fought at reaches the player no other way; the capital sells the Arsenal's trickle, gated by the Convergence; a fallen city's maker leaves the shelf. Not built.

Decided

Components are never modified.

So a destroyed one stings once. The chassis is what is tuned, by modules kept through wrecks; its passive is never swapped.

Decided

Wrecks are recoverable, and the loadout is the cost.

Permadeath is refused, so no loss ends a campaign early.

Decided

Between campaigns the pool carries, never power.

A blueprint found once may be offered again but must be found again. Only the pool, the codex, difficulty tiers (Slay the Spire's ascension, each clearable from a fresh start), one Sponsorship piece and livery carry.

Decided

Sponsorship.

At a campaign's end, won or lost, each maker whose measure the player topped offers one rare component for the next run's kit, with a plain notice in its voice; the player takes one. The measures are relative and never shown live: Kestrel hexes moved; Minerva damage taken on shield and armour; Wainwright & Sutler dice spent on entries that deal damage (a vent that blasts counts: Ryan, 22 October, "it should count"); Aphelion dice spent on entries that deal none. The Arsenal's notice comes only to a campaign that reached the Convergence's top tier. The tallies are built; the offer at the run's end waits on the catalogues.

Decided

Livery is unlocked for good, free on any machine.

Sponsorship unlocks colours; no rule reads paint.

Recommended

Each maker the player topped unlocks one colour, its offer taken or not.

Decided

The loop aims at fifteen to twenty-five real decisions over twelve battles, none a chore.

Open

Every number.

The economy pass (docs/economy_pass.md), with the runner's spending policy built first. If components lost per won battle pass about one, the destruction threshold moves, not the rule.

11

Technical plan

How the game is built, measured and kept honest. README.md has every command.

The engine

Decided

Godot 4.8 in GDScript, exported to Android in portrait.

Hexes are axial (cube implied), pointy-top. The project runs 4.8-beta2 until 4.8 ships, for the fix of the Compatibility renderer's MSAA leak on Android (Ryan, 30 October: “Let's update Godot so we can get the msaa fix”; the phone's rows below), and follows Godot's releases: a session checks for a newer build every two weeks and brings it to Ryan with what it changes here, the move his call (30 October: “check for godot updates periodically”; the fortnight Claude's).

Decided

The simulation is pure.

sim/ never touches scenes, runs headless and draws every random number from a seeded generator, so rules test headless and runs reproduce.

Decided

Numbers are questions, never stored totals.

Every stat, damage figure and component property is read through sim/effects.gd, in one fixed order, and nothing writes to a stat or a component. Every buff, debuff, reward, passive and module is a modifier with a source and a scope (the order and the scopes are in docs/rules.md).

Decided

Content is data the build validates.

sim/content_db.gd checks content/*.json in the tests, so a broken rule fails the build. Internal text lives under design; a draft the engine cannot run carries "status": "planned"; runtime defaults come from content, never a hardcoded id; a new term enters the vocabulary first.

Decided

A battle saves after every change and resumes where it stood.

A phone kills apps unasked. sim/save.gd keeps the map by its seed, every unit and live modifier, the turn and both random streams, so a resumed fight plays on blow for blow; another version's file loads nothing; scene runs and tests never write. The run and the profile save too.

Balance

Decided

Balance is measured by a ladder of bots and read as bands.

Random play (never rerolls, spends anywhere legal) beating a pack means too easy; greedy play (the best one-round trade on the player's own forecast) losing means a wall; the rungs landing together mark a cell with no decision; personas sit on top; two more rungs read the coordination gap, blind play below the greedy and the planner above it (the next row). tools/balance.gd runs every chassis alone, the starting team and each maker's first three against every pack, boss and domain into docs/balance.md. It is read by stage (Ryan, 23 October): which foes and compositions are harder for an early stock trio than for an endgame four, never how hard the roster is as a whole; the starter is a trio the tender deals, stocked, and the endgame four is fitted from pieces and modules up to tier two, tier three being powerful and rare by design, until the components' power curve is known. Pick share is the component signal. Play tests decide feel; no win rate is chased to a decimal. What a run can say (13 October): one cell near 50% carries a band of about 18 points at 30 seeds and 7 at 200, a row mean over twenty packs about 4, so a cell is read at 200 seeds or as a band, a quick run is a direction and a row that cites one says so, and every run a decision cites goes in docs/measurements.md, dated with its seeds, where the row keeps the reason and the exit. The greedy bot values only what it knows: a verdict on a mechanic it cannot value (a sentence, a doubling, a turn of the ranks, a wound) is a verdict on the bot, and says so.

Decided

Two rungs read the coordination gap: blind play, and a planner that looks two rounds ahead.

The greedy rung is a one-round bot that sets up only where the setup pays within the round, so the gap could not open on it (docs/measurements.md). Blind play is the tests' own (section 2: "a team that spends each die on the nearest foe"): the greedy's rerolls, then each weapon and debuff on the nearest foe in reach, each shield, vent and buff on its own machine, a die held only where its heat or its Volatile bite would wreck the machine, the gauge being in view. The planner plays the greedy's game and, at every spend with more than one worth making, plays its best few out on copies of the fight through the next round and its enemy turn, the greedy rung playing the same seeded futures for every candidate, and keeps the spend whose fight is then worth most, the greedy's pick on a tie (sim/policy.gd). So it values a mechanic the greedy cannot price by what two rounds of play make of it, and nothing past two rounds. It costs about four greedy fights a fight, so it runs on the team rows. Its rollouts once priced a Shock at the team's average blow, so a Shock debuff read as a bad roll and was rerolled away; a blow's Shock is now worth the biggest blow an ally still to act shows on that foe (23 October). What reopens it: a changed share near nothing where setups should pay, or a mechanic slower than two rounds (a boss's), which a longer horizon or more futures would read at more cost.

Decided

A component is balanced against the components of its tier; a chassis alone is a diagnostic.

Ryan, 12 October: chassis serve different roles, so a solo win rate is no main indicator, and a weapon is never balanced because a lone Prairie Dog or Kite loses four on one, a fight it should lose or win only at a real cost in hull. The measures that matter: a component against its tier's others in the same hardpoint of the same machines (its value and its pick share), the team rows, and whole runs. One machine that warps its kit never dictates the whole roster of components (Ryan, 13 October, of the gunship).

Decided

A piece is priced by its effectiveness, read in vanilla points; heat is priced up from the heatless vanilla; a rolled gun is its mean.

Ryan, 28 October, on the pricing run (docs/pricing_proposal.md; its runs in docs/measurements.md, 27 and 28 October): a keyword carries a price as a number does, so "the real metric is average EFFECTIVENESS. A tier 2 shield that shields only 4 but grants 4 overcharge is a strong component. So is a 1 damage splash 1 weapon that applies 4 flak" ("It's not a precise science, we have to best guess"). The baseline is the vanilla at each tier, a plain number with no keyword, as a card game prices its vanilla creatures by cost; vanillas are real pieces in the pool ("Sometimes you just need a big stick to make the build reliable") and sit low on the scale ("otherwise the game flattens to nothing"), the floor of their tier, every keyword piece and every hot piece above it by what it offers and what it pays. Heat is priced from the heatless vanilla up: "A component is allowed to have higher effectiveness for higher heat", and what a point of heat buys in effectiveness is estimated empirically, never set as a floor by hand (the proposal's first floors were "entirely arbitrary"). A rolled gun is its mean: across a run the roll flattens to its average, "so effectively, at the asymptote, a rolled gun is just a heatless flat gun anyway. Rolled damage is flavour", believable bullets and explosions, and a cool random gun against a reliable hot one "is perhaps a choice of preference. But statistically, it really isn't"; so a range is priced at its middle, and the heat row (section 5) no longer counts it a price. The measure (the pricing driver's vanilla ladder, tools/pricing_run.py): a flat heatless gun in a bench hardpoint at every number, every piece read in vanilla points, the heatless number that takes the same rounds off the row as the piece does, its points less its own number what its keywords buy or cost there; the same vanilla across the heat classes for the knee where a kit starts paying, the heat price read past it. Open, Claude's caveats of the same day: a rolled gun's thresholds (a kill its top alone makes) are second order, read on the rolled guns' points against their means; heat's price is a knee by kit and not a slope, so it is read where the kit pays, and a cold kit's free ride is the maxima's and the cooling's question; a shield's and a vent's effectiveness needs a bot that spends them (the planner, a persona), which the greedy is not.

Decided

The pricing pass of 28 October: the floors, the heat as measured, the keywords' prices, and the few numbers that moved.

Ryan's go on Claude's leans ("I trust your leans for this pass"), on the vanilla ladder (docs/measurements.md, 28 October; the proposal docs/pricing_proposal.md, retired with this row). The scale: a piece's vanilla points, the heatless flat number that takes the same rounds off a bench's row; on the car a point buys a third of a round up to 6 and a seventh past it (the knee where a blow kills any rank and file outright), the flat Indirect reads as the flat Direct, and the hot bench reads no number at all, only the keywords that reach a second foe. The floors, each tier's heatless vanilla in vanilla points: 2, 3.5, 5 and 7, about half a round a tier on the car, an even ramp in kill time rather than in points; a tier is a band from its floor to the next, its keyword pieces above the floor by what the keyword buys and its hot pieces by what their heat is allowed. Heat as measured: on a cold die heat 1, 2 and 4 cost nothing, heat 6 about a point (the third round's overheat, the heat row's test) and heat 8 two, or a quarter of a big gun's number; on a hot die heat is paid in hull and never rounds, since an overheat burns the excess one for one (section 5); and on a nine-entry die (the Clydesdale bench) a hot piece never reaches its knee, since its slot fires a ninth of the spends, so heat's price scales with how often the piece fires, the die's size a lever beside the maxima. So the warm classes are flavour on today's kits, a cool gun soft cooling on a hot die, and the allowance a hot piece carries over its floor is a design choice the cold die cannot price; the lever that would make heat 4 bite is the maxima (10) or the overheat's cost, section 12's question, never a piece's number. The keywords in vanilla points on the car: Flak 2 two, Burn 2 two and a third, Burn 3 and the Chain nearly three, the Shock one and a half beside strikes, the Breach about one, Penetrate 3 a third there and the piece no vanilla matches on the hot bench; a rolled gun reads a third of a point under its mean and a strikes gun a point under its total, the threshold a kill needs and the armour each strike pays. The fills, on his go: the Overclocked Railgun 10 to 8 (tier one's outlier at 8.8 points against a floor of 3.5, unable to move up a tier since the Buzzard stocks it; Breach and heat 8 kept, 10 and the capstone 12 left for tier three's finds); the Coriolis Accelerator 3 to 4 (4.2 against tier two's floor of 5); tier one's rolled vanillas to a mean of 4 (the Slugthrower, the Howitzer, the Plasma Arquebus and the Shotfire Rack 2 to 6), since at 3 they read as the tier-zero Laser Carbine; the Howitzer's Splash 1 (the chip Ryan named, 0.9 rounds off the car against 0.5 plain; the Meteor Artillery keeps none while its Burn 3 rides every foe struck, a fire on five foes at either number); the Ram Frame's hull +24 to +8 and the Artillery Rangefinder's the same line, read on the Indirect bench first (the Clydesdale's Howitzer slot, the sixth bench: the Frame 0.4 rounds off its row on the Clydesdale's strikes, the Rangefinder 0.1, its back-rank rule reading on the one back-rank foe type a tier-one formation fields, where tier three's field three; the tiered formations are walls for a stock trio (Wainwright & Sutler's row wins 29% of them), so its rule is read on the per-tier ladders with a tier-matched kit). What stays, with its reason: the Arc Fault Cannon's 4, read on Chain's form of the 29th at about 4.8 points, inside tier one's band, and about 6 on the formations (section 5's Chain row; its 6.7 points of the 28th were the kill and repeat's); the Cyclic Cannon and the Blazethrower within a direction's noise of their bands; the Meteor at tier two's top, its heat paying 0.7; tier two's setups, the Probe and the Condenser until the bot's valuation (the to-do); every shield and vent, since no bot spends one on these benches (the planner and a persona that shields and vents before the overheat are the next run's first part). Each landed delta is read again at 200 seeds (docs/measurements.md, the pass's entries). Exits: a floor moves when the tiered formations stretch the curve (the ladders per tier once the fresh start's ladder lands); a keyword's price moves on a bench whose row reads it differently; the hot guns' numbers move when heat bites more.

Decided

The roster is widened so compositions can be read.

Ryan, 13 October: the chassis proposals serve two things, feeling what mechanics are possible and giving the runner enough machines to see what a team of four can be. A support machine is never balanced against a damage machine as like against like; the unit of balance is the composition, and a solo row is a diagnostic (above). If every machine is a solo carry, a battle with four is trivial, so the roster needs machines that are only good together.

Decided

The battle and campaign bots stack on the fight bot as floors, not players.

sim/battle_bot.gd plays a battle by one plain map rule; tools/campaign_run.gd plays whole runs.

Decided

Every number inside a fight is single digits or low tens, derived from the tests and never from the old content.

The old scale (damage at fives, the rest at tens) came from an upgrade system since cut, and its ratios were never designed (Ryan, 21 October: "those hull numbers sound very high to me compared to the damage numbers"; "let's not make any assumptions based on existing content for any number, whether that's heat, damage, armor, shield, or health on both sides of the field"). The magnitudes, held to section 2's kill-time, coordination-gap and wear tests: a blow 1 to 6, big guns to 10, one capstone 12; an amp a point a stack (Armoured, Overcharged, Flak, Burning), which is the floor that keeps blows at 2 to 6 rather than 1 to 3, since two Flak stacks on a blow of 1 would triple it; foe HP 2 to 6 for rank and file, 30 to 40 massive, 12 to 24 heroes, the slate's bosses 48 at tier one, 60 and 72 at tiers two and three (30 October; a core in parts 40, a part 8 to 12, a boss that may rise once 40), where 80 to 100 was first written for them (Ryan's instinct: the big foes want more hits, for the gap to show): at 80 the default team took 7 to 13 rounds against each alone and a stock four 11 to 22 where the kill-time test asks three to five, at 48 the default team 4 to 7 (docs/measurements.md, 30 seeds, a direction), and Ryan left the number to the scaling (30 October: “Whatever you think works best for scaling”), so 48 stands until the kill time read on built fours, the squad the player fields, says otherwise; a foe's blow 2 to 8 (the rank and file's mostly 2 or 3), a massive foe's 7 or 8, a hero's 4 to 10, a boss's 6 to 10; machine hull 20 to 50 by size, 20 the floor so a telegraphed finisher never takes a machine from full (the Kite's paper hull 12, its row's one-off); shield 6 to 16, field recharge 4 to 8 (about half the shield a map turn out of fights, or the field rate would mean nothing), base armour 0 to 2 by role (section 5); heat 1 to 20, the maxima 10 to 14 (the Musk Ox's 8), cooling 0 to 2, a gun 0 to 8, a vent 3 to 8 (the Entropy Condenser's 10 the one capstone vent, paid in hull, as the guns have their 12), with 1 to 30 the fallback if heat proves unbalanceable at twenty steps (Ryan: "1 to 30 is fine as well if heat seems unbalanceable"). Still magnitudes, not steps (13 October): any whole number; a move is a count of hexes (section 4). Ryan, 21 October: "I do like the numbers in general. They are readable to a person. So I think that we should work with this for now." Every converter that crosses heat into another axis (the overheat, the Whip Scorpion's blast, the Afterburner, the Buzzard's regen, the Puffin's growth, the Musk Ox's ammo, Cryo, the Stingray's Suite, a machine's Burning and Scorched, the hot threshold) is read on its own merits at this scale, never carried over by ratio ("Just because we have mechanics that currently rely on converting heat to other values doesn't mean that they're currently doing the right call"). The campaign's gate, salvage and Favour are another economy and keep their numbers for the economy pass. How the numbers are set: the first fills, the old numbers divided unevenly, failed the tests (trash died to a die or two, every team won every pack but the finale, a massive foe took three to twelve rounds), so the landing set them by measured loops against section 2's tests on the levers the brief names (the foes' blows, cooling and the guns' heat, the massive elites' armour and regrow), and Ryan marks the structure, never a hundred numbers (Ryan, 22 October, agreeing); our hull and shield down a quarter and cooling 0 were read and set aside. The runs are in docs/measurements.md, the fills in tools/stock_fills.py (the brief: git show a550c2e:docs/briefs/stock_pass.md). Ryan kept the massive HP at 30 to 40 (23 October, section 2's tests row): lower HP would close the room the gap needs, so the kill time is read on a squad of four and the gap on built teams; the gap not opening on a lone massive foe is section 12's. What reopens it: the balance report's bands, the rounds-to-win gap and Ryan's play.

Open

Foe blows against shields, vents, heat, hull and armour.

The axis that sets a fight's wear and the wreck rate (section 10's turnover row). Ryan, 12 October: our armour may be too high; lower our numbers before raising anything, which the scales row did (above). Lower armour narrows the gap between heavy and light machines; what makes a team's fight hard is how much it fights at once, several packs or a formation, so the axis is read with the foes' fresh start (section 9's working set) and the campaign runner.

The phone

Decided

The phone is the reference glass.

A Pixel 9 on the Compatibility renderer, and the desk draws with the same renderer (Ryan, 29 October: “yes, switch the desk to the phone's renderer”), since the Vulkan renderer it had lit in linear space where the phone's lights in sRGB space, so a lit part's colour on the desk was not the phone's (a dark glow tile the phone multiplies up to its colour showed dull). A lit part holds its colour on that renderer: the light a glow multiplies is eased by the power 1/2.2 first, as the Vulkan renderer decoded a colour before brightening it, so a flare builds in its own colour and nears white only at the very top (Ryan, 30 October, of the two side by side: “I liked the colour-holding flash, since a lot of these are supposed to be looking 'hot' instead of 'bright' and white doesn't really sell that”); the plain multiply had turned every flare past a few times the rest light white, the Kestrel ember's and engines', the Baboon's eye and the Buzzard's charge among them. What reopens it: a glow that wants white. A halo for lit parts is on trial on the Buzzard (Ryan, 30 October, of a halo shell over the other form, a second render of the lit parts alone blurred over the machine: “Sure, let's try it”): round each lit part its glows name, a soft shell grown out along the part's smoothed normals in its own colour at its light, faded in as its flare climbs over its rest, spilling past the machine's outline, the outline pass keeping the soft fringe and every other machine's pixels as they were; Godot's own glow, tried first, cannot tell a lit part from white paint on that renderer and stops at each machine's outline, since every machine is drawn in its own see-through view. It is judged running before it reaches another machine. A screenshot read is reproduced on the desk machine at the phone's layout and density and fixed once for every glass; several "phone bugs" were layout or arithmetic the desk machine hid. tools/phone.sh (a Web build over the LAN) shows layout and touch; tools/android.sh (a debug APK over USB) judges frame rate, outlines and sound.

Decided

Nothing is one device pixel thin, and nothing on the map's overlay grows with the zoom.

The 720x1280 design stretches about 1.5 times on a phone: text is linear and pixel art nearest, the safe bands stay clear, and the map and the machines render at the glass's density, always (the fine map, its option gone: Ryan, 30 October, “set fine map on as the default and remove the option”). The map's grid and the machines' outline keep one width on screen at every zoom, smoothed (Ryan, 30 October: “I don't want the outline to thicken as I zoom in”; both grew with the zoom, and stepped zoomed out): the outline is one unbroken line round each machine, through the wall as before, drawn round the machines' own layer, since the engine's outline grew each of the models' flat faces apart, broke into pieces round a machine and grew with the world. So does every overlay mark (Ryan, the same day: “all UI overlay type items should hold at one width. It will look better”): a line's width, a dash, an inset, a glyph, a plaque, a gauge and a tag keep their zoom-1 size, while what a mark outlines or fills (a hex, a region, a route from hex to hex, a ring sized to its hex) stays on the map. His read on the phone: the whole map “looks way sharper”, the lines growing with the zoom “the biggest culprit”. MSAA runs on the phone as on the desk: the project runs Godot 4.8-beta2, and 4.8 stable once it ships, for the fix of its leak on Android (on a phone's GPU the Compatibility renderer set MSAA up again every frame, godotengine/godot#97967, fixed by #124643; Ryan, 30 October: “Let's update Godot so we can get the msaa fix”); the suite and the renders came out the same on it as on 4.7.2. The anti-aliasing is the player's setting, AA MODE: off, FXAA, MSAA 2x or MSAA 4x, the last the default (Ryan, 30 October: “Can we add an AA mode setting, not just for debug?”), so a weaker phone can trade it for frame rate; in the FXAA mode the map's compositor runs FXAA of its own on the 3D (the battle map's alone: a skirmish draws as with none), and in every mode the machines' textures read their mipmaps, so a machine drawn small never sparkles (Ryan, 30 October: “a lot of aliasing on models and hex grid lines on the phone”); edges come from the fine map, rendered at the glass's density at 60 fps. The traces' creases are the one thing still a device pixel wide at every zoom, and the grain that is left (his read: “its the traces that cause the graininess, with AA on it doesn't look too bad”); they stay. A debug build's TRACES toggle draws the map without the creases, to compare. What reopens it: the phone's frame rate or heat with MSAA or the outline on, or the Pixel's memory climbing with MSAA (AA MODE down, then).

Decided

The map's colours.

The HUD colour is the player's own; violet is the rift's and the enemy's, every foe's mark and the packs' intent alike (a striped two-violet field under the intent, light #c8a4ff and deep #6a30c0; the rings, bands and marks on a deep #2a0e4a edge, never black), the red-red only where a unit is harmed (a stop with no room, the gate's integrity low), never a foe's doing (Ryan, 30 October: “keep purple for enemy activities”). The rings part from the routes by structure, a ring of dashed hex edges against a line with its target; what reopens it is the two failing to part on the phone.

Decided

Under the battle map, a strip.

It holds a held readout or the orders board, never an overlay on the hexes; the log sits off screen behind LOG. The readout reads as the fight's holds do, never as a block of text (Ryan, 26 October: “For everything on the map, it all looks text-block-ish”): a name, its gauges with their numbers, then a line a fact. What reopens it: the strip judged on the phone.

Decided

A battery plan: the weld built, the props' fade and the 3D at 30 not yet.

Weld each machine's meshes by material within each moving group; fade props per hex inside the baked bands; drop only the 3D to 30 fps while nothing in it moves fast (the 2D stays at 60). The phone already holds 60; this saves battery and heat. The weld is built (25 October, game/ui/mech_weld.gd): at load, in the skirmish, the codex and on the map, the parts that move with the same thing (the body, a joint, a weapon's pin) and wear one material become one mesh, and nothing on screen changes (each welded mesh keeps its parts' scale, so an outline grows as it did; a group's trace is its parts' own edges on one mesh; a framing reads the parts' boxes; the importer's levels of detail come along). A part that moves, glows, scrolls or hides on its own needs a joint, a pin or its name in the sidecar's glow_parts (lowforge lists the acts' and gaits' glows and the treads there), or the weld merges it with its neighbours. The fourteen models go from 917 meshes to 521 and a machine's draw calls about halve on both screens, the battle map's whole frame drawing a third fewer (docs/measurements.md; frame times are the phone's to read); RIFTGATE_WELD=off on the desk, or the WELD toggle in a debug build's settings on the phone, draws the parts as authored, to compare.

Open

Phone reads owed.

The sound set, the overlay at one size zoomed in (the outline's width, the plaques and tags at their zoom-1 size), the grid back at its old light, the intent field in motion.

The documents and pages

Decided

Each document has one job.

docs/rules.md states the rules as they are now; this bible records decisions and why; docs/status.md says how things stand; docs/todo.md lists what is next; docs/vocabulary.md is generated from the vocabulary; docs/measurements.md holds every run a decision cites, dated with its seeds, so a bible row keeps the reason and the exit condition and never the run. A rule that changes is rewritten in place in the rules and the bible in one commit; neither keeps a changelog. The status legend at the top says what a Decided row may be and what a Lean is.

Decided

One hangar page.

tools/hangar.html holds the Codex, Content editor, Names and Models tabs over the published content; Ryan's saved edits wait in its database until a session lands them. It is republished with tools/hangar_data.py's map after any content, model or render change.

12

Open questions and next steps

What is built, the questions only Ryan can settle, and what is parked. The order of work is docs/status.md ("Next, in order"), kept at every handoff; the tasks are docs/todo.md. A question lives here and a task there, never both; a question answered in chat lands as a row and leaves this list.

Built

  • Engine and tools: the headless seeded simulation, the validated content pipeline, every number read through sim/effects.gd, save and resume, the Android build at 60 fps, the machines welded at load for the draw calls; the balance runner, the campaign runner, the map survey, the harness and the hangar page.
  • The battle: the round (moves planned and played on EXECUTE, ENGAGE or END TURN, END TURN asking while a machine can still act, orders by tap or drag, an undo in the order things were done, one fight at a time by ring and range, every pack's ring in the enemy's violet, hunters that catch), the boss fought after its reveal, movement in hexes (water the one priced ground, the horde wading it too, walls the flyer's, winged packs crossing, packs passing each other and flyers and the ground passing each other, hunters pouncing at a ring of two and taking the machine nearest a prey sealed off), six map shapes with their walls named, their fords and streets three or four hexes in all, their decoration laid to be read and their relief their own, targeting at random by default and by aim where a foe carries one, never over-committing; packs as compositions, the field at the start and the rift's cycle by the tier and the ramp, Rift Pressure on the ramp's timer with the tears and the seam, the whole wall struck, the Rupture meter with its Fractures, the elites at them and the seal with its stall guard.
  • The skirmish: the vector HUD (gestures, forecasts, feed effects, codex), every passive, keyword, status and trigger not marked planned, the plaque, and the current weapon rules (no crits, rolled or flat, two reaches, the die showing the blow's number), the Paradox meter and Numbed as the only resistance, the overheat at the end of the player's turn, the Badger's hopper, the Nemesis's rage with its roll again shown, the Storm Petrel's shield paying its weapons' heat with the zap, its plate and its regen, the Giant Petrel's bank of rerolls and its three locks, the foes' heat as Burning and Scorched, a rally landing at once and riding each foe's next move, the intents counting the rallies on their way under a pulsing preview chip; the enemy's three verbs (Silence muting keywords, Purge by source, a foe's Cleanse), Primed, Barrier, Ward and Regenerating on the foes, and the player's Purge; a foe's follow-up, steal, swallow, merge, drain and grudge, a move used once a fight, a last act and a boss in parts in content.
  • Content: five makers, twenty-four chassis, 81 components, 17 modules; four domains with forty-three wildlife (seventeen at tier one, the three old stand-ins among them, and the ladder's twenty-six recolours, the compositions by tier, and six elites as placeholder kits), the slate's twelve bosses as placeholder kits, three a domain, and eight heroes with their promoted forms, six foe moves still planned.
  • The campaign's rules, headless only (sim/campaign.gd): cities and gates, fronts, the Convergence, salvage, Favour, the tender, tiers, wrecks and rushes, the party dealt from the benches, its finals and the finale, a boss at its battle's tier, the rift's tier locked and the ramp, salvage by menace and Anathema. No screen shows them yet.
  • The quick campaign (section 3): its run, its screens with the game's first hangar, its save and its runner (sim/quick_run.gd, game/quick/, tools/quick_run.gd).
  • Art and sound: every machine's model by lowforge with joints, idle acts, gaits and size classes but five (the Cassowary, the Cottonmouth, the Whip Scorpion, the Swallowtail and the Musk Ox, which draw the Badger's); the first foe art (eighteen wildlife and eight chibi heroes, picked by Ryan, and the ladder's twenty-six recolours by hue), and rough placeholders for the bosses and parts with no pick (tools/placeholder_art.py); the holographic 3D battlefield and each domain's tileset on its painted ground; six tracks; the effects; Jenny's lines; every maker's machine death.

Questions for Ryan

Where the code and the design part, or the records cannot settle a point.

The rules

  • Promote the built Recommended rows to Decided: no player positioning and the interleaved phase, Rift Pressure, a passed turn as a turn, shields as a timer?
  • The merges across the sides still open (section 6): one word or two for Kindled and Overcharged, the same effect on two sides (Claude's lean: Overcharged, under the convention's many-sources exception, a rally then overcharging its own); Reraise's name of ours under the homage rule, its chip (Getting Back Up) free to stay; Retaliate for the foes' counters (the Martial Artist's Counter, the Salamander's Ember Hide); Purge's carriers and numbers.
  • Whether heat below 6 should bite (section 11's pricing row): the vanilla ladder read heat 1, 2 and 4 free on a cold die with one hot piece, since an overheat burns only the excess, so the warm classes are flavour today and the allowance a hot piece carries over its floor is a design choice; the levers are the maxima, what an overheat costs (the excess one for one today), a soft zone above two thirds of the maximum, or the cooling, never a piece's number. Measured the same evening as a what-if (docs/measurements.md, the heat knee by the maximum): the knee moves a class a step with the maximum, 6 at 12, between 4 and 6 at 10 (heat 4 still free in rounds on a cold die) and 4 at 8, where heat 4 costs half a vanilla point, heat 6 one and a half and heat 8 two and a half; a fleet at 8 would overheat the hot kits every round (Kestrel's row at 128% peak on the tiered formations at its 14), so the bite for the warm classes wants a selective lever. Ryan, 28 October: heat is a core pillar and undervalued as a gameplay element, and his frame is section 2's triangle row (every component priced, the basics too; passive cooling the lever, his lean to cut it; vent payoffs); what stays his to settle: the cooling's cut and what keeps its cooling, each free piece's price (the to-do's heat session bench), whether a shield's price in heat is wanted (a hot machine that cannot shield is the spiral, the wrench or the trap by his call), the soft zone or a harder overheat if the prices and the cooling leave the warm classes free, and heat between fights after the skirmish's bite is right.
  • Heat between fights: zero at every fight's start, as today, or carried from fight to fight and cooled by the field rate out of fights, as shield is, which would make heat a battle resource and sharpen burst against endurance (section 5's heat row)?
  • Flak's clock, reopened by Ryan on 22 October for Flak alone: a stack fading a round, Burning's clock, in place of the halving, since a debuff dies with its foe where Overcharged rides any blow its machine lands and wants the use-it-or-lose-it. The two clocks part only where a team lays two or more a round on one foe, where a stack a round climbs with the feed and strikes multiply what stands; the loops read both alike on today's feeds (docs/measurements.md), so the call is readability alone. Claude's lean: the halving kept, since one clock reads simpler and the ramp lands only on the foes that outlive a round.
  • Where the coordination gap is read: on trios and squads of four fitted with a Shock and Flak it reads about 1.0 to 1.3 against section 2's about 2 (23 October, docs/measurements.md, 30 seeds, a direction), least on a lone massive foe, where a setup pays whoever cashes it, so blind play, spending every die on the one foe, banks it as well; it shows most where a fight has several foes. The gap read on the fights with several foes alone, a lone massive foe being a kill-time read; setups whose worth depends on the blow that cashes them (a Shock under a capstone gun, a lay only the same round's blows read) as the content's lever; or the test's half widened? Claude's lean: the first, read again when the foes' fresh start fields massive foes inside formations, with the second weighed in the makers' sessions.
  • Which stage section 2's tests describe? Claude's lean: every stage, each team held to them against the compositions placed for its stage, so a foe's numbers follow from where it sits on the curve (section 3's curve row), never from one team's results. The massive HP question this replaces retired with Ryan's read by stage (23 October).
  • Numbed per foe (section 5's row): Ryan, 30 October, of by boss: “Maybe it's a per-unit thing?” Claude's lean: by role, two and three on a boss or a massive foe, until a foe wants a number of its own, since a rule reads off the chip where a number per foe is one more to learn.
  • Chips as buffs and debuffs, the word "status" retired; Numbed stays its own kind (section 5).
  • What shown text calls a machine's die, which looks like no die: the problem that kept "face" out of shown text. A session of its own.
  • The enemy's turn under the spend chain: a machine lost, Jenny's line at the landing in place of the kill blast, or after it as now?

Content

  • The Armadillo at tier one (Ryan, 2 November: “armadillo is probably too much of a wall for tier 1. It's very good at its job”). Sealed, its armour is 5 against tier one's rank-and-file blows of 2 or 3, so once its shield is down they take nothing off it: flat armour at or over a tier's blows is immunity at any number, so Lockdown at +2 (armour 4) changes nothing that matters. The runner cannot see it (docs/measurements.md, 2 November, 30 seeds): the greedy bot seals too rarely in a team (the Canopy 6% of the trio's spends), so no what-if on the seal or the base armour moves the Armadillo's trio, and every starter trio walls tier one's rank and file whoever sits in the middle seat (0 to 2% hull lost, the Zombie Knights aside). The bench, each with the rule it would test: a floor of 1 past armour on a blow that reaches the hull, on both sides (armour stays strong at every tier and is never immunity; it reaches every tank and every armoured foe, so the matrix reads it at 200 seeds before it lands); tier one's threats raised in the fresh start's compositions instead (the root being the tier's blows, since every trio walls them; the compositions wait on your marks already); the seal harder to hold at tier one (its heat a turn up, or the shield it spends not kept while sealed: a price, never immunity's cure); the Armadillo moved to tier two, where blows outgrow armour 5, and Minerva given another tier-one front; or accepted (a wall is its job, and tier one's danger is the formations and the clock). Claude's lean: the floor of 1, since it is the root on both sides and the rest treat a symptom; then the compositions for the tier as a whole. Ryan, 2 November: “Just note down the armadillo for now, I suspect tier 2 is smarter but we need more options first”: parked, the move to tier two his suspicion, once there are more options.
  • The Storm Petrel's burn on basics (its brief's hole, here since its engine landed, 30 October): a tier one stocks tier zero, and the hottest basic is the Laser Carbine at heat 2 (the Ankle Mortar 1, the Twin Autoguns 0), so its stock burns 2 shield a spend at most for a zap of 2, and its gauge peaks at a tenth on the runner (docs/measurements.md, its first rows); its big hot gun and mid-heat gun are tier-one finds (the Overclocked Railgun at 8, the Transonic Rifle at 4). Accept it (the burn is the build's; the plate and the lure carry the start, and alone it already stands in the Armadillo's band), price the burn by the spend rather than the heat point (the zap a floor), or let the passive double the heat it burns? The heat session's prices on the basics (a heat point on the Twin Autoguns, the triangle row) move it a point. Claude's lean: accept it until the heat session's prices land, then read its stock again; the burn is the build's by design, and a floor would part the zap from the heat that is its whole read. Ryan, 31 October, of heat paid beside the shield: “the answer is no, the shield is the cost”; none of the three pays both, and the heat reaches the gauge only past an empty shield, as built.
  • Which explosives carry area, and at what number (section 5's area row; Splash N since 28 October): the stock pass's tables gave no shell both shapes, so the landing carried none; Claude's lean, for Ryan to move: the two big shells (the Howitzer and the Meteor Artillery) carry Splash, a chip at their neighbours (1 or 2), the basic Ankle Mortar none, the Incendiary Shells their Burn alone. The basic kit holds no flat Indirect gun (the Rivet Gun and the Laser Carbine are both Direct), where section 7's kit row asks for Direct and Indirect each rolled and flat: a fifth basic gun, or the row reworded? Two questions from the stun discussion: the component pool's size (Ryan, 22 October: unsure of the target; Claude's lean, 50 to 75 unlocked blueprints across tiers one to three against 40 as landed, so one consumer a setup status until the payoffs differ in kind), and Aphelion's slottable control once the Inducer is the Arsenal's: Claude's three ideas are logged as possibilities in docs/briefs/README.md's bench (a status spreader, a stasis piece, the Zenith Emitter's tier-three return as a status reader), and none gets a brief until the Aphelion roster's powers are read (Ryan, 22 October: or an overpowered Umbra Inducer happens again).
  • What the go on the roster analysis left open (docs/roster_analysis.md, 14 October): the tiers the briefs propose (docs/briefs/README.md's Tiers, nothing decided), and each brief in its own session.
  • The bosses' slate (docs/bosses_proposal.md, its twelve landed 30 October as placeholder kits): Gilgamesh a domain's boss or a domain-agnostic elite (Claude's lean in the proposal); the swallow's early release (Ryan, 30 October: Clair Obscur releases on a stagger, “So maybe it's a damage threshold? Every quarter or third releases? Either that or he gets a unique stagger mechanic”); the steal's fake and the follow-up's break, explained in chat the same day; the escorts by boss and tier, Claude's to write and his to mark; more massive foes for the ranks, a line a domain; and whether a boss's armour scales with the tier (his question of the Cinder Tortoise: “Should max allowed armour scale?”). The names are working names until his content pass.
  • The foe roster after the 13 October picks: whether the Bomb plays as more than an annoyance, judged in play (it stays until Ryan plays it, 15 October; the change on the table if it does not is a burst that also hits the foes beside it, which makes a hurt Bomb a tool for the player); new types from the candidates Ryan marked (the goblin shaman, an elite, Ryan, 15 October; a goblin elite or boss, another goblin type, and the shielded skeleton knight now that the drowned take is the Revenant; marked in tools/foeforge/picks/picks.json).
  • Silence's, Purge's and the foe's Cleanse's carriers (section 5's row, section 9's levers): the Meadow's shaman the first silence, the Waste's tier-two and tier-three casters and the bosses the purges (the Augur's Dispel a purge special), the Marsh's healer the cleanse; Ryan's marks on the ladder (git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md) place them.
  • The promoted forms' one move more for the Warrior, the Martial Artist, the Thief, the Lancer, the Augur and the Black Mage, and Fáfnir's mechanics for the Summoner's: a slate in docs/heroes_proposal.md (21 October), the Augur's a purge by lean, which would answer the carriers' question too; in a session of its own on Ryan's word (21 October).
  • Names: the loot (working names Derelict, Carcass, Relic), the game.

The campaign and the economy

  • The quick campaign is an experiment, not a feature (Ryan, 28 October; section 3's row), so its runs read nothing for any decision until he says otherwise; what its first battle showed (the trio's chain against whole formations, moved most by the fights' size: docs/measurements.md, 27 October) belongs to the foes' fresh start's fight sizes, not to the mode.
  • Three or four (Ryan, 27 October: revisit it, a lot has changed since four was locked on 13 October, section 4's four-machines row). What has: the single-digit scale and the stock pass, targeting at random, the Gel as a wall, area at half, the one damage state, the bosses at their tier, the quick campaign's draft of three with the fourth from an offer, and the fights' reads (docs/measurements.md, 27 October): a stock four beats every formation as it stands at 100% on the greedy rung for 3 to 14% of its hull, with an area piece or without; a trio sits in a decision band on the Meadow and the Waste and is walled on the Marsh and the Peak; formations a third thinner are a trio's decision band. So the fights as they stand are a trio's, whole ones nobody's, and the fresh start sizes them for whichever squad is chosen. What four buys: the four roles in one fight (a tank, a setter, a casher, a support), a spare for a detachment on a map nine wide whose crossing takes three to hold, a wreck leaving a fieldable three, and the fourth slot as the run's first growth. What three buys: a leaner round on the phone (three dice and three views under the thirty seconds), every pick a third of the team, the hangar's rotation a decision from the first offer, a starker horde against few machines, and Into the Breach's three beside Slice and Dice's five. What would settle it: Ryan's feel on the phone with four views against three, and the planner rung on built trios against built fours, whether a setup pays more in a trio, each die a bigger share, or cannot be afforded there, every die needed for a kill, an afternoon's run. Ryan, 28 October: four kept, and three stays an option if four proves too difficult to balance, detaching one from four being easier to balance than one from three; its own question, left open.
  • The front's card, now that the curve's form is settled (30 October, section 3's curve row: the tier locked and shown, the ramp live): the rift's tier where the Convergence's showed, its count of elites, and their kinds once scouted, so scouting buys the kind and an unscouted elite's kind stays the curveball; the runner's front choice by tier and pay; the breadth of components a run deals (Ryan: breadth, not quantity) is the economy pass's, with loot at a battle's end waiting to see.
  • With four domains, does one lead and at most one guest a battle still stand, the last tier anything from the roster?
  • What makes a Sponsorship piece rare now that tiers exist, and what does a domain-themed research token draw from?
  • Modules: ever blueprints, or each one a single item; and a priced stat bundle beside a priced rule, generated to one budget (section 10), or the catalogue's rules with generated stat lines?
  • Whether a starting chassis is replaced or kept through a run, and rolled module stats (section 10).
  • More turnover in the roster (machines easier to scrap), read with the foes' damage axis once the campaign's fall is understood (section 11).
  • The Fracture's prize (section 8), after the meter is played: one of three, a battle-long modifier, a one-shot or a boon, or none (Ryan, 24 October: “maybe not warranted at all”). And the ramp's numbers once the turtle read is in: whether the step inside a battle moves off zero, and whether the bounty cap leaves the bench (section 4's stall row).
  • Every price and yield, the Convergence's threshold, the finals' counts, loot density, the destroyed-is-gone threshold: the economy pass (docs/economy_pass.md).
  • The fronts' pressure (git show 85e28fd:docs/curve_proposal.md, the fronts). Ryan, 24 October: does a rift ever close on its own, and is a perfect run, every city standing, possible, or does one fall as a matter of course? Today no rift closes but by a win, or with its domain's final won elsewhere (26 October, section 3's row), and the schedule never fells a city: while every battle is won the board holds two or three fronts and none is more than two bleeds down when fought, so falls come from lost battles; the third tier's extra rift never opens; and the bleed short of a fall costs nothing once its rift is won (24 October, docs/measurements.md, a model of the turn loop). Ryan, restated: should rifts close on their own? Thematically no, and a rift that has done its damage making way for a higher tier "to finish the job" pays the neglect a tier, since the loot follows the tier ("more like a reward for ignoring it"; so too a rift widening in place, Claude's deepening again): no rift closes on its own, and none steps up. City damage costs its maker's stock on the shelf instead, dearer and then rarer while the siege lasts, gone at the fall: "a discrete reason to go fix it" (Ryan). Claude's leans: a perfect run possible and hard, never a scheduled fall; and the win's Favour docked for the battle alone, so a rescue pays in full, the carrot (Claude's siege dock and Claude's discount for a whole gate withdrawn on Ryan's points: the first left a rescue paying nothing, the second rewards nursing one maker and "stifles diversity"). The bleed's numbers stay the pacing and economy sessions'.
  • The finals' weight (section 9's finals row; docs/measurements.md, 23 October; the review of 27 October). A hero's final reads easier than every formation but the Waste's for a starter trio (greedy 99 to 100%, 1 to 29% of the trio's hull, against 43 to 48% for the Peak's, the Marsh's and the Meadow's), and since the set finals of 26 October every rift a domain leads turns final at once, so a run meets more of them (the 26th's report read 23 in 40 runs where 13 were, won 86%, on a bot read since found to overstate its wins; the rerun of the 27th met none, the plain player beating no domain's three bosses, so the count waits on a runner player that fields a squad), each won final closing the domain's other rifts free. So the domain's capstone is its cheapest big fight and now its most frequent, and the three bosses before it are the whole price. Ryan, 27 October: "if the final is the easiest fight, wouldn't that softly indicate that maybe the player should need to kill the whole escort? Otherwise it's just focus down the small boss for little cost." The engine already asks it: a fight ends only when no foe stands or waits, so a final's escort dies before the fight is won, and the hero's fall alone decides the battle only once the fight is over; the final is cheap because its escort is three wildlife (one a column) beside the tier's two or four, where a formation is eight to sixteen, so the lever is the escort's size. Meant, the hero the reward the bosses gate, or should a final press like a boss's fight: the hero's numbers read as a boss's, its escort the tier's composition (the foes' proposal gives a final the tier's escort composition), or the finals gated harder? Ryan's first call (27 October): the whole escort must die, as the engine already asks, and the audit's 26 closes that way, so the hero's fall alone never wins. What is left is the escort's size: Claude's lean the escort first, since the hero scales with the tier already (section 9's boss row) and a final before Convergence 4 needs one domain's three bosses in the first three battles; and the two modes field different bosses today, the campaign's unit bringing the tier's escort alone where the quick campaign's boss brings its whole formation beside it, which the proposal's escort compositions (its question 11) would make one exam; read again on the merged content, since the 23 October grid predates the bosses at their tier.
  • A fallen city's maker leaves the offers and the shelf, so the roster's growth dies with the cities and a finale fought with most cities fallen is supplied by the Arsenal and loot alone: the spiral's teeth, as intended, or does a fallen maker keep a trickle at the capital?
  • The campaign's plain player wins no run since the Armadillo's rework (20 October; docs/measurements.md): the old Armadillo was its one lone carry, and its fights are still mostly one machine alone (three in four at the stock pass's numbers, a lone machine winning about a quarter of them). The lone fights are the roster's, not the walk's (26 October, docs/measurements.md): four battles in five are fought by one or two machines, wrecks sitting out with no spares, and the squad walking as one and joining a hunter's catch moves none of it; what the boss row of 25 October moved is the battles won (1% to 15%), a trio that reaches the boss fighting it together, which is the read that says the proposal's seal matters; so the campaign report reads the spiral the economy answers and the boss row, and the fights' shape is read on the quick campaign until a run can field a squad. Is a tier-one machine meant to carry a lone fight, or does the campaign's number wait on a runner player that fights as a team, with the foes' fresh start? Claude's lean: the latter, with that player brought forward as a runner change of its own, since the report reads nothing while every run is lost. The finale's read waits on the same player (section 9's finale row).
  • The runner reads all four domains now: is that the baseline the commander layer waits for? The commander layer itself (section 8): the command die or one-shots only, city abilities, what a Fracture offers.

The battlefield and the foes

  • The foes' fresh start, built 30 October with the pacing session (its brief retired: git show 85e28fd:docs/briefs/fresh_start.md; the proposal git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md). What waits on Ryan's marks: the Peak's elite and a Waste hunter; the five placeholder kits' names and numbers, and which Fracture each tier spawns them at beyond the table's; the compositions' partition of the ladder's lists; the escort compositions hand-tailored by tier; roles (his six words, shown or internal); one rider on one advanced foe a domain and enemy Shock, telegraphed, if any; the heroes' extra moves (the Martial Artist has four written: which go); the Rune-bearer's execute against the retired Execute.
  • When and how the boss takes three hexes again.
  • Ally wrecks: does a wreck block a hex, for a round or the battle; its death on the map. Corruption in a pack's wake.
  • Rules on the rift’s painted ground, art alone for now (Ryan, 29 October), and a flyer weakness, if wanted, only there (the twisted landscape’s row): a skirmish never reads our own ground (30 October).
  • Base defences: gate guns, then turrets. The reinforcement phase: can the player act on it?

Look, sound and lore

  • Boss themes: one a domain or one a boss; do the old dirge and brass seeds carry over; does the finale get its own?
  • Where a boss theme's map half plays, now every track is a pair (section 1's pair row). Claude's lean: in the map's turn like any track, the boss's reveal still starting the battle half from its top as the one hard change; or the boss's battle plays the theme's map half from its first turn and the reveal flips to the battle half in place like any fight.
  • Jenny's foe-down lines: each domain mapped to an old family pool (as the blows are), or pools of their own?
  • Should a blow's sound follow its tags (Beam a laser, Plasma a plasma)? Today every flip between rolled and flat needs a content label.
  • Looks: a sci-fi gate; a render margin round each skirmish machine view; attack acts, a session of their own; walkers' pace on the map.
  • Sensor contacts at the campaign layer; the lore holes (section 1); the game's name.

Pinned

  • Room to expand in the lore (section 1).
  • A secret true final boss: an Arsenal extradimensional superweapon (Ryan).
  • An aim that seeks plate, for a breaching move: a breacher aims at random as the rank and file do, or at the exposed, which a breach finds on every machine alike, so it has no taste for the tank; a plate-seeking aim is free to add where a breacher's threat calls for one (22 October; any foe may carry the aim its threat calls for, 24 October).
  • The gravity piece (section 7): a tier-three blueprint with an area effect, shelved with lots of potential (Ryan, 13 October).
  • Hellfire, free now that no demon faction wears it and the Summoner's move is Pyroclasm (13 October): an Arsenal component prefix, later (Ryan, 12 October); the King James Bible's own words, so it keeps the liturgy.
  • A map taunt for a slow Arsenal bruiser; a machine that moves after it fights.
  • A low-hull berserker or last-stand entry.
  • Transforming chassis; a large Aphelion hovercraft; long guns while the engagement range may change (section 7).
  • Command entries with a battlefield meaning too.
  • An endless mode after the bounded campaign.
  • Jenny's idle nag.
  • Livery by Sponsorship, once the hangar exists.
The design record for Riftgate. The game's rules as they stand are in docs/rules.md; this document says why. History lives in git and docs/status.md, never here.
